A priori obstructions to resolution of singularities
Tobias Shin

Abstract
We present an argument due to Thom to formulate a priori cohomology obstructions for a projective variety to admit an embedded resolution of singularities, and generalize the argument to a field of characteristic p > 0 p>0 . We show that these obstructions are defined for a general cohomology theory that satisfies localization, étale excision, and 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -invariance, and is equipped with a proper pushforward. As examples, we show that odd degree Steenrod homology operations on higher Chow groups mod ℓ \ell , defined by a suitable E ∞ E_{\infty} -algebra structure, vanish on the fundamental class of a smooth variety, where ℓ \ell is any prime. We extend this general vanishing result to arbitrary varieties for operations defined mod ℓ ≠ p \ell\neq p . Along the way, we also obtain a Wu formula for the mod p p Steenrod operations in the case of closed embeddings of smooth varieties.



1
Introduction

The aim of this paper was to naïvely define topological obstructions for a variety to admit a resolution of singularities over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. We do not succeed in this endeavor; instead, we present a vanishing theorem for generalized cohomology theories satisfying suitable axioms. As an example, we can consider motivic cohomology with coefficients mod
\ell
where
\ell
is any prime (including
\ell=p
). There exist mod
\ell
Steenrod operations
Q^{\bullet}
on motivic cohomology, arising from an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure (see Theorem
3.1
). We let
\beta
denote the Bockstein and its dual.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed embedding of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
Q^{\bullet}
be the simplicial Steenrod operations on motivic cohomology
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
, where
\ell\neq p
, and let
Q_{\bullet}
denote the dual operations on
H_{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
. The odd degree operations
\beta Q_{s}
vanish on the fundamental class
[X]
. If
X
is also assumed smooth, then the odd degree operations
\beta Q_{s}
vanish on the fundamental class
[X]
for
\ell=p
.

Theorem
1.1
is a special case of Theorem
1.2
, which applies to
generalized cohomology theories
in the sense of Panin–Smirnov (see Definition
3.2
). Here, a cohomology theory
A
is a contravariant functor that has a long exact sequence of a pair, and satisfies étale excision and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance. Examples of such theories can be found in the article of Panin
[
63
]
, such as classical singular cohomology over
\mathbb{C}
, mod
\ell\neq p
étale cohomology over a field of characteristic
p>0
, higher Chow groups with integral coefficients or coefficients mod
\ell
where
\ell
is any prime (including when
\ell=p
), algebraic
K
-theory, algebraic cobordism theory, any representable theory, etc. We further want our cohomology theory to have a ring structure and to come from the cohomology of a sheaf (or hypercohomology of a complex of sheaves) (see Definitions
3.3
and
4.2
). Moreover we require our theory
A
to be equipped with a
proper pushforward
(Definition
3.5
). It is then a theorem of Panin–Smirnov that this is equivalent to equipping
A
with a notion of Chern classes, which is also equivalent to equipping
A
with a notion of Thom class and Thom isomorphism (see Theorem
3.9
).

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
A
be a bigraded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov, that is given by a sheaf cohomology theory valued in modules over a field, and is equipped with a proper pushforward. Suppose
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
. Let
\phi=\phi^{\bullet}
be a ring operation with well-defined Todd genus that preserves even degrees in the first index, and let
\beta
be a natural operation that sends bidegrees
(i,j)
to
(i+1,j)
that commutes with proper pushforward. Moreover, assume
\beta
is a derivation that vanishes on the Todd class of any bundle. Suppose
X\hookrightarrow P
is a closed embedding of a smooth variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. The operation
\beta\phi^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau\in A^{2c,c}_{X}(P)
where
c
is the codimension of
X
in
P
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
A
be a theory with the same hypotheses as in Theorem
1.2
. Suppose
X\hookrightarrow P
is a closed embedding of a (possibly
singular
) variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
over a field of characteristic
p>0
. If
A
is valued in
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
-modules, where
\ell\neq p
, then the operation
\beta\tilde{\phi}^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau\in A^{2c,c}_{X}(P)
, where
\tilde{\phi}
is the operation associated to
\phi
that commutes with proper pushforward. Moreover, if
A
is valued in
\mathbb{Z}/p
-modules and
X
admits an embedded resolution of singularities, then the operation
\beta\tilde{\phi}^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau\in A^{2c,c}_{X}(P)
.

Theorem
1.3
essentially defines a priori cohomological obstructions to a projective variety
X
admitting an embedded resolution of singularities. If
X
admits a resolution, then certain mod
p
cohomological operations must vanish. Thus, in theory, one could construct an example of a singular variety
X
where these operations are non-zero. As of this writing, and to the author’s knowledge, the problem of resolution of singularities in positive characteristic is still open; if proven true, then Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.3
can be interpreted as vanishing theorems.

However, the only examples we have either already vanish for reasons other than resolution of singularities, or do not satisfy the hypotheses of the theorems: the mod
\ell
operations on motivic and étale cohomology vanish due to alterations, and we do not know how to pushforward the mod
p
operations.

Our motivation begins with the work of Thom
[
76
]
. In private communication, Sullivan informed the author of his belief that Thom, after his work on Steenrod’s problem of representing homology classes by manifolds, was motivated to formulate cohomological obstructions to resolving the singularities of a compact complex algebraic variety; Sullivan explains this belief explicitly in the article
[
72
]
. The cohomology obstructions are defined as follows: first, for a given compact complex algebraic variety
X
, one topologically embeds
X
into a large euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. There are mod
\ell
Steenrod operations
Q^{\bullet}
defined on the cohomology of a pair
(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}-X)
, for any prime
\ell
. By Alexander–Lefschetz–Poincaré duality, these cohomology operations dualize to mod
\ell
operations
Q_{\bullet}
on the (Borel–Moore) homology of
X
. These operations do not depend on the embedding or dimension of euclidean space. Moreover, these homology operations commute with proper pushforwards. Sullivan then explained the following argument, which he attributed to Thom. We abuse notation and let
\beta
denote the dualized Bockstein operation on homology.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
X
be a compact complex algebraic variety. Then the operations of odd degree
\beta Q_{\bullet}
vanish on the fundamental class
[X]
.

Proof.

We first prove the case when
X
is smooth. We smoothly embed
X
into a large euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
X
is complex, its normal bundle is stably almost complex, so
N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a complex bundle for some
k
. Embed
X
into the larger euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
where
N=n+k
, so that its normal bundle is complex. By definition of the operations, it suffices to show
\beta Q^{\bullet}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau
in the relative cohomology
H^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}-X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)
. We have a natural isomorphism
H^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}-X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)\cong H^{*}(N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N},N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N}-0,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)
where
N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N}
denotes the normal bundle of
X
with respect to our embedding. In this equivalence, the dual class
\tau
is identified with the Thom class of the normal bundle, so we will use the same notation for both.

The normal bundle
N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N}
induces a classifying map
X\rightarrow Gr(c)
where
Gr(c)
is the infinite Grassmannian of complex
c
-dimensional subspaces, where
c
is the complex codimension of
X
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{X}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr(c)}

where
\gamma^{c}
is the tautological bundle of complex
c
-dimensional subspaces. The classifying map induces a map on the Thom spaces, and so a map
H^{*}(\gamma^{c},\gamma^{c}-0,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)\rightarrow H^{*}(N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N},N_{X}\mathbb{R}^{N}-0,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)
, and sends a cohomology class
\gamma
in cohomology of the the universal Thom space to the Thom class
\tau
of the normal bundle, by functoriality of the Thom isomorphism. The cohomology of the complex Grassmannian
Gr(c)
is concentrated in even degrees, and so operations
\beta Q^{\bullet}
vanish on
\gamma
. By naturality, they then vanish on the Thom class
\tau
, and so the dual homology operations
\beta Q_{\bullet}
vanish on the fundamental class
[X]
.

Suppose now
X
is singular. Then
X
has a resolution of singularities
Y
by Hironaka
[
39
]
, where
Y
is smooth and the map
Y\rightarrow X
is proper and birational. In particular, the fundamental class
[Y]
is sent to
[X]
. The odd degree operations vanish on
[Y]
by the argument above, and they commute with proper pushforward, so they also vanish on
[X]
.
∎

If
X
is a singular variety that admits a resolution, then the odd degree homology operations must vanish on its fundamental class. Thus, these operations are “obstructions” in the sense that, finding an example of a variety where these operations are non-zero would give a variety that had no resolution. They are “a priori” as they do not require resolution of singularities to be defined.

The idea of this paper is to translate this differential topology argument into algebraic geometry, over a field of characteristic
p>0
. The central difficulty was, and still may be, finding the correct cohomology theory suitable for this program. Initially, Sullivan considered using étale cohomology
[
72
]
but learned from de Jong
[
19
]
that a priori mod
\ell\neq p
obstructions would vanish. The argument then required a suitable
p
-adic cohomology theory, which captured
p
-torsion information. There is a large amount of literature in the field exploring the tension between a “nice”
p
-adic cohomology theory, Steenrod operations, and resolution of singularities (for example, see the articles
[
38
]
,
[
23
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
62
]
). We believe this tension is related to the Thom argument. That is, given a sufficiently robust
p
-adic cohomology theory, we believe one can formulate Thom obstructions.

A suitable
p
-adic cohomology theory for this program should have something similar to Steenrod operations, although any graded ring endomorphism should do. This ruled out considering theories which used rational coefficients, such as rigid cohomology or Monsky–Washnitzer cohomology. Motivated by the work of Mandell
[
55
]
, we were led to look for
p
-adic theories that came from
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures. As a result, Sullivan and the author were led to prismatic cohomology
[
14
]
, crystalline cohomology, and general Weil cohomology theories, but found that naïve topological constructions failed, as ideas such as excision or local contractibility did not hold. For example, the affine line has infinite–dimensional prismatic cohomology, which made local constructions difficult.

After initial difficulties with prismatic cohomology, we were led to consider logarithmic de Rham–Witt forms
[
42
]
,
[
45
]
and observed that naïve topological ideas worked after taking fixed points of the (inverse) Cartier operator. To the author, this looked like taking fixed points of Frobenius, which kept contributing infinite–dimensional cohomology for affine varieties. Stripping away the Frobenius, the author was led to observe that the affine line was acyclic with respect to mod
p
log de Rham–Witt cohomology, which is equivalent to mod
p
motivic cohomology. Unfortunately, most of the contemporary literature on motivic cohomology takes a highly categorical point of view, with a substantial amount of results depending on resolution of singularities. As a result, we have opted to present the material we need as simple and geometric as possible, making sure none of the results we used depend on resolution. For example, we take motivic cohomology as the higher Chow groups of Bloch
[
9
]
and avoid the
cdh
-topology at all costs. This is the reason for the entirely expository Section
2
.

Aside from finding the right cohomology theory for the project, the main difficulties of translating the argument of Proposition
1.1
from differential topology to algebraic geometry over characteristic
p>0
were in determining which topological arguments were still allowed. For example, in Thom’s original argument, one defines the homology operations by embedding a variety into a large euclidean space. This step is not possible in algebraic geometry unless the variety is affine. However, for our purposes, it suffices to considers pairs
(P,X)
where
X
is a variety with a closed embedding into a smooth variety
P
. By considering pairs, we could try and formulate obstructions to an embedded resolution of singularities. Moreover, Thom uses a classifying map to a Grassmannian, which again does not exist in algebraic geometry without passing to stacks; however, we bypass this difficulty in section
4.1
with an observation due to Deligne–Sullivan, as long as our cohomology is homotopy invariant.

Thus, one needs a homotopy invariant
p
-adic cohomology theory that has a notion of cohomology for a pair, which fits into a long exact sequence. Moreover, this theory only needs to be defined on the category of smooth varieties, since we can handle the Borel–Moore homology of a singular variety
X
embedded in a smooth variety
P
by working with the cohomology of a pair
A(P,P-X)
. For
X
embedded in a smooth variety
P
, there is a notion of motivic cohomology with support, which is naturally dual to the higher Chow groups of the subvariety
X
. This fits the picture of higher Chow groups behaving more like a Borel–Moore homology theory than a cohomology theory; thus we can avoid any complications of having to work with motivic cohomology of a singular variety, which was not even well defined until recent work of Elmanto and Morrow
[
21
]
.

By work of Joshua
[
43
]
and Kriz–May
[
47
]
, there are Steenrod operations
Q^{\bullet}
on motivic cohomology mod
\ell
for smooth varieties, where
\ell
is any prime (including the characteristic). The operations are derived from the product structure on Bloch’s cycle complexes, which is only well defined up to quasi-isomorphism. This gives the cycle complexes an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure, which then inherits generalized Steenrod operations by work of May
[
57
]
. We examine these operations closely in Section
3
and extend them to cohomology with support. We emphasize here that these Steenrod operations are
not
the same as those defined by Voevodsky
[
81
]
or Primozic
[
65
]
, as those operations vanish on fundamental classes for trivial degree reasons (see Remark
3.8
).

However, homotopy theory is one thing, but geometry is another. Once the operations are defined for cohomology with support, there is now another nuance; we thank Patrick Brosnan for informing us of this crucial point. In classical topology, one can define the Borel–Moore homology groups of a space
X
as the Alexander–Lefschetz–Poincaré dual of the cohomology of a pair
(\mathbb{R}^{N},\mathbb{R}^{N}-X)
. The groups are then proved to be independent of the choice of embedding. The cohomology of a pair admits Steenrod operations; however, the dualized homology operations
do
depend on the embedding and ambient space. In particular, the operations
do not
commute with proper pushforward. At best, there is a Riemann–Roch type formula that describes their failure to commute with pushforward, with error terms arising from characteristic classes of the tangent bundle of the ambient space. We call these error terms a
Todd genus
(see Definition
3.10
). Thus, if one embeds into euclidean space, the error terms all vanish. The work of Panin–Smirnov
[
66
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
67
]
,
[
64
]
,
[
70
]
gives a general Riemann–Roch type formula for generalized cohomology theories in the sense above, given a notion of proper pushforward on cohomology (see Definition
3.5
) and a suitable ring operation
\phi
(see Definition
3.3
). Given such a Riemann–Roch type formula, one can twist the operation by the Todd genus, and we can define new operations
\tilde{\phi}
that do commute with pushforward. It is through these Riemann–Roch type formulas that the geometry of the ambient space is crucial to our homology operations. This is the content of Section
3.3
.

This paper is then about transporting the entire framework of differential topology to algebraic geometry, to then mimic the argument of Thom in Proposition
1.1
. Moreover, once this is done, one can use alterations of de Jong
[
19
]
with appropriate degree
[
75
]
to extend the vanishing results to singular varieties, so long as the coefficients are valued mod
\ell\neq p
. The proofs of the Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.3
are the content of Sections
4
and
5
.

As mentioned prior, we do not know how to pushforward the mod
p
motivic operations, as there is no Riemann–Roch type formula. This is discussed in the latter parts of section
3.3
. Although there is no general Riemann–Roch type formula for the mod
p
operations for general proper pushforwards, we are able to obtain one for the special case of closed embeddings of smooth varieties. This is proven as Theorem
3.3
.

Theorem 1.4
.

(Wu formula)
Let
i:X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed embedding of smooth varieties, and let
i_{!}
denote the proper pushforward on mod
p
motivic cohomology. We obtain the formula

Q^{\bullet}i_{!}(\alpha)=i_{!}(Q^{\bullet}(\alpha)\cup c_{\textnormal{top}}^{p-1}(N_{X}P))

for
\alpha\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p(*))
, where
Q^{\bullet}
are the mod
p
simplicial operations on mod
p
motivic cohomology, and
c_{\textnormal{top}}(N_{X}P)
is the top Chern class of the normal bundle of
X
in
P
.

Finally, we would like to say that our approach is naïve, in that we have given a set of axioms sufficient but not necessary for Thom’s argument to work. For example, one could relax the homotopy invariance axiom and still obtain many of the formal results needed for the argument (see Section
7
). Our approach is naïve in the sense that having the homotopy invariance axiom allows for simpler arguments. It is still possible for there to be a more general cohomology theory with a suitable choice of ring operation that succeeds where we have failed. It is also entirely true of course that the program of finding obstructions is vacuous, if resolution of singularities holds true, in which case we simply have these vanishing theorems.

We also want to note the similiarity in spirit of our work with that of Fulton (Example 19.1.8 in
[
29
]
), Kawai
[
46
]
, Benoist
[
6
]
, Quick
[
68
]
, and Atiyah–Hirzebruch
[
3
]
who all utilize Steenrod operations as obstructions to cohomology classes being represented by subvarieties (for example, disproving the integral Hodge conjecture).

Structure of the paper.
Sections
2
and
3
are entirely expository, with nothing new in them. The expert in higher Chow groups and its formal properties should feel free to skip section
2
, and the expert in Steenrod operations should feel free to skip the first half of Section
3
. The first half of Section
3
concerns results that are known, but either hard to find in the literature or in unpublished material. The real meat of the paper is in the latter half of Section
3
, starting with section
3.2
. The notion of generalized cohomology theory is given in section
3.2
, and the Riemann–Roch type theorems of Panin–Smirnov are discussed in Section
3.3
. Section
4
discusses an observation due to Deligne–Sullivan that allows us to use classifying maps in algebraic geometry, and section
5
brings all aforementioned sections together to generalize the Thom argument for generalized cohomology theories. It also proves the Thom argument for certain Steenrod operations defined on motivic cohomology. Section
6
shows the mod
\ell\neq p
operations all vanish. Section
7
is purely conjectural and has no meaningful content.

Notation.
Throughout this paper, we fix an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. A variety
X
is an integral, separated scheme over
k
of finite type. All varieties are quasiprojective unless specified otherwise. All products and dimensions of spaces are over the field
k
. Likewise, all tensor products of abelian sheaves are over
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
, where
\ell
is any prime.

Acknowledgements.
We would like to thank Dennis Sullivan, who originally allowed the author to work on this project as his thesis, and for telling us the Thom argument and the Deligne–Sullivan trick. We would like to also thank Thomas Geisser and Marc Levine for patiently answering the author’s basic questions on higher Chow groups, Patrick Brosnan for teaching us that the dual Steenrod homology operations on Borel–Moore homology do not commute with proper pushforward, Peter May for helping the author understand some nuances about
E_{\infty}
-algebras, and Spencer Bloch for clarifying some points in his private letter. We also want to thank Benson Farb for helpful comments on an initial draft of this paper.

2
Motivic cohomology and proper pushforward

Most of the material in this mainly expository section can be found in work of Bloch
[
9
]
, Geisser
[
31
]
,
[
33
]
, Levine
[
51
]
,
[
53
]
, and Mazza–Weibel–Voevodsky
[
61
]
. We will discuss the basic properties of higher Chow groups and motivic cohomology, such as pullback, proper pushforward, cup products, projection formulas, and twisted Poincaré duality in the sense of Bloch–Ogus
[
12
]
. This twisted duality will allow us to analyze singular varieties that admit embeddings into smooth ones. We will also discuss how the two theories are related for smooth varieties. For a nice summary of the properties of higher Chow groups, see Part I, Chapter II.2 in Levine
[
50
]
.

2.1
Higher Chow groups

We first recall the definition of Bloch’s cycle complex
[
9
]
, following Geisser
[
33
]
and Levine
[
51
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
algebraic
n
-simplex
is defined as the affine hyperplane
\textnormal{Spec }k[t_{0},...,t_{n}]/(t_{0}+...+t_{n}=1)
. A
face

F
of
\Delta^{n}
is a subscheme defined by equations of the form
t_{i_{0}}=...=t_{i_{r}}=0
(or
\Delta^{n}
itself). The
vertices

v_{i}
are the faces given by
t_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
. An order-preserving map of simplices
g:[n]\rightarrow[m]
defines a map
\Delta^{n}\rightarrow\Delta^{m}
by defining a map on the vertices
v^{n}_{i}\rightarrow v^{m}_{g(i)}
and then extending affine-linearly. We write
\Delta^{\bullet}
as the cosimplicial scheme
[n]\rightarrow\Delta^{n}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
be a variety. Define the
cycle complexes
as follows: let
z_{n}(X,i)
be the free abelian group generated by closed integral subschemes
W
of dimension
n+i
in
X\times\Delta^{i}
such that
\textnormal{dim}W\cap X\times F\leq n+i+\textnormal{dim}X\times F-\textnormal{dim}X\times\Delta^{i}
for all faces
F
of
\Delta^{i}
, i.e.,
W
meets all faces properly in
X\times\Delta^{i}
.

We obtain a chain complex
z_{n}(X,*)
by taking as our differential the alternating sum of intersections with faces, which we call the
n
-th cycle complex. That is,
\partial(W)=\sum_{j}[W\cap V(t_{j})]
, where
V(t_{j})
is the variety determined by
t_{j}=0
, and where
[W\cap V(t_{j})]
is the linear combination of the reduced irreducible components of the intersection
W\cap V(t_{j})
, with coefficients given by intersection multiplicities. The
higher Chow groups
are defined as the homology of this chain complex, with
CH_{n}(X,i)
denoting the
i
-th homology group. We call this the
i
-th higher Chow group of
n
-cycles.

Remark 2.1
.

We can also grade the cycle complex by codimension, with
z^{n}(X,i)
denoting the free abelian group generated by closed integral subschemes
W
of codimension
n
in
X\times\Delta^{i}
such that
W\cap X\times F
has codimension
\geq n
in
X\times F
for all faces
F
, i.e.,
W
meets all faces
X\times F\subset X\times\Delta^{i}
properly. This again yields a homological chain complex
z^{n}(X,*)
, whose
i
-th homology we write as
CH^{n}(X,i)
. By definition, we have that
z_{n}(X,i)=z^{d-n}(X,i)
for a pure
d
-dimensional variety
X
. Note in this case we have an equality on the underlying sets, and that the regrading is purely formal. We will see later that Poincaré duality for smooth varieties recovers this abstract isomorphism of groups through a suitable pairing defined by a product structure; this product structure is not necessarily available for singular varieties, although the abstract isomorphism of groups still is.

Taking the
0
-th homology for the cycle complexes
z_{n}(X,*)
imposes the relation of rational equivalence on subschemes of
X
, thereby recovering the ordinary Chow groups
CH_{n}(X,0)\cong CH_{n}(X)
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The cycle complexes and homology groups satisfy the following properties:

1.

(Proper pushforward) If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a proper map, then the map
f\times Id:X\times\Delta^{i}\rightarrow Y\times\Delta^{i}
is proper. This induces a pushforward map on algebraic cycles
(f\times Id)_{*}:z_{n}(X,i)\rightarrow z_{n}(Y,i)
given by sending a closed subscheme
W
to
[k(W):k((f\times Id)(W))](f\times id)(W)
if the dimension of
(f\times Id)(W)
equals the dimension of
W
, and
0
otherwise. The map
(f\times Id)_{*}
commutes with the differentials (see Proposition 1.3 in
[
9
]
). We abuse notation and denote the induced map on homology by
f_{*}:CH_{n}(X,i)\rightarrow CH_{n}(Y,i)
, for every
n
and every
i
.

2.

(Flat pullback) If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a flat map (of relative dimension
c
), then the map
f\times Id:X\times\Delta^{i}\rightarrow Y\times\Delta^{i}
is flat. This induces a pullback map
(f\times Id)^{*}:z_{n}(Y,i)\rightarrow z_{n+c}(X,i)
given by sending a closed subscheme
W
to
(f\times Id)^{-1}(W)
. The map
(f\times Id)^{*}
commutes with the differentials (see Proposition 1.3 in
[
9
]
). We abuse notation and denote the induced map on homology by
f^{*}:CH_{n}(Y,i)\rightarrow CH_{n+c}(X,i)
for every
n
and every
i
.

3.

(Localization) For a closed immersion
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
, the left exact sequence

0\rightarrow z_{n}(X,*)\xrightarrow{i_{*}}z_{n}(P,*)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}z_{n}(P-X,*)

defines a distinguished triangle in the derived category of abelian groups for every
n
, where
i_{*}
is the pushforward induced by the closed immersion, and
j^{*}
is the pullback induced by the open immersion
(P-X)\xhookrightarrow{j}P
.

4.

(Homotopy) If
\pi:E\rightarrow X
is a vector bundle over a smooth
X
, the induced pullback
\pi^{*}
on the cycle complexes is a quasi-isomorphism for all
n
. In particular, (beware the grading!)
CH^{n}(X,i)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}CH^{n}(E,i)
is an isomorphism for all
i
and
n
.

Remark 2.2
.

The original article of Bloch
[
9
]
has an error in its proof of its Theorem 3.3 (the “moving lemma”), which was used to prove the localization property above. The lemma was later proven by Bloch in
[
10
]
with an alternative argument. For the proof of Proposition
2.1
(1), (2), and (4), see Bloch
[
9
]
, and for the proof of localization (3), see Bloch
[
10
]
and Theorem 0.3 in Levine
[
53
]
.

The functors
U\mapsto z_{n}(U,*)
and
U\mapsto z^{n}(U,*)
yield homological complexes of Zariski sheaves. We re-grade and re-index the above complexes to interpret them as complexes of Zariski sheaves below.

Definition 2.3
.

We denote
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(n)=z_{n}(-,*)[2n]
as the cohomologically graded complex of sheaves with the sheaf
z_{n}(-,-i-2n)
in degree
i
. Similarly, we denote
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)=z^{n}(-,*)[-2n]
as the cohomologically graded complex of sheaves on
X
with the sheaf
z^{n}(-,-i+2n)
in degree
i
.

Again following Geisser
[
33
]
, we define below our notion of (Borel-Moore) homology we will use in this paper. We also specify what we mean by cohomology; any other sheaf cohomology appearing in this paper will specify the sheaf as coefficients unless otherwise noted.

Definition 2.4
.

We write homology as
H_{i}(X,j):=H^{-i}R\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(j))
. We also write cohomology as
H^{i}(X,j):=H^{i}R\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}_{X}(j))
. Here, we are taking hypercohomology for the respective complexes of sheaves with respect to the Zariski topology.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. We define
local motivic cohomology
or
motivic cohomology with support
as
H^{i}_{X}(P,j):=H^{i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(j))
.

Although we are interpreting the complexes as complexes of sheaves and taking their hypercohomology, it is a remarkable fact that this is no different from taking homology of the global sections when our spaces are separated, Noetherian, and of finite Krull dimension. This is a consequence of the localization property of the cycle complexes (Property (3) in Prop.
2.1
).

Lemma 2.1
.

(Zariski descent) The hypercohomology
H^{i}R\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(j))
agrees with the cohomology
H^{i}\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(j))
of the complex of abelian groups, and similarly for the complexes
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(j)
for all
j
. In particular, we have
H_{i}(X,j)\cong CH_{j}(X,i-2j)
and
H^{i}(X,j)\cong CH^{j}(X,2j-i)
.

Proof.

The localization property implies the cycle complexes satisfy the Mayer-Vietoris property (see Def. 2.2 in
[
77
]
). By the argument in Theorem 3.2
[
31
]
, which uses Thomason’s criterion (see Prop. 2.3 and Def. 2.6 in
[
77
]
), the Mayer-Vietoris property implies the homology of the cycle complexes agrees with the hypercohomology of the sheaves
z_{n}(-,*)
. This follows from a theorem of Brown and Gersten that the Mayer-Vietoris property implies a certain spectral sequence degeneration (Theorems 3 and 4 in
[
7
]
). This concludes the proof. Warning: the Mayer-Vietoris property is not preserved under quasi-isomorphisms (see the remark under Section 2.4 in the survey
[
32
]
). See also the argument in Theorem 3.2 of
[
31
]
or Theorem 19.11 in
[
61
]
.
∎

Suppose now
X
is a variety of dimension
n
. Then we have that
H_{2n}(X,n)
equals
CH_{n}(X,0)
, the
n
-th Chow group of
X
. Thus, there is a canonical element corresponding to the variety
X
itself as an algebraic cycle, which we denote by
[X]
and call the
fundamental class
.

2.1.1

Contravariance for maps between smooth varieties

Higher Chow groups were historically treated as a cohomology theory for smooth varieties. By Proposition
2.1
we see that they resemble a Borel-Moore homology theory; it is then natural to expect difficulty in proving contravariance for maps between smooth varieties. The main difficulty is that Bloch’s cycle complexes
z^{n}(-,*)
are not contravariant for arbitrary maps between smooth varieties: one can only pull back algebraic cycles if the map is flat, or if a cycle meets the map transversely.

As a result, one proceeds as follows: first, one defines an appropriate subcomplex of
z^{n}(-,*)
on which pullbacks are well-defined. Then one proves a moving lemma to show that the inclusion of this subcomplex is a quasi-isomorphism; this was the initial strategy of Bloch (Theorem 4.1 in
[
9
]
). Both Bloch’s later proof in
[
10
]
and Levine’s proof (Part I, Chapter II, Theorem 3.5.14 in
[
51
]
) conclude that the inclusion of the subcomplex is a quasi-isomorphism for smooth affine varieties. However, this is sufficient: one then takes a covering by affine open sets and uses the localization property to construct the pullback for arbitrary smooth varieties. We briefly outline this construction in this section, following Geisser–Levine (2.3 in
[
35
]
). See also Chapters 17–19 in
[
61
]
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism between smooth varieties. For every integer
j
, we write
S_{j}(f)=\{x\in Y\ |\ \textnormal{dim}_{k(x)}f^{-1}(x)\geq j\}
. We denote by
z^{n}(Y,i)_{f}
the subgroup of
z^{n}(Y,i)
generated by algebraic cycles
W
that meet
S_{j}(f)\times F
properly, for all
j
and for all faces
F
of
\Delta^{i}
.

This construction actually yields a subcomplex
z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\subset z^{n}(Y,*)
. It follows from the definition that the pullback
(f\times Id)^{*}
is defined for
W\in z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}
, which then gives a map of complexes

(f\times Id)^{*}:z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\rightarrow z^{n}(X,*).

For a proof of the following theorem, we defer to
[
10
]
or (Part I, Chapter II, Theorem 3.5.14 in
[
51
]
).

Theorem 2.1
.

(Bloch, Levine) For a smooth affine variety
Y
, the inclusion
z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\xhookrightarrow{i}z^{n}(Y,*)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Theorem
2.1
then gives us a pullback map
f^{*}:z^{n}(Y,*)\rightarrow z^{n}(X,*)
in
D(\textbf{Ab})
for smooth affine
Y
, defined as the composition
(f\times Id)^{*}\circ i^{-1}:z^{n}(Y,*)\xhookleftarrow{\simeq}z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\xrightarrow{(f\times Id)^{*}}z^{n}(X,*)
.

For the general setting, we follow Definition 19.13 in
[
61
]
. We can sheafify the subcomplex
z^{n}(-,*)_{f}
and, after regrading and re-indexing, obtain an inclusion of complexes of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}(n)_{f}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}(n)
. By theorem
2.1
, this inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves, by passing to affine opens locally. If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a morphism of smooth varieties, we then obtain a map of complexes of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{Y}(n)_{f}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
by construction of the pullback on affines, and since
Y
is locally affine. We then conclude the construction of the pullback on higher Chow groups by using that these complexes have no hypercohomology (i.e., they satisfy Zariski descent). The constructed pullback
f^{*}
agrees with the constructions thus far (for flat maps and for smooth affine targets respectively).

Definition 2.6
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism between smooth varieties. Define
f^{*}
as the composition

CH^{j}(Y,2j-i)\cong H^{i}R\Gamma(Y,\mathbb{Z}_{Y}(j))\cong H^{i}R\Gamma(Y,\mathbb{Z}_{Y}(j)_{f})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}H^{i}R\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}_{X}(j))\cong CH^{j}(X,2j-i)

for all
j
and
i
, where the first and last isomorphisms are from Lemma
2.1
, the second isomorphism is induced by the quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves
Z_{Y}(j)_{f}\hookrightarrow Z_{Y}(j)
, and the middle arrow is (abusing notation) the map obtained from the map of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{Y}(j)\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{X}(j)
.

Remark 2.3
.

The construction of a pullback
f^{*}
in Chapters 17–19 of
[
61
]
is similar to the construction we outlined above; however, instead of using Zariski descent (Lemma
2.1
) to glue the map together on open affines, the authors there use Jouanolou’s trick (Lemme 1.5 in
[
44
]
and extended to all smooth varieties by Thomason, Proposition 4.4 in
[
84
]
) and homotopy invariance of higher Chow groups to pass from affines to general varieties. The authors then prove that their pullback agrees with the construction outlined by Theorem
2.1
(Proposition 19.16 in
[
61
]
).

2.1.2

Products in higher Chow groups

This section is meant to briefly summarize the product structure on higher Chow groups for a smooth variety. This in turn gives the direct product of higher Chow groups the structure of a graded commutative ring. For more details, see the Appendix of
[
35
]
.

As stated earlier, the original article of Bloch
[
9
]
has an error in its proof of its Theorem 3.3 (the “moving lemma”). A part of the construction of the product structure (Lemma 5.0 in
[
9
]
) relies on this moving lemma; the article
[
10
]
proves Theorem 3.3, but the Appendix of
[
35
]
notes its argument may not be enough to prove Lemma 5.0, and gives a separate construction. As such, we will follow the construction in the Appendix of
[
35
]
and sketch their proof below. The central idea is that one sends a subvariety
V
of
X\times\Delta^{i}
and a subvariety
W
of
Y\times\Delta^{j}
to the product cycle
V\times W
in
X\times Y\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
. The problem is that we now require
V\times W
to meet the “faces” of the product of simplices
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
properly, which requires a moving lemma and thus only defines a product up to quasi-isomorphism. First we quickly define the notion of a “face” of
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Define a partial order on the product of finite sets
[i]\times[j]
where
(a,b)\leq(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
if and only if
a\leq a^{\prime}
and
b\leq b^{\prime}
. Identify
[i]\times[j]
with the vertices of
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
. An order-preserving map
g:[n]\rightarrow[i]\times[j]
extends uniquely to a map
\Delta g:\Delta^{n}\rightarrow\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
. A
face
of
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
is a subscheme of the form
\Delta g(\Delta^{n})
for some injective
g
.

Proposition 2.2
.

(External product) For varieties
X
and
Y
, there is an external product map on the cycle complexes
\times:z^{n}(X,*)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}z^{m}(Y,*)\rightarrow z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*)
which induces a product map
CH^{i}(X,j)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}CH^{r}(Y,s)\rightarrow CH^{i+r}(X\times Y,j+s)
. For
X
smooth, one can further pull back by the diagonal to obtain a product map
CH^{i}(X,j)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}CH^{r}(X,s)\rightarrow CH^{i+r}(X,j+s)
, which we denote by
\cup
and call the
cup product
. The cup product on cohomology
\oplus_{i,j}H^{i}(X,j)
(not the complexes!) is graded commutative in
i
and commutative in
j
.

Proof.

First, for a variety
X
, one forms the free abelian group
z^{n}(X,i,j)
generated by irreducible codimension
n
closed subschemes
W
of
X\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
, such that each irreducible component of
W\cap X\times A\times B
has codimension
n
on
X\times A\times B
for each face
A
of
\Delta^{i}
and
B
of
\Delta^{j}
. The
z^{n}(X,i,j)
form a bisimplicial abelian group and we let
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)
be the total complex of the associated double complex. Define the sub-double complex
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)_{\tau}
of
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)
generated by irreducible subschemes
W
of
X\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
such that
W\cap X\times A
has codimension
n
for each face
A
of
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
.

One obtains a map of complexes
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)_{\tau}\rightarrow z^{n}(X,*)
as follows: an injective map
g=(g_{1},g_{2}):[i+j]\rightarrow[i]\times[j]
determines a
(i,j)
-shuffle permutation, which sends
k\in\{1,...,i+j\}
to
g_{1}(k)
if
g_{1}(k-1)<g_{1}(k)
and to
g_{2}(k)+i
if
g_{2}(k-1)<g_{2}(k)
. Let
\textnormal{sgn}(g)
denote the sign of this shuffle permutation. Define a map
T_{i,j}:z^{q}(X,i,j)_{\tau}\rightarrow z^{q}(X,i+j)
as
\sum_{g}\textnormal{sgn}(g)\Delta g^{*}
, where
\Delta g^{*}
is the induced pullback map on cycles. Since the subvarieties in
z^{q}(X,i,j)_{\tau}
meet the faces of
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
properly by definition, the map is well defined, and we take
T=\sum T_{i,j}
for a map
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)_{\tau}\rightarrow z^{n}(X,*)
.

Finally, there is a map of complexes
\boxtimes:z^{n}(X,*)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}z^{m}(Y,*)\rightarrow\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*,*)
which sends a subvariety
V
of
X\times\Delta^{i}
and a subvariety
W
of
Y\times\Delta^{j}
to the product
V\times W
in
X\times Y\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}
.

The main lemma of the construction in the Appendix of
[
35
]
is showing that the inclusion of double complexes
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)_{\tau}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Tot }z^{n}(X,*,*)
is a quasi-isomorphism. We omit this proof here and defer it to the Appendix of
[
35
]
. This yields the following sequence of maps:

z^{n}(X,*)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}z^{m}(Y,*)\xrightarrow{\boxtimes}\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*,*)\xhookleftarrow{\simeq}\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*,*)_{\tau}\xrightarrow{T}z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*)
.

Since the cycle complexes are free abelian groups, this product passes to the derived category
D(\textbf{Ab})
. We thus obtain a product
z^{n}(X,*)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}z^{m}(Y,*)\rightarrow z^{n+m}(X\times Y,*)
in
D(\textbf{Ab})
.
∎

While the product is only defined up to quasi-isomorphism, we note that when it is defined on the chain level on certain subcomplexes that it is actually (graded) commutative. In section
3
we will analyze this product structure closer, and see that this defines a partial algebra structure on Bloch’s cycle complexes; this gives rise to a natural
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure. This will be the fundamental source of our Steenrod operations, in contrast to Voevodsky’s construction of operations in
[
81
]
. Interestingly enough, this also contrasts with the case of the cup product on singular cochains, where the product is well defined on the chain level but fails to be (graded) commutative there.

The cup product gives
\oplus_{i,j}H^{i}(X,j)
the structure of a bi-graded commutative graded ring for a smooth variety
X
. The product extends naturally to cohomology with support; we defer the details to Appendix
A
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism between smooth varieties. Then the induced pullback
f^{*}
is a ring homomorphism.

Proof.

We follow the same argument as in Theorem 5.2(a) of Levine
[
50
]
. Assume first that
Y
is smooth and affine.

We can again form the sub-double complex
z^{n}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f}
on which the pullback
(f\times f)^{*}
is defined, where
z^{n}(Y\times Y,i,j)_{f\times f}
is the subgroup generated by algebraic cycles
W
that meet
S_{k}(f\times f)\times A\times B
properly for all integers
k
and all faces
A
,
B
of
\Delta^{i}
and
\Delta^{j}
respectively. Notice that if
W\in z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}
and
V\in z^{m}(Y,*)_{f}
, then
W\times V\in z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f}
: first,
S_{k}(f\times f)=\bigcup_{r+s=k}S_{r}(f)\times S_{s}(f)
. It follows that
\textnormal{dim }S_{k}(f\times f)=\textnormal{max }\textnormal{dim }S_{r}(f)\times S_{s}(f)
where the maximum is taken over integers
r
and
s
that sum to
k
. Let
r_{0}
and
s_{0}
be the pair that yield the maximum dimension for
W\cap S_{r}(f)\times A
and
V\cap S_{s}(f)\times B
respectively, where
r+s=k
. Since
W
and
V
meet all
S_{r}(f)\times A
and
S_{s}(f)\times B
properly, for all integers
r,s
and faces
A,B
, we have that
\textnormal{dim}(W\times V\cap S_{k}(f\times f)\times A\times B)=\textnormal{dim}(W\times V\cap S_{r_{0}}(f)\times S_{s_{0}}(f)\times A\times B)\leq\textnormal{dim}(W\times V)+\textnormal{dim}(S_{r_{0}}(f)\times S_{s_{0}}(f)\times A\times B)-\textnormal{dim}(Y\times Y\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j})\leq\textnormal{dim}(W\times V)+\textnormal{dim}(S_{k}(f\times f)\times A\times B)-\textnormal{dim}(Y\times Y\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j})
. Thus
W\times V
meets
S_{k}(f\times f)\times A\times B
properly for all integers
k
and faces
A
and
B
. This also proves that the external product
\boxtimes
satisfies
(f\times f)^{*}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta)=f^{*}\alpha\boxtimes f^{*}\beta
since
(f\times f)^{-1}(W\times V)=f^{-1}W\times f^{-1}V
for algebraic cycles
W,V\in z^{*}(Y,*)_{f}
.

We then have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n}(Y,*)\otimes z^{m}(Y,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\otimes z^{m}(Y,*)_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f,\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*)_{f\times f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n}(X,*)\otimes z^{m}(X,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(X\times X,*,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(X\times X,*,*)_{\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{n+m}(X\times X,*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\otimes f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\times f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\times f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\times f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

which commutes by the above discussion and naturality of the maps constructed in Definition
2.5
and Proposition
2.2
. For brevity, we omit the proofs of the inclusions of
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f,\tau}
into
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{f\times f}
and
\textnormal{Tot }z^{n+m}(Y\times Y,*,*)_{\tau}
being quasi-isomorphisms.

Finally, since
\Delta_{Y}\circ f=(f\times f)\circ\Delta_{X}
, we have that
\Delta^{*}_{X}\circ(f\times f)^{*}=f^{*}\circ\Delta_{Y}^{*}
. By naturality of the quasi-isomorphism and the above relation, we conclude
f^{*}
is a ring homomorphism when
Y
is smooth and affine. For the general case, we again sheafify the construction and apply Zariski descent.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Levine gives an alternative construction of higher Chow groups in
[
50
]
, which uses cubical complexes. The product structure arises from the fact that the product of cubes is a cube. The arguments in
[
50
]
show the cubical complexes are naturally integrally quasi-isomorphic with the simplicial complexes. Moreover, this quasi-isomorphism should be compatible with the product structures once one defines the appropriate notion of pull-back for the simplicial complexes. The paper however does not define the integral pull-back for simplicial complexes, as they only define the pull-back for rational coefficients. As we will not use the cubical structure, we leave the product compatibility to the reader, using the pull-back defined earlier.

We conclude this section with a key lemma about algebraic cycles and the projection formula.

Lemma 2.2
.

(Proper base change for cycles)
Suppose we have a fiber product diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where all varieties are smooth, and
f
is proper. Suppose
W\in z^{n}(X,*)
is an algebraic cycle such that
g^{\prime*}W
is defined. Then
g^{*}f_{*}W
is defined, and
g^{*}f_{*}W=f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime*}W
.

Proof.

The lemma is a special case of Lemma 2.2(4) in Fulton
[
28
]
. See also Lemma 17A.10 in
[
61
]
.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

(Projection formula) Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper map between smooth varieties. We have the following formula:

f_{*}(\alpha\cup f^{*}\beta)=f_{*}\alpha\cup\beta

for
\alpha\in H^{*}(X,*)
and
\beta\in H^{*}(Y,*)
.

Proof.

We follow the same argument as in Theorem 5.2(b) of Levine
[
50
]
. By the argument in Proposition
2.3
, we have for
\alpha\in z^{*}(X,*)
and
\beta\in z^{*}(Y,*)_{f}
, that
\alpha\boxtimes\beta\in\textnormal{Tot }z^{*}(X\times Y,*,*)_{id\times f}
and that
(id\times f)^{*}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta)=\alpha\boxtimes f^{*}\beta
. Similarly, we have
(f\times id)_{*}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta)=f_{*}\alpha\boxtimes\beta\in\textnormal{Tot }z^{*}(Y\times Y,*,*)
. By naturality of the quasi-isomorphism and the map
T
in the definition of the external product
\times
, we moreover have these identities for the external product in place of
\boxtimes
. Finally, we have that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id\times f)\circ\Delta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{Y}}

is a fiber product diagram. Thus by lemma
2.2
, we have the identity
\Delta_{Y}^{*}(f\times id)_{*}=f_{*}\Delta_{X}^{*}(id\times f)^{*}
for cycles in
z^{n}(X\times Y,*)
for which
\Delta_{X}^{*}(id\times f)^{*}
is defined. We then have

\begin{split}f_{*}(\alpha\cup f^{*}\beta)&=f_{*}(\Delta_{X}^{*}(\alpha\times f^{*}\beta))\\
&=f_{*}(\Delta_{X}^{*}(id\times f^{*})(\alpha\times\beta))\\
&=\Delta_{Y}^{*}(f\times id)_{*}(\alpha\times\beta)\\
&=\Delta_{Y}^{*}(f_{*}\alpha\times\beta)\\
&=f_{*}a\cup\beta.\end{split}

∎

The projection formula also holds for cohomology with support, which we leave to the Appendix (Lemma
A.6
).

2.2

Twisted duality in the sense of Bloch–Ogus

Recall that, for a closed embedding
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
of a (possibly singular) variety into a smooth variety
P
, we have defined motivic cohomology with support as
H^{i}_{X}(P,j):=H^{i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(j))
. We briefly give an alternative construction below, which we hope provides a more geometric perspective than an abstract sheaf theoretic one.

Let
U:=P-X\xhookrightarrow{j}P
be the open immersion of the open complement of
X
. Since the map is flat, we can form the induced pull-back
j^{*}:z^{n}(P,*)\rightarrow z^{n}(U,*)
and its kernel, which we denote by
z^{n}_{X}(P,*)
. Supposing now that
X
has pure codimension
c
in
P
, we have the induced pushforward
z^{n-c}(X,*)\xrightarrow{i_{*}}z^{n}(P,*)
. Since
j^{*}i_{*}=0
, the map
i_{*}
factors through the kernel, so we have a map
z^{n-c}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{n}_{X}(P,*)
. Localization then says this map is a quasi-isomorphism.

We can sheafify and form the sheaf of chain complexes
\underline{z}^{n}_{X}(P,*)
, and re-index and re-grade as we did the ordinary cycle complexes. That is, we can consider the kernel sheaf of
\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\rightarrow j_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{U}(n)=j_{*}j^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)
, and this agrees with the suitably re-indexed and re-graded sheafification
\underline{z}^{n}_{X}(P,-*+2n)
.

By Zariski descent and naturality of localization, we have that
H^{i}_{X}(P,j)\cong H^{i}(P,\underline{z}^{n}_{X}(P,-*+2n))
and that the global sections of the complex of sheaves
i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(j)
are given precisely by the complex
z^{j}_{X}(P,-*+2j)
. We will call elements of
z^{*}_{X}(P,*)

cycles with support on
X
or
relative cycles
.

Below we present another corollary of localization, which we will call
purity
(see
[
33
]
). This is essentially the sheaf theoretic version of the discussion on relative cycles above, except phrased in terms of dimension rather than codimension.

Lemma 2.3
.

(Purity)
Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed embedding of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. We have an isomorphism in the derived category of abelian sheaves

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(n)\cong Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{P}(n)
.

for any
n
.

Proof.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed embedding of
X
into
P
with open complement
U\xhookrightarrow{j}P
. We have two distinguished triangles:

i_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{P}(n)\rightarrow j_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)

and

i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rightarrow Rj_{*}j^{*}\mathcal{F}

where the first triangle exists by localization, and the second holds for any abelian sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
P
. Since
j^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{P}(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)
, it suffices to show
Rj_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)\cong j_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)
. This follows from the same argument as in Corollary 3.3 of
[
31
]
: one proves the quasi-isomorphism stalkwise over the point
x\in Spec(k)
. Let
p
denote the structure morphism for
P
to
Spec(k)
. We then have

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{s}((p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n))_{x}&\cong H^{s}(\Gamma(P,(p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)))\\
&\cong H^{s}(P,(p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n))\\
&\cong H^{s}(R\Gamma(P,(p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)))\end{split}

where the first isomorphism holds by definition of the stalk of the cohomology sheaf, the second holds by Theorem 3.2 in
[
31
]
(i.e., by localization and Thomason’s criterion), and the third by definition of the stalk of higher direct image. This gives us a quasi-isomorphism
(p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)\cong R(p\circ j)_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{U}(n)
. We then apply
p^{*}
to finish the proof.
∎

We are now ready to discuss Poincaré duality for higher Chow groups. Before we continue, we must emphasize that higher Chow groups are somewhat misleading when it comes to Poincaré duality: as mentioned in Remark
2.1
, for an irreducible
d
-dimensional variety
X
(singular or not) there is an abstract isomorphism of groups
z_{n}(X,*)\cong z^{d-n}(X,*)
. However, as we have seen so far in the previous sections, higher Chow groups only behave like a cohomology theory for smooth varieties. They should really be viewed naturally as a Borel-Moore homology; thus, the non-trivial aspect of Poincaré duality for higher Chow groups is not the abstract isomorphism of groups, but contravariant functoriality and the presence of a product structure, which are properties obtained if
X
is smooth.

Let us call by
\chi
the naïve isomorphism of groups
z_{n}(X,*)\cong z^{d-n}(X,*)
for a
d
-dimensional irreducible variety
X
, given by simply re-indexing from dimension to codimension. By abuse of notation, we will use
\chi
also to denote the quasi-isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{X}(d-n)[2d]
of complexes of sheaves induced by the isomorphism of groups. Always keep in mind that this re-indexing is purely formal, and that it is only when the space is smooth that the higher Chow groups behave like a cohomology.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
of dimension
d
. We have the following isomorphism

\chi\circ i_{*}:H_{i}(X,j)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H_{X}^{2d-i}(P,d-j)

which we will refer to as
twisted Poincaré duality
.

Proof.

We have by localization that
z_{j}(X,*)\xrightarrow{i_{*}}z_{j}^{X}(P,*)
is a quasi-isomorphism, where
z_{j}^{X}(P,*)
denotes cycles with support on
X
graded by dimension. The map
i_{*}
induces the purity isomorphism on the level of sheaves (Lemma
2.3
), which gives the isomorphism of groups
i_{*}:H_{i}(X,j)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{X}_{i}(P,j)
, where
H^{X}_{i}(P,j)
denotes the cohomology of the cycles with support again graded by dimension. That is,
H^{X}_{i}(P,j):=H^{-i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{P}(j))
. In other words, we have the following chain of isomorphisms:

\begin{split}H_{i}(X,j)&:=H^{-i}R\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(j))\\
&\cong H^{-i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{P}(j))\\
&\cong H^{-i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(d-j)[2d])\\
&\cong H^{2d-i}R\Gamma(P,i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(d-j)):=H^{2d-i}_{X}(P,d-j)\end{split}

where the first isomorphism is induced by the pushforward
i_{*}:H_{i}(X,j)\rightarrow H_{i}^{X}(P,j)
, the second isomorphism is
\chi
re-indexing from dimension to codimension, and the third is re-grading the complexes appropriately.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. Then
H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
is a
H^{*}(P,*)
-module with respect to cup product.

We defer the proof of the above property to the Appendix (Lemma
A.4
).

Definition 2.8
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed embedding with
P
smooth. We define the
(twisted) cap product

\cap
as the following composition of maps:

H^{r}_{X}(P,s)\otimes H_{i}(P,j)\xrightarrow{id\otimes\chi}H^{r}_{X}(P,s)\otimes H^{2d-i}(P,d-j)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{2d-i+r}_{X}(P,d-j+s)\cong H_{i-r}(X,j-s)

where the last isomorphism is the inverse of the twisted duality isomorphism of Theorem
2.2
. In particular, if
P
is
d
-dimensional, the map
-\cap[P]:H^{r}_{X}(P,s)\rightarrow H_{2d-r}(X,d-s)
is exactly the inverse of the twisted duality isomorphism of Theorem
2.2
, since
\chi[P]=1
. We will then denote this abstract inverse by
-\cap[P]
.

Now suppose
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
is a closed immersion, where
X
is of pure codimension
c
in smooth
P
. We refer to the class in
H_{X}^{2c}(P,c)
corresponding to the fundamental class
[X]\in H_{2d-2c}(X,d-c)
under duality as the
dual class
, which we denote by
\tau
.

We also have the following corollary, which is somewhat trivial from the point of view of higher Chow groups (due to how duality is defined). However we state it nonetheless for completion. We also only state it for the case when the subvariety
X
is smooth, as that is when the higher Chow groups of
X
are only useful as a cohomology theory; indeed, the second statement of Corollary
2.1
only makes sense when
X
is smooth, as that is when the pullback of the embedding is defined.

Corollary 2.1
.

(Cohomological purity)
Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a regular embedding of codimension
c
, with
X
and
P
smooth. We have an isomorphism

H^{i}(X,j)\cong H^{i+2c}_{X}(P,j+c)

for all
i,j\geq 0
given by the pushforward
i_{*}
. Moreover, we have
i_{*}i^{*}\alpha=\tau\cup\alpha\in H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
for
\alpha\in H^{*}(P,*)
.

Proof.

Let
d^{\prime}=\textnormal{dim }X
. We have that the composition of maps

H^{i}(X,j)\xrightarrow{\chi^{-1}_{X}}H_{2d^{\prime}-i}(X,d^{\prime}-j)\xrightarrow{\chi_{P}\circ i_{*}}H^{2d-(2d^{\prime}-i)}_{X}(P,d-(d^{\prime}-j))

is a composition of isomorphisms. Since the
\chi
maps are formal re-grading between dimension and codimension, it follows that the composition agrees with
i_{*}
(the push-forward defined on cohomology). For the latter formula, we use the projection formula (with support): since
i_{*}(1)=\tau
the dual class, we have that
i_{*}i^{*}\alpha=i_{*}(1\cup i^{*}\alpha)=i_{*}(1)\cup\alpha=\tau\cup\alpha
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

There is a similar purity result due to Morel–Voevodsky (Theorem 2.23 in
[
60
]
) which requires a topology as fine as the Nisnevich topology, but the result is much stronger than what we stated or need; the Morel–Voevodsky purity theorem yields an equivalence in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category. Corollary
2.1
holds for both the Nisnevich and Zariski topologies.

We now contextualize the cohomological purity theorem to a setting more familiar to topologists.

Corollary 2.2
.

(Thom isomorphism) Let
E\xrightarrow{\pi}X
be a vector bundle of rank
c
with
X
smooth. We then have an isomorphism

H^{i}(X,j)\cong H^{i+2c}_{X}(E,j+c)

for all
i,j
given by the pushforward induced by the zero section
s_{*}
. In this case, we call the dual class
s_{*}(1)=\tau\in H^{2c}_{X}(E,c)
the
Thom class of
E
. Moreover, the pushforward induced by the zero section satisfies the formula
s_{*}x=\tau\cup\pi^{*}x
for all
x\in H^{*}(X,*)
.

Proof.

We only prove the last statement. Since
E\xrightarrow{\pi}X
is a vector bundle, by homotopy invariance we have that
\pi^{*}
induces an isomorphism on higher Chow groups, with inverse given by
s^{*}
. Thus by the projection formula above, we have

\tau\cup\pi^{*}x=s_{*}s^{*}\pi^{*}x=s_{*}x.

Thus we recover the usual Thom isomorphism from topology.
∎

We conclude the section with a definition.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
and
X^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
be closed immersions into smooth varieties
P
and
P^{\prime}
, and suppose we have proper maps
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

We define the map
f_{!}:H^{*}_{X}(P,*)\rightarrow H^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},*)
as the composition
f_{!}:=(\chi_{P^{\prime}}\circ i^{\prime}_{*})\circ f^{\prime}_{*}\circ(\chi_{P}\circ i_{*})^{-1}
, where
f^{\prime}_{*}
is the induced pushforward map pre- and post-composed with the twisted duality isomorphisms. We call
f_{!}
the
backwards map
or the
projective pushforward (with supports)
on motivic cohomology with support.

Notice that if we forget the supports
X
and
X^{\prime}
(that is, we take
X=P
and
X^{\prime}=P^{\prime}
), then the backwards map
f_{!}
is simply the induced pushforward
f_{*}
by the proper map
f
on the higher Chow groups (albeit regraded cohomologically by Poincaré duality). Indeed,
f_{!}
is exactly the proper pushforward induced on cohomology with support, under the assumption
f(X)\subset X^{\prime}
.

We stress that the backwards map does not preserve the bidegree despite the notation, as the dimensions of
P
and
P^{\prime}
may differ in general. By duality, we have that the map sends
H^{i}_{X}(P,j)
to
H^{i+2d^{\prime}-2d}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},j+d^{\prime}-d)
where
d^{\prime}
and
d
are the dimensions of
P^{\prime}
and
P
respectively. However, in this paper we will only be concerned when the maps
f
and
g
are birational or finite, so we will assume the dimensions of
P
and
P^{\prime}
are equal. In this case, the bidegrees are indeed preserved.

The reason we emphasize these definitions, though they may seem tautological or trivial, is that we will work with supports
X
that can be arbitrarily singular. In this case, the cohomology of
X
is not well understood, but the homology of
X
can be handled by realizing it as the cohomology with support of the ambient space
P
, which is always assumed smooth.

2.3

The cycle class map and mod
\ell
étale cohomology

In this section we discuss the cycle class map from higher Chow groups into étale cohomology. We will take coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n):=\mathbb{Z}(n)\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
, where
\mathbb{Z}(n)
denotes Bloch’s cycle complex graded by codimension and appropriately sheafified, and
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
denote constant sheaves, where
\ell
is prime to the characteristic of the underlying field.

We briefly sketch the construction of the
cycle class map
following 2.3 in
[
52
]
with details provided from 3.4 and 3.7 in
[
35
]
. The mod
\ell
cycle class map is a map

\textnormal{cl}^{q}:H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))\rightarrow H^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

(1)

where
P
is a smooth variety, and
\mu_{\ell}
denotes the sheaf of
\ell
-th roots of unity, with the
q
-fold tensor product over
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
. That is,
\mu_{\ell}
fits in the short exact sequence of étale sheaves

0\rightarrow\mu_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{*}_{P_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{(-)^{\ell}}\mathcal{O}^{*}_{P_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}}\rightarrow 0

and in fact, since we assume the underlying field
k
is algebraically closed, we have that
\mu_{\ell}
is isomorphic to the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
.

We recall the
Godement resolution
(see
[
36
]
or Tag 0FKR in
[
71
]
) for an étale sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on our variety
P
: let
P^{\prime}
denote the disjoint union of schemes
\bigsqcup_{x\in P}\overline{x}
, where
u_{x}:\overline{x}\rightarrow x
is a geometric point for each
x
in
P
. We then have a map
u:P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
, and for every étale sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
P
, we obtain a canonical flasque resolution

\mathcal{F}\rightarrow u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow u_{*}u^{*}u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow...

of étale sheaves. In fact, the resolution is the complex associated to the cosimplicial object (with augmentation) given by the natural maps of functors

id
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}u^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{*}u^{*}u_{*}u^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

induced by iterated adjunctions (see Tag 08N8 in
[
71
]
). We now let
G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
denote the global sections of the Godement resolution applied to the sheaf of
\ell
-th roots of unity on
P
. We extend the definition of the Godement resolution to cosimplicial schemes
Y^{\bullet}
as follows: we first form the double complex
G^{a}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(Y^{-b},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
and then form the total complex where, in degree
n
, we have
G^{n}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(Y^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell}):=\prod_{a+b=n}G^{a}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(Y^{-b},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
with corresponding differential. Moreover, we define the
Godement resolution with support
as

G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},X}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell}):=\textnormal{cone}(j^{*}:G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})\rightarrow G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P-X,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell}))[-1]

where
j^{*}
is the induced pullback from the open inclusion
P-X\xhookrightarrow{j}P
. For more detail on extending the Godement resolution to cosimplicial schemes and the case with support, see 3.1 in
[
35
]
.

The cycle class map depends on a chain level construction, which we recall here. Suppose that
X
is a closed subvariety of a smooth variety
P
, and suppose that
X
is also smooth of codimension
q
. We denote by
z^{q}_{X}(P)
the group of codimension
q

cycles on
P
with support in
X
. By the purity theorem for étale cohomology (Theorem 5.1, Chapter VI in
[
59
]
) and the construction given in Cycles, Chapter 2 in
[
18
]
, there is a canonical isomorphism
\textnormal{cyc}^{q}_{X}:z^{q}_{X}(P)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2q}_{X}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
. One extends the result that there is a canonical isomorphism
\textnormal{cyc}^{q}_{X}
for arbitrary closed subvarieties
X
by noetherian induction and localization for étale cohomology with support.

We write
G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},(q)}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell}):=\varinjlim_{W\in z^{q}(P,\bullet)}G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},W}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
using the notion of a relative Godement resolution for a cosimplicial scheme (see 3.1 in
[
35
]
). We then obtain a diagram

z^{q}(P,*)[-2q]\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell\xrightarrow{\textnormal{cyc}^{q}_{(q)}[-2q]}H^{2q}(G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},(q)}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell}))[-2q]\xleftarrow{\alpha}\tau_{\leq 2q}G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},(q)}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\beta}G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

where
\textnormal{cyc}^{q}_{(q)}
is the induced direct limit of the maps
\textnormal{cyc}^{q}
without support,
\tau_{\leq 2q}G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},(q)}(-,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
denotes the truncation where groups are
0
above
2q
and
\alpha
is the composition of inclusion and the map to cohomology, and
\beta
denotes the composition of inclusion and the direct limit of maps from the Godement resolutions with support. Lemma 3.8 in
[
35
]
proves
\alpha
is a quasi-isomorphism, which gives us a map

z^{q}(P,*)[-2q]\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell\rightarrow G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

in
D({\textnormal{{Ab}}})
. Moreover, by the homotopy property of étale cohomology, we have that the map induced by projection
G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})\rightarrow G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
is a quasi-isomorphism. Composing the two maps in
D(\textnormal{{Ab}})
gives us the map

\textnormal{cl}^{q}:z^{q}(P,*)[-2q]\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell\rightarrow G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

which then passes to cohomology as the cycle class map
\textnormal{cl}^{q}:H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))\rightarrow H^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
. Assuming
X
is a closed subvariety of
P
and restricting the construction given above to relative cycles and relative cohomology all throughout, we further obtain a
relative cycle map

\textnormal{cl}^{q}_{X}:z^{q}_{X}(P,*)[-2q]\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell\rightarrow G^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},X}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

which also descends to cohomology
\textnormal{cl}^{q}_{X}:H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))\rightarrow H^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t},X}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
.

Proposition 2.5
.

The cycle class maps satisfy the following properties:

1.

The cycle class maps are natural with respect to maps between smooth varieties. Moreover, consider the category of pairs
(P,X)
where
X
is closed in
P
, and a morphism
(P,X)\rightarrow(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is given by a map
f:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
such that
f^{-1}(X^{\prime})\subset X
. Such a morphism induces the pullback map on cohomology with supports, and the relative cycle class map commutes with this induced pullback map.

2.

For
P
smooth, the map
\textnormal{cl}:\oplus_{p,q}H^{p}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))\xrightarrow{\oplus\textnormal{cl}^{q}}\oplus_{p,q}H^{p}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})
is a ring homomorphism (of bi-graded rings).

3.

The cycle class maps are compatible with the localization sequences and Gysin homomorphisms.

For the proof of the above proposition, see Proposition 4.2, Proposition 4.5, and Proposition 4.7 in
[
35
]
.

We now discuss the
motivic Bott element
following
[
52
]
. We have for a field
k
that

H^{p}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}(1))=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $p\neq 1$}\\
k^{\times}&\text{if $p=1$}.\end{cases}

The long exact sequence associated to the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}(1)\xrightarrow{\cdot\ell}\mathbb{Z}(1)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1)\rightarrow 0

of motivic complexes gives an isomorphism
H^{0}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))\cong\mu_{\ell}(k)
. Since we assume our underlying field
k
is algebraically closed, there exist primitive
\ell
-th roots of unity. A choice
\zeta
of primitive
\ell
-th root of unity then gives us a corresponding element
B
in
H^{0}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
under the aforementioned isomorphism. In fact, we have that
H^{0}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))\cong\mu_{\ell}(P)
for any smooth variety
P
over
k
(see p. vi in
[
61
]
) and so our choice of
B
in
H^{0}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
gives a canonical choice of
B
for every
P
.

The cycle class map
\textnormal{cl}:H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))\rightarrow H^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes*}_{\ell})
is a ring homomorphism with
\textnormal{cl}(B)=\zeta
. As
k
contains
\zeta
, multiplication by
\zeta
induces an isomorphism on étale cohomology, and so
cl
induces the ring map
H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))[B^{-1}]\rightarrow H^{*}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(P,\mu^{\otimes*}_{\ell})
. Levine in
[
52
]
proves this induced map is an isomorphism (for fields not necessarily algebraically closed), using results of Suslin–Voevodsky
[
73
]
for algebraically closed fields of characteristic
0
and de Jong’s alterations
[
19
]
in characteristic
p
.

We conclude the section on the cycle class map with the following theorem.

Theorem 2.3
.

(Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture)
Let
X
be a smooth variety over a field of characteristic
p
. Then the cycle class map

\textnormal{cl}^{q}:H^{p}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))\rightarrow H^{p}_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}}(X,\mu^{\otimes q}_{\ell})

is an isomorphism for all
p\leq q
.

Theorem
2.3
has a rich and deep history which we shall not explain here; its history can be read in the introduction of
[
41
]
. It was implied as a corollary of the norm residue isomorphism theorem, which can be explained in detail in
[
82
]
. See also
[
80
]
.

2.4

Chern classes

The theory of ordinary Chow groups has well defined Chern classes (see, for example, Fulton (Section 3.2 in
[
29
]
)). Since ordinary Chow groups are the
0
-th homology of higher Chow groups, Bloch defines Chern classes in
[
9
]
using the same definition as Fulton.

Proposition 2.6
.

(Chern classes) For
\pi:E\rightarrow X
a vector bundle of rank
n
, there exist
Chern class
operators
c_{i}(E)\cap-:CH^{a}(X,b)\rightarrow CH^{a+i}(X,b)
satisfying the following properties:

1.

(Vanishing) For
i>n
, the operators
c_{i}(E)\cap-
are zero.

2.

(Commutativity) For bundles
E,F
on
X
, we have
c_{i}(E)\cap((c_{j}(F)\cap\alpha))=c_{j}(F)\cap((c_{i}(E)\cap\alpha))
.

3.

(Projection formula) Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a proper morphism. Then
f_{*}(c_{i}(f^{*}E)\cap\alpha)=c_{i}(E)\cap f_{*}(\alpha)
.

4.

(Flat pullback) Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a flat morphism. Then
c_{i}(f^{*}E)\cap f^{*}\alpha=f^{*}(c_{i}(E)\cap\alpha)
.

5.

(Whitney sum) For any exact sequence of bundles on
X

0\rightarrow E^{\prime}\rightarrow E\rightarrow E^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0

we have
c(E)=c(E^{\prime})c(E^{\prime\prime})
, where
c(E)
denotes the
total Chern class
given by the sum
c(E)\cap-=\Sigma_{i\geq 0}^{n}c_{i}(E)\cap-
.

6.

(Projective bundle formula) Let
\mathbb{P}E\xrightarrow{p}X
denote the projective bundle associated to the bundle
E\xrightarrow{\pi}X
. Let
\xi:=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(1))\cap-
denote the first Chern class of the dual of the tautological bundle of subspaces over
\mathbb{P}E
. One has the identity

\xi^{n}(-)+c_{1}(p^{*}E)\cap(\xi^{n-1}-)+...+c_{n}(p^{*}E)\cap-=0

and the isomorphism for any
m\geq 0

\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\xi^{i}\circ p^{*}:\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\bigoplus_{r\geq 0}CH^{r}(X,m)\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{s\geq 0}CH^{s}(\mathbb{P}E,m).

In particular, if
X
is smooth, there is a graded isomorphism of
CH^{*}(X,0)
-modules

CH^{*}(\mathbb{P}E,m)\cong CH^{*}(X,m)\otimes_{CH^{*}(X,0)}CH^{*}(\mathbb{P}E,0).

When
X
is smooth, with vector bundle
E\rightarrow X
, the Chern class operations
c_{i}(E)\cap-
coincide with multiplication by the classes
c_{i}(E)\cap[X]\in CH^{i}(X,0)
. In this case, we often omit the
-\cap[X]
and simply write
c_{i}(E)
for the Chern classes. As a concrete example, recall that for an isomorphism class of line bundle
[L]
(equivalently, an isomorphsim class of invertible sheaf), we have a unique associated rational equivalence class of divisor
[D_{L}]
, given by the zero set of a generic section of
L
. The first Chern class
c_{1}(L)
is then precisely the element
[D_{L}]
in the ordinary Chow group
CH^{1}(X)
. Moreover, recall that if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are two line bundles on a smooth variety
X
, then we have that
c_{1}(L\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}L^{\prime})=c_{1}(L)+c_{1}(L^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(Splitting principle) Let
X
be a smooth variety with a vector bundle
E
of rank
n
. Then there exists a smooth variety
Y=Fl(E)
with map
f
to
X
such that the following statements hold:

1.

f^{*}E
is a direct sum of line bundles over
Y
,

2.

for any closed subvariety
Z
in
X
, the induced map
f^{*}:H^{*}_{Z}(X,*)\rightarrow H^{*}_{f^{-1}(Z)}(Y,*)
is a split injection,

3.

for any smooth variety
X^{\prime}
and any map
g:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
, the pullback map
Y\times_{X}X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
induces a split injection on cohomology.

Proof.

Here we outline the splitting principle following Lemma 3.24 in
[
63
]
. First one forms the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}E\xrightarrow{p}X
and we consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{E}(-1)\rightarrow p^{*}E\rightarrow E^{\prime}\rightarrow 0

where
E^{\prime}
is a bundle over
\mathbb{P}E
of rank
n-1
. The map
p^{*}
induces a split injection on cohomology by the projective bundle formula. To obtain
f:Y\rightarrow X
, we iterate this construction and obtain
Y
as the associated flag bundle
Fl(E)
; one moreover obtains a filtration
(0)\subset E_{1}\subset E_{2}\subset...\subset E_{n}=f^{*}E
of the bundle
f^{*}E
such that
E_{i}/E_{i-1}
are all line bundles.
∎

We note that in the splitting principle, all we crucially used was the projective bundle formula. This point will arise again in the axiomatic setting of section
3.2
, where we will see that the splitting principle and projective bundle formula both hold for generalized cohomology theories.

2.5

Motivic cohomology

We now introduce the motivic complexes in the sense of Suslin and Voevodsky
[
74
]
,
[
61
]
,
[
78
]
.

Definition 2.10
.

For a smooth variety
X
, define
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X)
as the presheaf with transfers (i.e., an additive contravariant functor
SmCor_{k}\rightarrow\textbf{Ab}
) which assigns to each smooth scheme
U
of finite type over
k
the abelian group
Cor_{k}(U,X)
. This abelian group is generated by closed integral subschemes of
U\times_{k}X
finite and surjective over
U
.

Define the
motivic complexes
as follows: let
\mathbb{G}_{m}
denote the pointed scheme
(\mathbb{A}^{1}-0,1)
. Define
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
as the cokernel of the map
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{1}-0)
induced by the specified point.

Let
(X_{1},x_{1}),...,(X_{n},x_{n})
be
n
pointed schemes. Define
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\wedge...\wedge X_{n})
as the cokernel of the map
\oplus^{n}_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times_{k}...\hat{X_{i}}...\times_{k}X_{n})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times_{k}...\times_{k}X_{n})
given by
id\times...x_{i}...\times id
, where
x_{i}
is the map induced by the specified point.

Let
C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m})
be the chain complex obtained from the simplicial presheaf of abelian groups
U\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m})(U\times_{k}\Delta^{\bullet})
, where again
\Delta^{\bullet}
denotes the algebraic simplex.

Finally, we re-grade our complex of presheaves and let
\mathbb{Z}(n):=C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n})[-2n]
. We can restrict this presheaf of chain complexes defined on
SmCor_{k}
to the small Zariski site of a smooth variety
X
to obtain a complex of Zariski sheaves. We denote this complex by
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
and call this the
n
-th motivic complex of
X
.

We will often omit the subscript
X
in
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
if the specified space is clear within context. We are at liberty to use the same notation as for Bloch’s cycle complexes for smooth varieties due to the following theorem of Voevodsky
[
79
]
. Note that the proof does not use resolution of singularities.

Theorem 2.4
.

(Voevodsky) Let
X
be a smooth variety. Then there is a natural quasi-isomorphism of complexes of Nisnevich (and therefore Zariski) sheaves

\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)\cong z^{n}(-,*)[-2n]

where the left-hand side denotes motivic complexes in the sense of Suslin–Voevodsky, and the right-hand side denotes Bloch’s cycle complex regraded (cohomologically) and re-indexed (with the sheaf
z^{n}(-,-i+2n)
in degree
i
).

By Voevodsky’s theorem, we can abuse notation and write
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
to mean either the motivic complex or Bloch’s cycle complex graded cohomologically, as they are quasi-isomorphic. For most of this paper the distinction will not matter, and when it does we shall specify which complex we mean.

Remark 2.6
.

Since we will only work with cohomology for smooth varieties, the hypercohomology groups defined by the Zariski and Nisnevich topologies agree (see Theorem 1.1 in
[
74
]
).

Note that if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a map between smooth varieties, then it is much easier to construct an induced pullback: since motivic cohomology is defined as the cohomology of sheaves on the small Zariski site, the restriction maps
f^{-1}(U)\rightarrow U
for open
U
in
Y
induce the map on sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{Y}(n)\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
. This in turn induces the pullback
f^{*}
on motivic cohomology. We state this to emphasize again that higher Chow groups act more naturally as a Borel-Moore homology theory, whereas motivic is designed to be a cohomology theory. Of course, the quasi-isomorphism of Theorem
2.4
is natural so that the induced pull-backs all agree with those for higher Chow groups.

Proposition 2.7
.

(Étale excision) Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be an étale morphism between smooth varieties, and let
Z\subset Y
be a closed subvariety such that the map
Z^{\prime}=f^{-1}(Z)\xrightarrow{f|_{Z^{\prime}}}Z
is an isomorphism. Then the induced map
f^{*}:H^{i}_{Z}(Y,j)\rightarrow H^{i}_{Z^{\prime}}(X,j)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is a corollary of twisted duality. Since
f:Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
is an isomorphism, we obtain an isomorphism on homology
H^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(X,*)\cong H_{*}(Z^{\prime},*)\xrightarrow{f_{*}}H_{*}(Z,*)\cong H^{*}_{Z}(Y,*)
