. The bi-degrees do not change as the map is étale. The inverse of the isomorphism is the induced map
f^{*}
on pairs. See also Lemma 4.11 in
[
74
]
.
∎

We conclude this section with a discussion on product structures, following Construction 3.11 in
[
61
]
. First, for pointed schemes
(X_{s},x_{s})
for
s=1,...,j
, we define a map

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\wedge...\wedge X_{i})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{i+1}\wedge...\wedge X_{j})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\wedge...\wedge X_{j})

for every
i<j
as follows: for every smooth scheme
U
of finite type, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times...\times X_{i})(U)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{i+1}\times...\times X_{j})(U)&=Cor(U,X_{1}\times...\times X_{i})\otimes Cor(U,X_{i+1}\times...\times X_{j})\\
&\rightarrow Cor(U\times U,X_{1}\times...\times X_{j})\end{split}

(2)

where the arrow sends a closed subscheme
V
of
U\times X_{1}\times...\times X_{i}
and a closed subscheme
W
of
U\times X_{i+1}\times...\times X_{j}
to a closed subscheme
V\times W
of
U\times U\times X_{1}\times...X_{j}
. One then composes with the pullback induced by the diagonal
\Delta:U\rightarrow U\times U
to obtain a map

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times...X_{i})(U)\otimes Z_{tr}(X_{i+1}\times...X_{j})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times...\times X_{j})(U)

(3)

where by pullback, we mean composition with the correspondence associated to
\Delta
(see Chapters 1 and 2 in
[
61
]
). By definition, the presheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\wedge...\wedge X_{j})
are quotients of the presheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(X_{1}\times...\times X_{j})
, and one checks that the map
3
descends to the quotients.

Finally, to define the product structure, we need to define a map

\mathbb{Z}(m)\otimes\mathbb{Z}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}(m+n)

using the map
3
on quotients, applied to
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{m})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{m+n})
. Let
U
be a smooth scheme. The map
3
gives us a map

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m}_{m})(U\times\Delta^{i})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n})(U\times\Delta^{i})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m+n}_{m})(U\times\Delta^{i})

(4)

for every
i
. The map
4
induces a simplicial map from the diagonal simplicial complex

\textnormal{diag}(\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m}_{m}(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m}(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})))\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m+n}_{m}(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})

which in turn induces a map on the associated chain complexes. Finally, we have the quasi-isomorphism given by the Eilenberg–Zilbur map (Theorem 8.5.1 in
[
83
]
)

C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m}_{m})(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})\otimes C_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m})(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\simeq}C_{*}(\textnormal{diag}(\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{m}_{m}(U\times\Delta^{\bullet}))\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{G}^{n}_{m}(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})))

where
C_{*}(-)
denotes the chain complex associated to the given simplicial object. Composing the Eilenberg–Zilbur map with the map induced by the simplicial map (with appropriate degree shifts) yields the product structure.

Weibel
[
85
]
proved that the original comparison theorem of Voevodsky, which relied on resolution of singularities, was an isomorphism of rings. Kondo and Yasuda
[
48
]
then extended Weibel’s result to show that the comparison theorem of Voevodsky which does not rely on resolution (Theorem
2.4
) is also an isomorphism of rings. Thus we can identify the two product structures (one on higher Chow groups and the other on motivic complexes) without assuming resolution.

Theorem 2.5
.

(Kondo–Yasuda)
The natural quasi-isomorphism of Theorem
2.4
induces an isomorphism of graded rings.

We will add one last discussion regarding the presheaves with transfer
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(-)
following Chapter 12 in
[
27
]
, as later we will see that Joshua
[
43
]
uses them to construct explicit
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures on motivic cohomology. First we note an alternative equivalent definition of motivic cohomology.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})
denote the cohomologically graded complex of sheaves where in degree
i
we have the sheaf
C^{i}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q}):=\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})(-\times\Delta^{i})
.

We have the following theorem of Friedlander and Suslin (Proposition 12.1 in
[
27
]
).

Theorem 2.6
.

(Friedlander–Suslin)
For any smooth variety
X
, the complex of sheaves
C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex of sheaves given by Bloch’s cycle complexes
z^{q}(-,*)
.

In fact, we note here that Voevodsky’s equivalence (Theorem
2.4
) passes through the above equivalence: Voevodsky proves his motivic complexes
\mathbb{Z}(q)
are quasi-isomorphic to
C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})[-2q]
. As a result, the theorem of Kondo–Yasuda (and all others mentioned above) pass through the above theorem of Friedlander–Suslin.

We now analyze the product structure on
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})
as these are the complexes on which Joshua builds an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure. Note that we have a pairing

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m})(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{n})(Y)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times Y)

(5)

by taking the direct product of cycles. We then obtain a natural map of bisimplicial abelian groups

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times X\times\Delta^{\bullet}\times\Delta^{\bullet})

(6)

which we can then post-compose with the pullback defined via the correspondence associated to the diagonal
X\rightarrow X\times X
(see 1A.6 and 1A.8 in
[
61
]
), to obtain a map of bisimplicial abelian groups

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet}\times\Delta^{\bullet}).

(7)

We then consider the associated total complex
\textnormal{Tot}(\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet}\times\Delta^{\bullet}))
. We have a natural map
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\rightarrow\textnormal{Tot}(\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet}\times\Delta^{\bullet}))
defined as pull-back induced by projection to the first simplicial component, which is actually a homotopy equivalence (see section 0 in
[
74
]
): the homotopy inverse is given explicitly as the shuffle map

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j})\xrightarrow{\sum_{\phi:[i+j]\rightarrow[i]\times[j]}\epsilon(\phi)\phi^{*}}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{i+j})

(8)

where the sum is over all strictly increasing maps
\phi:[i+j]\rightarrow[i]\times[j]
,
\epsilon(\phi)
is the sign of the unique corresponding shuffle, and (by abuse of notation)
\phi
also denotes the linear isomorphism of schemes
\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}\rightarrow\Delta^{i+j}
which agrees with
\phi
on the vertices. This gives us an explicit canonical pairing of complexes of sheaves

\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{m+n})(X\times\Delta^{\bullet})

given by the formula

V\otimes W\mapsto\sum_{(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}):[i+j]\rightarrow[i]\times[j]}\epsilon((\phi_{1},\phi_{2}))\phi_{1}^{*}(V)\times_{X\times\Delta^{i+j}}\phi_{2}^{*}(W).

So again we see that, similar to Bloch’s cycle complexes, the external product requires passing to the derived category to be well defined, despite being graded-commutative on potential subcomplexes where it is defined. We conclude the section with a slight digression to yet another interpretation of motivic cohomology.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
Z
be a scheme of finite type over
S
such that every irreducible component of
Z
dominates a component of
S
. We say
Z
is
equidimensional over
S
of relative dimension
m
if either every fiber
Z_{s}
is empty (for every
s\in S
), or each of its components have dimension
m
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
X
be a variety and let
r\geq 0
be an integer. For every smooth scheme
S
over
k
, we let
z_{equi}(X,r)(S)
denote the free abelian group generated by the closed and irreducible subvarieties of
S\times X
which are equidimensional of relative dimension
r
over a component of
S
.

Given a map
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, one can define a natural pullback of relative cycles (see 1A.6 and 1A.8 in
[
61
]
) which induces a map
z_{equi}(T,r)(S)\rightarrow z_{equi}(T,r)(S^{\prime})
. This then defines a presheaf
z_{equi}(X,r):Sm/k\rightarrow\textnormal{{Ab}}
. In fact, this is a Zariski sheaf for every
X
and every
r
, and satisfies the usual properties of contravariance for flat maps and covariance for proper maps in
X
(with suitable shifts in dimension).

Definition 2.14
.

We let
\mathbb{Z}^{SF}(i):=C_{*}z_{equi}(\mathbb{A}^{i},0)[-2i]
be the chain complex obtained from the simplicial presheaf of abelian groups
U\rightarrow z_{equi}(\mathbb{A}^{i},0)(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})
with shift in degree. We call the
\mathbb{Z}^{SF}(i)
the
Suslin–Friedlander complexes
.

It is immediate from the definition that the sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})
are subsheaves of
z_{equi}(\mathbb{A}^{q},0)
. The theorem of Voevodsky (Theorem
2.4
) moreover says that
\mathbb{Z}^{SF}(i)
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}(i)
as complexes of Zariski sheaves. Finally, we describe the product structure on the Suslin–Friedlander complexes as follows: for smooth varieties
X,Y,
and
U
, by taking the fiber product of cycles, we obtain a map

z_{equi}(X,r)(U)\otimes z_{equi}(Y,s)(U)\rightarrow z_{equi}(X\times Y,r+s)(U)

since if
V
in
U\times X
is equidimensional of dimension
r
over
U
, and
W
in
U\times Y
is equidimensional of dimension
s
over
U
, then
V\times_{U}W
is equidimensional of dimension
r+s
over
U
. To extend this product structure on cycles to the complexes themselves seems to require resolution of singularities (see Proposition 8.6 and Proposition 8.7 in
[
30
]
) so we will not analyze it further; however, it stands to reason that it should agree with the product structure we have considered on
C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})[-2q]
without using resolution.

3

Generalized cohomology, proper pushforwards, and ring operations

In this section, we analyze Steenrod operations on the mod
\ell
motivic cohomology of smooth varieties, where
\ell
is any prime (including the characteristic of the field
k
). We review explicit constructions of
E_{\infty}
-operad actions due to Kriz–May
[
47
]
and Joshua
[
43
]
, and obtain operations using the generalized framework of May
[
57
]
. We then extend these operations to mod
\ell
motivic cohomology with support for a closed embedding
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
where
P
is smooth. These operations on local motivic cohomology crucially depend on the ambient space
P
of the embedding. We then analyze a Riemann-Roch type formula describing the failure of commutativity for these operations under proper pushforward for
\ell\neq p
by applying the axiomatic framework of Panin–Smirnov–Levine
[
63
]
,
[
67
]
,
[
64
]
,
[
70
]
,
[
54
]
. Unfortunately, we are unable to analyze how to push forward the mod
\ell=p
operations.

For alternative constructions of Steenrod operations on mod
\ell\neq p
étale cohomology, one can either utilize the Godement resolution
[
58
]
, or use étale homotopy theory as in
[
5
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
26
]
. For Steenrod operations in general homological algebra, see also
[
22
]
.

3.1

E_{\infty}
-algebras and Steenrod operations

First we review the existence of Steenrod operations on mod
\ell
motivic cohomology, where
\ell
denotes any prime,
including the case when
\ell=p
, the characteristic of the underlying field. For the rest of the paper, we will take cohomology with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n):=\mathbb{Z}(n)\otimes_{\underline{\mathbb{Z}}}\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
, where
\mathbb{Z}(n)
denotes either the motivic complex or Bloch’s cycle complex graded by codimension, and
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
denote constant sheaves. Recall the complex of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}(n)
is a complex of flat
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
-modules, since it is a complex of sheaves of free abelian groups, so tensor product and derived tensor product agree. All results discussed so far were stated for integral coefficients (except section
2.3
), and hold identically for mod
\ell
coefficients (including when
\ell=p
).

We can consider the short exact sequence of sheaves induced by the corresponding short exact sequence of groups, given by

0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{2}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n)\rightarrow 0

for every
n
, and the induced long exact sequence on cohomology. We call the connecting homomorphism
\beta:H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n))\rightarrow H^{i+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(n))
the
Bockstein
homomorphism (which exists for every
n
and every
\ell
).

Theorem 3.1
.

For a smooth variety
X
, there exist linear maps (which we call
simplicial Steenrod operations
)

Q^{s}:H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))\rightarrow H^{i+2s(\ell-1)}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j\ell))

and

\beta Q^{s}:H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))\rightarrow H^{i+2s(\ell-1)+1}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j\ell))

satisfying the following properties:

1.

(Contravariance) If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a map of smooth schemes, then
f^{*}\circ Q^{s}=Q^{s}\circ f^{*}
.

2.

(Vanishing and
\ell
-th power) If
x\in H^{i}(X,j)
then
Q^{s}(x)=0
if
2s>i
,
\beta Q^{s}(x)=0
if
2s\geq i
, and if
2s=i
then
Q^{s}(x)=x^{\ell}
.

3.

If
\beta
is the Bockstein, then
\beta Q^{s}=\beta\circ Q^{s}
.

4.

(Cartan formula)
Q^{s}(x\otimes y)=\Sigma_{i+j=s}Q^{i}(x)\otimes Q^{j}(y)
and
\beta Q^{s}(x\otimes y)=\Sigma_{i+j=s}\beta Q^{i}(x)\otimes Q^{j}(y)+Q^{i}(x)\otimes\beta Q^{j}(y)
.

5.

(Adem relations) For integers
i,j\geq 0
we denote
(i,j)=\frac{(i+j)!}{i!j!}
with the convention
0!=1
. We also let
(i,j)=0
if
i<0
or
j<0
. If
\ell>2
,
a<\ell b
, and
\epsilon=0,1
, or if
\ell=2
,
a<\ell b
and
\epsilon=0
then
\beta^{\epsilon}Q^{a}Q^{b}=\Sigma_{i}(-1)^{a+i}(a-\ell i,(\ell-1)b-a+i-1)\beta^{\epsilon}Q^{a+b-i}Q^{i}
where
\beta^{0}Q^{s}=Q^{s}
and
\beta^{1}Q^{s}=\beta Q^{s}
. We also have that if
\ell>2
,
a\leq\ell b
, and
\epsilon=0,1
, then
\beta^{\epsilon}Q^{a}\beta Q^{b}=(1-\epsilon)\Sigma_{i}(-1)^{a+i}(a-\ell i,(\ell-1)b-a+i-1)\beta Q^{a+b-i}Q^{i}-\Sigma_{i}(-1)^{a+i}(a-\ell i,(\ell-1)b-a+i)\beta^{\epsilon}Q^{a+b-i}\beta Q^{i}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Q^{0}
is not the identity map in general, and so
\beta Q^{0}
is not necessarily
\beta
. On elements
x\in H^{0}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))
for any
j
, we see that
Q^{0}(x)=x^{\ell}
.

The operations defined by Kriz and May (Example 6.3 in
[
47
]
) and those defined by Joshua
[
43
]
both satisfy Theorem
3.1
. This is because both constructions use the general framework provided by May
[
57
]
. We will explicitly review these constructions in the next two subsections. Guillou and Weibel
[
37
]
construct similar operadic operations for mod
\ell\neq p
coefficients, and assert that they agree with the two aforementioned constructed operations; see Remark
3.3
.

As mentioned, the proof of Theorem
3.1
uses the theory of
[
57
]
. The proof for any of the operadic constructions we have mentioned is identical: one shows that a given cochain theory (whether it is Bloch’s cycle complexes, or Friedlander–Suslin motivic complexes, etc.) has the structure of an
E_{\infty}
-algebra. Once this is shown, the general theory of
[
57
]
immediately constructs Steenrod operations on the mod
\ell
cohomology, for any
\ell
(see the proof of Theorem 1.1 in
[
15
]
). From this general theory, one can compute how the operations affect the bidegree
(i,j)
: the change in the first degree is by construction, and the change in the second degree is because the complexes themselves admit a simplicial degree. The construction of the operations involve taking
\ell
-fold powers of the complexes, and so elements
x
of simplicial degree
j
are sent to elements
x^{\otimes\ell}
which are of simplicial degree
j\ell
.

These operations are decidedly not the same as those constructed by Voevodsky or using stable motivic homotopy theory. Voevodsky constructs mod
\ell\neq p
operations in
[
81
]
and Primozic defines mod
\ell=p
operations for smooth varieties in
[
65
]
; we call these operations
motivic Steenrod operations
, to distinguish them from our simplicial operations. The motivic mod
\ell
operations, which we denote as
P^{s}
and
\beta P^{s}
, are such that the operation
P^{s}
sends the bidegree
(i,j)
to
(i+2s(\ell-1),j+s(\ell-1))
, whereas our operation
Q^{s}
in the theorem above sends the bidegree
(i,j)
to
(i+2s(\ell-1),j\ell)
.

Moreover, whereas our operations
\{Q^{s}\}
are built using the general theory of operads, the operations
\{P^{s}\}
are constructed analogously to the construction of Steenrod operations via taking a product of a given space with a contractible space on which the cyclic group of order
\ell
acts freely. This is done within an appropriate homotopy category of sheaves, thus leaving the category of varieties. In particular, the operations of Theorem
3.1
do not commute with Voevodsky’s notion of suspension, again due to the change in grading. In fact, Voevodsky shows in
[
81
]
that his operations are generated by a suitable motivic Steenrod algebra at least in characteristic 0; the work of
[
40
]
extends this to characteristic
p
using alterations. As mentioned in the introduction, we elected not to utilize the operations
P^{s}
of motivic homotopy theory, as they preserve the ordinary Chow groups; thus, the odd degree operations, such as
\beta P^{s}
, vanish on the fundamental class of any variety for trivial degree reasons (see Proposition
3.8
and Theorem 8.4 in
[
81
]
).

Recall that Bloch’s cycle complexes are only contravariant functorial for flat maps, so technically Kriz and May in
[
47
]
only prove contravariance of these operations for flat maps between smooth varieties. We add a small lemma to extend contravariance to arbitrary maps between smooth varieties.

Lemma 3.1
.

The simplicial Steenrod operations constructed via Bloch’s cycle complexes (see Section
3.1.1
) are contravariant for maps between smooth varieties.

Proof.

For a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
where
X,Y
are smooth affine varieties, we can again consider the subcomplex
z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\subset z^{*}(Y,*)
of codimension
n
cycles that can be pulled-back, as in Definition
2.5
. By Theorem
A.1
, we can transfer the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
z^{*}(Y,*)
to the subcomplex
z^{*}(Y,*)_{f}
, as the inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism. The proof of Proposition
2.3
shows that the pull-back map
f^{*}:z^{n}(Y,*)_{f}\rightarrow z^{n}(X,*)
is a map of partial algebras (see section
3.1.1
), and therefore a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras. Sheafifying the cycle complexes, we obtain maps of sheaves of
E_{\infty}
-algebras, and by Zariski descent we get that the pullback map
f^{*}
on higher Chow groups between any two smooth varieties
X
and
Y
commutes with the operations
Q^{\bullet}
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

We will require Lemma
3.1
for the deformation to the normal cone later; however, for the central argument about certain degree operations vanishing for smooth varieties realized as zero sections of bundles, all the maps involved will be between smooth varieties with equidimensional fibers. Thus, by miracle flatness (Theorem 23.1 of
[
56
]
) the maps involved are all flat.

We will see later in section
3.1.1
a sketch of the construction of Kriz–May–Bloch that Bloch’s cycle complexes are an
E_{\infty}
-algebra. To extend our simplicial operations to cohomology with support, we require the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras with unit. The kernel of
f
is a
E_{\infty}
-algebra. Moreover, the inclusion
i:F\hookrightarrow A
commutes with the operad actions
\mathcal{E}(n)
for all
n>0
.

Proof.

In general, the kernel of a map of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, as follows: let
F
denote the kernel of the map
f:A\rightarrow B
, so that there is a natural left exact sequence

0\rightarrow F\xrightarrow{i}A\rightarrow B.

We wish to define maps
\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes F^{\otimes n}\rightarrow F
with the usual associativity and equivariance conditions. Notice that the composition
\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes F^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{id\otimes i^{\otimes n}}\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{\theta}A\xrightarrow{f}B
actually equals the composition
\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes F^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{id\otimes i^{\otimes n}}\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{id\otimes f^{\otimes n}}\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes B^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{\theta}B
since
f
is a map of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras. But the latter composition equals
0
, since
\theta\circ(id\otimes f^{\otimes n})\circ(id\otimes i^{\otimes n})=\theta\circ(id\otimes(f\circ i)^{\otimes n}))=0
. Thus, the map
\theta\circ(id\otimes i^{\otimes n})
factors through the kernel
F
. By construction, the inclusion map
i:F\hookrightarrow A
commutes with operad actions
\mathcal{O}(n)
for all
n
except
n=0
. The verification of the algebra axioms follows from a diagram chase.
∎

Remark.
We note that if
A
and
B
are
\mathcal{O}
-algebras with unit, then the kernel of
f
is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra that cannot be given a unit in a compatible way. That is, the inclusion
i:F\hookrightarrow A
does not commute with the
\mathcal{O}(0)=k
action. This parallels how the kernel of a map of commutative rings (with unit) itself does not have a unit element. However, the kernel still has a multiplicative structure.

Remark.
In fact, a more general statement is true: the homotopy fiber of a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras is naturally an
E_{\infty}
-algebra. The argument is largely the same; one constructs the map on the fiber using the universal property of the homotopy fiber. However, there is a higher categorical nuance: although the homotopy fiber itself is functorial and independent of choices, its universal property depends on a choice of nullhomotopy. By passing to higher categories, we could encode this nullhomotopy as part of the data to make the construction completely functorial, but that will take us too far afield.

In other words, granting for now that Bloch’s cycle complexes are
E_{\infty}
-algebras for smooth varieties, we have that the cycle complexes with support inherit an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure from the localization sequence.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed immersion where
P
is a smooth variety. Then the complex
\oplus_{i,j}z^{i}_{X}(P,j)
is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra.

We will also see later that the motivic complexes of Friedlander–Suslin are an
E_{\infty}
-algebra in section
3.1.2
. However, to deal with motivic complexes with support, we must consider the object in the derived category of sheaves
\oplus_{n}i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)
. To avoid the nuances of the homotopy fiber, we can use that the motivic complexes with support is quasi-isomorphic to the sheaf of relative cycles
\oplus_{n}z^{n}_{X}(-,*)[-2n]
. This latter sheaf is again the kernel of
j^{*}:\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\rightarrow Z_{U}(n)
, where
j:U:=P-X\hookrightarrow P
is the open complement of
X
. We can then use the quasi-isomorphisms between Friedlander–Suslin motivic complexes and Bloch’s cycle complexes to transfer the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the motivic complexes over to the cycle complexes. This then gives an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the cycle complexes with support by Lemma
3.2
, which then passes over again to the motivic complexes with support via the quasi-isomorphism. The fact that quasi-isomorphisms can transfer
E_{\infty}
-algebras is nontrivial (see Theorem
A.1
); however, the point of this discussion is to show that, regardless of how one represents motivic cohomology, one can obtain Steenrod operations on cohomology with support. Whether the transferred
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure is actually equivalent with the one on the homotopy fiber or not, is a matter we leave to the reader (see Problem
3.1
).

We obtain the following corollary of Lemma
3.2
and Corollary
3.1
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. There exist operations

Q^{s}:H^{i}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))\rightarrow H^{i+2s}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j\ell))

and

\beta Q^{s}:H^{i}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))\rightarrow H^{i+2s(\ell-1)+1}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j\ell))

satisfying the same properties as in Theorem
3.1
, where property (1) is modified as follows: given closed immersions
X^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
and
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
where
P^{\prime}
and
P
are smooth, and a flat map
P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}-X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P-X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is commutative, the operations
Q^{s}
and
\beta Q^{s}
commute with the induced pullback map on cohomology of a pair
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))\rightarrow H^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
. Moreover, the operations commute with the natural map
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(\bullet))\rightarrow H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(\bullet))
in the long exact sequence of a pair.

Proof.

The existence of these operations now follow from May
[
57
]
(see Theorem I.7.2 in
[
47
]
). The operations in
[
57
]
are defined for any pair
(K,\theta)
where
K
is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra and
\theta
is the induced map on homology of the
\ell
-th operad action on
K
. In particular, we take our pair to be
(K,\theta)
where
K=\oplus_{i,j}z^{i}_{X}(P,j)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell
and
\theta
to be the map
H_{*}(\mathcal{E}(\ell)\otimes K^{\otimes\ell},\mathbb{Z}/\ell)\rightarrow H_{*}(K,\mathbb{Z}/\ell)
, induced from the operad action
\mathcal{E}(\ell)\otimes K^{\otimes\ell}\rightarrow K
. This defines the simplicial Steenrod operations on cohomology with support. Moreover, since the inclusion of the kernel
K\hookrightarrow\oplus_{i,j}z^{i}(P,j)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/\ell
commutes with operad actions above degree
0
(as in Lemma
3.2
), we see that the operations on
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(\bullet))
commute with the map
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(\bullet))\rightarrow H^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(\bullet))
in the long exact sequence.

Given a flat map
f:P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
, we have that the induced pull-back map
f^{*}:z^{*}(P,\bullet)\rightarrow z^{*}(P^{\prime},\bullet)
on cycles is a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras (see section
3.1.1
). By the given commutative diagram, we have an induced map on kernels
f^{*}:z^{*}_{X}(P,\bullet)\rightarrow z^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},\bullet)
, which is the restriction of
f^{*}
to the kernel. For shorthand notation, let
K=z^{*}_{X}(P,\bullet)
and
K^{\prime}=z^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},\bullet)
, so we have a map
f^{*}:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
. Let
A=z^{*}(P,\bullet)
and
A^{\prime}=z^{*}(P^{\prime},\bullet)
, with
i:K\hookrightarrow A
and
i^{\prime}:K^{\prime}\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}
the respective inclusions. The claim is that the induced map on kernels is compatible with the operad action, which follows from commutativity of the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(n)\otimes K^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(n)\otimes A^{\prime\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(n)\otimes K^{\prime\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

All inner squares commute, by construction of the operad action in Lemma
3.2
and by the diagram in the hypothesis, and so the outer square commutes. Again, for the operations to commute, all one needs is commutativity with respect to the
\mathcal{E}(\ell)
action.

∎

3.1.1

Operations on higher Chow groups

We will study the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on Bloch’s cycle complex, as analyzed by Kriz and May (I.7 and II.6 in
[
47
]
). Kriz and May observe that the Bloch cycle complexes form a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra, due to the following argument of Bloch, which was explained to Kriz and May in private communication. With the permission of everyone involved, the author was able to obtain a copy of this letter, and for the sake of transparency, we sketch Bloch’s argument below in this section. We thank Igor Kriz for finding the letter. We also thank Spencer Bloch for explaining portions of this letter and for the beautiful figures he draws within, which we copy here. Again, we emphasize this section has zero original content, and is all copied from the private letter from Bloch to Kriz and May. Assume throughout this section that
X
is a smooth variety.

Let
Z(X)
denote the direct product
\oplus_{r,q}z^{r}(X,q)
. The aim of this letter is to prove that the homology of
Z(X)
has a graded algebra structure, by an “easy” moving lemma; the “hard” moving lemma of
[
10
]
shows that the homology of
Z(X)
has a differential graded algebra structure. More precisely, we will define a domain
Z(X)_{*}
for
Z(X)
in the sense of May and Kriz (see Definition
A.5
); the claim is that
Z(X)
equipped with this domain has a partial commutative algebra structure. May and Kriz then consider
Z(X)
as a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra via pull-back with respect to a suitable map of operads
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow Comm
. They then apply their conversion theorem (Theorem
A.2
) that functorially associates to a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra a quasi-isomorphic
E_{\infty}
-algebra. In particular, they have the same mod
\ell
Steenrod operations (see Remark
A.1
).

First we shall consider the simplest case when
X=\textnormal{Spec }k
. The idea is to pass to an infinite dimensional simplex so as to freely move cycles around to meet all possible faces properly. Let
K\supset k
be the purely transcendental extension of
k
generated by independent variables
p_{ij}
for
1\leq i<\infty
and
1\leq j\leq i
. We also let
p_{i0}=1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq i}p_{ij}
so we have
\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}p_{ij}=1
for a fixed
i
. We write
Y=\textnormal{Spec }K
and write
\iota:z^{r}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*)
for the pull-back map on cycles.

Consider the subcomplex
Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}
generated by products of closed irreducible subvarieties
V_{1}\otimes...\otimes V_{j}\in z^{r_{1}}(X,q_{1})\otimes...\otimes z^{r_{j}}(X,q_{j})
such that the product over any subset of indices
V_{i_{1}}\times...\times V_{i_{k}}
for
k\leq j
meets the faces of
\Delta^{i_{1}}\times...\times\Delta^{i_{k}}
properly, where a face for the product of simplices is defined as in Definition
2.7
. We must show that
Z(X)_{j}=\oplus_{r}Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset Z(X)^{\otimes j}
is a domain in the sense of May and Kriz; that is, we must show the inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proposition 3.1
.

(Bloch)
The inclusion
Z(X)_{j}\subset Z(X)^{\otimes j}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

The aim is to then define a map
\psi:z^{r}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*)
with a homotopy
H:z^{r}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*+1)
such that
dH-Hd=\iota-\psi
, so we can “homotope” products not meeting faces properly to those that do. One wants to construct maps (respectively homotopies)
z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*)^{\otimes j}
(respectively
z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*+1)^{\otimes j}
) defined by taking
\iota
in some factors and
\psi
in others (respectively
H
on one factor,
\iota
on some factors, and
\psi
on others). In the end, the maps
\iota^{\otimes j}
,
\psi^{\otimes j}
and the homotopy

\mathcal{H}_{j}=\sum^{n}_{i=0}\psi^{\otimes i}\otimes H\otimes\iota^{\otimes j-i-1}

send
Z^{r}(X)_{j}
to
Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
; the map
\psi
shall actually be constructed so that
\psi^{\otimes j}(z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j})\subset Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
. Moreover, we will have an induced homotopy
d\mathcal{H}_{j}+\mathcal{H}_{j}d=\iota^{\otimes j}-\psi^{\otimes j}
. This in turn implies that the induced map
\overline{\iota^{\otimes j}}:z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}/Z^{r}(X)_{j}\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*)^{\otimes j}/Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
is nullhomotopic.

However, one then shows that the induced map
\overline{\iota^{\otimes j}}
is injective on homology; since it is nullhomotopic, this will give that the inclusion of complexes
Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}
is a quasi-isomorphism. To show it is injective on homology, one writes the field
K
as a limit
K=\varinjlim R_{\alpha}
where
R_{\alpha}
is a smooth
k
-subalgebra of finite type, with
k\subset R_{\alpha}\subset K
. Since
K
is purely transcendental over
k
, there exist sections
\sigma:\textnormal{Spec }k\rightarrow\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha}
to the natural structure maps. Again, one considers subcomplexes
Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}\subset Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
and
z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)^{\otimes j}\subset z^{r}(Y,*)^{\otimes j}
.

By the arguments as in Theorem
2.1
and Proposition
2.3
, there exist subcomplexes
\overline{Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}}
and
\overline{z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)}^{\otimes j}
with

Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset\overline{Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}}\subset Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}

and

z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}\subset\overline{z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)}^{\otimes j}\subset z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)^{\otimes j}

so that the inclusions
\overline{Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}}\subset Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}
and
\overline{z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)}^{\otimes j}\subset z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)^{\otimes j}
are quasi-isomorphisms, and so that cycles meet the sections
\sigma
properly. Thus, there are induced pull-back maps
\sigma^{*}:\overline{Z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha})_{j}}\rightarrow Z^{r}(X,*)_{j}
and
\sigma^{*}:\overline{z^{r}(\textnormal{Spec }R_{\alpha},*)}^{\otimes j}\rightarrow z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}
. Thus the map
\overline{\iota^{\otimes j}}
is a direct limit of injective split maps and so is injective on homology.

The maps
H
and
\psi
are constructed recursively. We will construct
H
as a formal combination of simplicial maps
\Delta_{Y}^{n+1}\rightarrow\Delta_{Y}^{n}
, where
\Delta_{Y}^{n}
denotes the
n
-simplex over the field
K
. We then postcompose with the basechange map
\Delta_{Y}^{n}\rightarrow\Delta_{X}^{n}
to get a map
\Delta^{n+1}_{Y}\rightarrow\Delta^{n}_{X}
. By pulling back cycles along these maps, we obtain a map
z^{r}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*+1)
.

Figure 1.
[formula]
is a homotopy between
[formula]
and
[formula]
.
Non-vertex faces are hyperplanes.

Let
\Delta^{\infty}=\{\sum a_{n}e(n)\ |\ \textnormal{almost all }a_{n}=0,\sum a_{n}=1\}
be the infinite dimensional simplex over
K
. For
S=\{s_{0},...,s_{m}\}\subset\{0,...,n\}
, we write
e(S):=e(s_{0},...,s_{m}):=\sum_{j\leq m}p_{mj}e(s_{j})
where
e(s_{j})
is a formal variable (that will represent a vertex of a simplex) and where
p_{mj}\in K
are the transcendental basis elements. Note by definition that
e(0,...,n)
is in the
K
-linear span of
e(0),...,e(n)
.

For points
\alpha(0),...,\alpha(n)\in\Delta^{\infty}
, we write
\langle\alpha(0),...,\alpha(n)\rangle
for the map of the standard simplex
\langle 0,...,n\rangle
into
\Delta^{\infty}
. For example, the notation
\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle
denotes the map of the standard simplex sending vertices to the points
e(0),...,e(n)
in
\Delta^{\infty}
. Since
e(0,...,n)
is in the
K
-linear span of
e(0),...,e(n)
, we note that
e(0,...,n)
sits in the face
\Delta^{n}
with vertices
e(0),...,e(n)
.

If
\phi:\langle 0,...,n\rangle\rightarrow\Delta^{\infty}
is an affine map, and
\alpha\in\Delta^{\infty}
, we write
\phi\llcorner\alpha
to be the map
\langle 0,...,n+1\rangle
which agrees with
\phi
on
\langle 0,...,n\rangle
and sends
n+1
to
\alpha
. Thus,
\langle\alpha(0),...,\alpha(n)\rangle\llcorner\alpha=\langle\alpha(0),...,\alpha(n),\alpha\rangle.
For such an affine map, or a
n
-simplex
\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle
, we define their differential
d
to be the formal linear combinations of
(n-1)
-simplices given by their boundary faces (i.e.,
d
is the boundary operator). Then we obtain that
d(\phi\llcorner\alpha)=(d\phi)\llcorner\alpha+(-1)^{n+1}\phi
. By definition, extending the operator
d
to the cycle complexes by taking intersections of cycles with the corresponding boundary faces gives the usual boundary operator already present on the cycle complexes.

Let
H\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle
denote a formal linear combination of simplicial maps from the standard
(n+1)
-simplex
\langle 0,...,n+1\rangle\rightarrow\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle
. The homotopy
H
is defined on a cycle by pulling back along these maps, with the recursive formula for
n\geq 1
given by

H\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle=(\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+(-1)^{n-1}Hd\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle)\llcorner e(0,...,n)

with base case
H\langle e(0)\rangle=0
. We also note that
H
only depends on the dimension of the simplex rather than any labelling of its vertices; that is,
H\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle=H\langle e(1),...,e(n+1)\rangle
under the order-preserving identification
\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle\cong\langle e(1),...,e(n+1)\rangle
, since the coefficient
p_{mj}
only depends on
j
and not
s_{j}
. For example,
H\langle e(0),e(1)\rangle=(\langle e(0),e(1)\rangle+H(e(1)-e(0))\llcorner e(0,1)=\langle e(0),e(1)\rangle\llcorner e(0,1)=\langle e(0),e(1),e(0,1)\rangle
.

Proposition 3.2
.

We have the formula

(dH-Hd)\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle=(-1)^{n+1}\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+\sum\pm\langle e(S_{0}),...,e(S_{n})\rangle

where the sets
S_{i}\subset\{0,...,n\}
run through all subsets

\varnothing\subsetneq S_{0}\subsetneq...\subsetneq S_{n}=\{0,...,n\}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
. For
n=0
, we have
H\langle e(0)\rangle=0
, and the sum on the right hand side is
\langle e(0)\rangle-\langle e(S_{0})\rangle
where
e(S_{0})=e(0)
. For
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle(dH-Hd)\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle=(d\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+(-1)^{n-1}dHd\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle)\llcorner e(0,...,n)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n+1}(\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+(-1)^{n-1}Hd\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle)

\displaystyle-Hd\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\langle e(0),...,e(n)+(d\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+(-1)^{n-1}(dH-Hd)d\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle)\llcorner e(0,...,n)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+(-1)^{n-1}\sum\sum\pm\langle e(S_{i,0}),...,e(S_{i,n-1})\langle\llcorner e(0,...,n)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\langle e(0),...,e(n)\rangle+\sum\pm\langle e(S_{0}),...,e(S_{n})\rangle

where the double sum runs over
0\leq i\leq n
and
\varphi\subsetneq S_{i,0}\subsetneq...\subsetneq S_{i,n-1}=\{0,...,\hat{i},...,n\}
.
∎

Thus, by construction,
H
is a formal linear combination of simplicial maps
\Delta^{n+1}\rightarrow\Delta^{n}
defined on vertices and faces, then extending linearly. Pulling back cycles gives a map
H:z^{r}(X,*)\rightarrow z^{r}(Y,*+1)
.

Once
H
is constructed, one simply defines
\psi
to be
\iota-dH+Hd
, which coincides with pull-back of cycles over the sum
\sum\pm\langle e(S_{0}),...,e(S_{n})\rangle
in the Proposition above. One also checks that
H
sends
Z^{r}(X)_{j}
into
Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
, which follows from the coefficients
p_{mj}
being chosen generically.

Proposition 3.3
.

The map
\psi^{\otimes j}
sends
z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}
into
Z^{r}(Y)_{j}
.

Proof.

Let
Z_{1}\otimes...\otimes Z_{j}
be a tensor product of cycles, where each cycle
Z_{k}
meets the faces of
\Delta^{m_{k}}
properly. Let
e_{k}(0),...,e_{k}(m_{k})
be the vertices of
\Delta^{m_{k}}
. Let
G_{k}
be the algebraic group of affine linear transformations of
\Delta^{m_{k}}
stabilizing the flag of all simplicial faces
\langle e_{k}(0)\rangle\subset\langle e_{k}(0),e_{k}(1)\rangle\subset...\subset\langle e_{k}(0),...,e_{k}(m_{k})\rangle.
As
\psi
and
H
were constructed as pull-backs of cycles over simplicial maps, we have that the map
\psi
is a pullback map
g_{k}^{*}
for some affine linear transformation
g_{k}\in G_{k}
in each component. Thus we have that

\psi^{\otimes j}(Z_{1}\otimes...\otimes Z_{j})=g^{*}(Z_{1}\otimes...\otimes Z_{j})

where
g=(g_{1},...,g_{j})\in G=G_{1}\times...\times G_{j}
is the product of affine linear transformations keeping faces fixed determined by Proposition
3.2
. We must then show that the product
g^{*}(Z)=g^{*}(Z_{1}\times...\times Z_{j})
meets all diagonal faces
D
of
\Delta^{m_{1}}\times...\times\Delta^{m_{j}}
properly.

If we restrict
g
to

\Delta^{m_{1}}\times...\times\Delta^{m_{i-1}}\times\langle e_{i}(0),e_{i}(1),...,e_{i}(m_{i}-1)\rangle\times...\times\Delta^{m_{j}}

(9)

then
g
remains an affine linear transformation that stabilizes all faces. By induction on dimension, we can assume no bad component of
D\cap g^{*}(Z)
is contained in a face of the form Equation
9
. Thus, we may replace
\prod\Delta^{m_{i}}
with the open variety
X=\prod(\Delta^{m_{i}}-\langle e_{i}(0),...,e_{i}(m_{i}-1)\rangle)
and
Z
with
Z\cap X
. We also have that
G
acts transitively on
X
. The proposition now follows from Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
G
be an algebraic group acting transitively on a variety
X
. Let
Z
and
D
be closed subvarieties of
X
. Then there exists a non-empty open
U\subset G
such that for
g\in U
, the intersection
g^{*}(Z)\cap D
is proper.

Proof.

Let
\pi
denote the composition

G\times Z\rightarrow G\times X\rightarrow X

where the map on the right is the action of
G
. The map
\pi
is equivariant. Since
G
acts transitively on
X
, it follows that the fibers of
\pi
are all isomorphic, of dimension equal to
\textnormal{dim}(G)+\textnormal{dim}(Z)-\textnormal{dim}(X)
. This gives that
\textnormal{dim}(\pi^{-1}(D))=\textnormal{dim}(D)+\textnormal{dim}(G)+\textnormal{dim}(Z)-\textnormal{dim}(X)
. Now we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The fiber of the composed map
f:\pi^{-1}(D)\rightarrow G
over
g
is
g^{*}(Z)\cap D
. Moreover, the fiber
f^{-1}(g)
for general
g
will either be empty, or have dimension equal to
\textnormal{dim}(\pi^{-1}(D))-\textnormal{dim}(G)=\textnormal{dim}(D)+\textnormal{dim}(Z)-\textnormal{dim}(X)
. That is, the intersection will be generically proper.
∎

We now generalize to the case when
X
is an arbitrary smooth variety.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth variety and let
Z(X)
denote the direct product
\oplus_{r,q}z^{r}(X,q)
. Then
Z(X)
is a partial commutative algebra.

Proof.

We again define the domain
Z(X)_{*}
to be given by the subcomplexes
Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset z^{r}(X,*)^{\otimes j}
generated by products of closed irreducible subvarieties
V_{1}\otimes...\otimes V_{j}\in z^{r_{1}}(X,q_{1})\otimes...\otimes z^{r_{j}}(X,q_{j})
where the product over any subset of indices meets the faces of the product simplex properly. We will show that the inclusion of the subcomplex
Z(X)_{j}=\oplus_{r}Z^{r}(X)_{j}\subset Z(X)^{\otimes j}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

We write
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
to be the sheaf associated to the presheaf
U\mapsto Z(U)_{j}
for
U
Zariski open in
X
. Similarly, we write
\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes j}
to be the sheaf associated to the product presheaf
U\mapsto Z(U)^{\otimes j}
. We obtain an inclusion of complexes of sheaves
\mathcal{Z}_{j}\subset Z^{\otimes j}
. Moreover, since the cycle complexes satisfy localization and therefore Zariski descent (Lemma
2.1
), the complexes
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes j}
also satisfy Zariski descent. Thus their hypercohomology is computed by the cohomology of their global sections. If we show that the complexes of sheaves are quasi-isomorphic, then they have the same hypercohomology, and by Zariski descent, the cohomology of their global sections are isomorphic, which is the statement we wish to prove.

To check that a map of complexes of sheaves is a quasi-isomorphism, it suffices to check on the level of stalks, since taking stalks is exact. The stalk of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
is exactly
Z(\textnormal{Spec }k)_{j}
, and the stalk of
\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes j}
is exactly
Z(\textnormal{Spec }k)^{\otimes j}
. The proof then reduces to the case when
X=\textnormal{Spec }k
, which the rest of the letter has done.
∎

Figure 2. The original big triangle
\langle e(0),e(1),e(2)\rangle
is cut into six pieces
\langle e(S_{0}),e(S_{1}),e(S_{2})\rangle
. The map
\psi
pulls back cycles via the simplicial maps defined by the subdivision. Both figures 1 and 2 due to Spencer Bloch.

3.1.2

Operations on motivic cohomology

This section analyzes the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure constructed by Joshua
[
43
]
directly on the motivic complexes
\mathbb{Z}(q):=C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})[-2q]
. We again emphasize this section has no original content and is all due to Joshua. Joshua first constructs explicit higher order homotopies for the different permutations of product multiplication on the motivic complexes, and then uses these homotopies to define an explicit operad action. The higher order homotopies are constructed first for smooth affine varieties, and then extended to all smooth varieties via Zariski descent.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{k}
denote the symmetric group on
k
elements. We denote by
E\Sigma_{k}
the simplicial group which in degree
n
is given by the
(n+1)
-fold direct product
\Sigma_{k}^{n+1}
. We let
Z
denote the free abelian group functor. Then
ZE\Sigma_{k}
is a simplicial abelian group, and we can associate to it the normalized chain complex
NZE\Sigma_{k}
. We call this sequence of chain complexes
NZE\Sigma=\{NZE\Sigma_{k}\}
the
simplicial Barratt–Eccles operad
. For shorthand notation in this section, we will denote the operad terms by
\mathcal{E}(k):=NZE\Sigma_{k}
.

Recall that an element in degree
n
(an
n
-simplex) of
E\Sigma_{k}
is given by an
(n+1)
-tuple
(\sigma_{0},...\sigma_{n})
where
\sigma_{i}
are elements of
\Sigma_{k}
. For shorthand, let
A=\oplus_{q}\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q)
where
\ell
is again any prime, including possibly the characteristic of the field. The construction in
[
43
]
gives an operad action

\mathcal{E}(k)\otimes A^{\otimes k}\rightarrow A

which we will outline below. The action is actually constructed on
\oplus_{q}\mathbb{Z}(q)
and we obtain our action by taking mod
\ell
.

Assume
U
is a smooth affine scheme. We will first do an easy example with two factors; that is, we will construct a pairing

\mathcal{E}(2)\otimes\mathbb{Z}(m)\otimes\mathbb{Z}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}(m+n).

Recall that
\mathbb{Z}(q)(U):=C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})(U)[-2q]=\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})[-2q]
where we have
\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q})(U\times\Delta^{\bullet})=Cor(U\times\Delta^{\bullet},\mathbb{A}^{q})
. Let
V\in\mathbb{Z}(m)(U)
and
W\in\mathbb{Z}(n)(U)
be closed subvarieties; i.e., we assume
V
is a closed subscheme of
U\times\Delta^{i}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
and
W
is a closed subscheme of
U\times\Delta^{j}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
, both finite and surjective over
U\times\Delta^{i}
and
U\times\Delta^{j}
respectively. The idea behind Joshua’s construction of the pairing will be to build explicit homotopies between
V\cap W
and
W\cap V
, where the latter intersection product is interpreted as
\Sigma_{2}
permuting the affine space factors
\mathbb{A}^{m}
and
\mathbb{A}^{n}
alone.

Recall the product structure from section
2.5
: we form the direct product
V\times W
, which is a closed subscheme of
U\times U\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
and we pull-back by the product of the diagonal with the identity
\Delta\times id:U\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n}\rightarrow U\times U\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
to obtain a closed subvariety
(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)
in
Cor(U\times\Delta^{i}\times\Delta^{j},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})
. Then we shuffle using the map of equation
8
; let us call the resulting cycle
s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)
.

A closed point
a
on
s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)
is given in coordinates as a point in
U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
by
a=(a_{1},a_{2})
with
a_{1}\in U\times\Delta^{i+j}
and
a_{2}\in\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
. Let
J_{a}
denote the maximal ideal corresponding to
a
in
\Gamma(U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n},\mathcal{O}_{U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times A^{m+n}})
. Let
J_{s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)}
denote the ideal corresponding to the union of subvarieties underlying the cycle
s(\Delta\times Id)^{*}(V\times W)
. Then we have that

J_{s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)}=\bigcap_{a\in s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)}J_{a}.

Moreover, since we are assuming
U
is affine, we have that the ideals
J_{a}
are defined by polynomials
\{p_{a}\}
which are polynomials in
m+n
variables
(x_{1}-(a_{2})_{1},...,x_{m+n}-(a_{2})_{m+n})
depending on
a_{2}
with coefficients in the ring
\Gamma(U\times\Delta^{i+j},\mathcal{O}_{U\times\Delta^{i+j}})
depending on
a_{1}
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{2}
denote the transposition that switches the two factors of
\mathbb{A}^{m+n}=\mathbb{A}^{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
. By abuse of notation, we also denote the corresponding automorphism
\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
by
\sigma
. Let
s_{1}=(id,\sigma)
denote the corresponding
1
-simplex in
E\Sigma_{2}
. Given a maximal ideal
J_{a}
of
\Gamma(U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n},\mathcal{O}_{U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n}})
generated by polynomials
\{p_{a}\}
, we can define a new ideal
J_{s_{1}(a)}
in
\Gamma(U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\Delta^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n},\mathcal{O}_{U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\Delta^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{m+n}})
where we replace the variable
a_{2}\in\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
by the variable
ta_{2}+(1-t)\sigma(a_{2})
in all the defining polynomials
p_{a}
. This defines polynomials
p_{s_{1}(a)}
which we take to generate
J_{s_{1}(a)}
.

We define a pairing

Z(s_{1})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{i},\mathbb{A}^{m})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{j},\mathbb{A}^{n})\rightarrow Cor(U\times\Delta^{i+j+1},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})

where
Z(s_{1})
denotes the subcomplex of
NZE\Sigma_{2}
generated by the
1
-simplex
s_{1}
. The pairing is defined by mapping
s_{1}\otimes V\otimes W
to
s_{1}(V\times W)
where the cycle
s_{1}(V\times W)
is defined by the ideal

J_{s_{1}(V\times W)}:=\bigcap_{a\in s(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)}J_{s_{1}(a)}

while keeping coefficients fixed, which gives us an element of
Cor(U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\Delta^{1},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})
. Using the shuffle maps of section
2.5
, we obtain a map
Cor(U\times\Delta^{i+j}\times\Delta^{1},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})\rightarrow Cor(U\times\Delta^{i+j+1},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})
. The intuition is that the variety
s_{1}(V\times W)
is a “homotopy” between
(\Delta\times id)^{*}(V\times W)
and
(\Delta\times id)^{*}(W\times V)
. Notice that by restricting to the vertices
t=0
and
t=1
in the
\Delta^{1}
factor before shuffling, we obtain maps

Z(s_{1})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{i},\mathbb{A}^{m})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{j},\mathbb{A}^{n})\rightarrow Cor(U\times\Delta^{i+j},\mathbb{A}^{m+n})

by sending
\sigma\otimes V\otimes W
to the cycle
s_{1}(V\times W)|_{t=0}
consisting of subvarieties defined by equations
p_{s_{1}(a)}
where the variable
a_{2}\in\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
is replaced with the variable
\sigma(a_{2})\in\mathbb{A}^{m+n}
, and by sending
\sigma\otimes V\otimes W
to
s_{1}(V\times W)|_{t=1}
, determined by the other vertex of the
1
-simplex
s_{1}
. We can then take the formal sum
s_{1}(V\times W)|_{t=0}+s_{1}(V\times W)|_{t=1}
to obtain one map for our operad action. Finally, notice that the symmetric group action only permutes the affine space factors.

In general, one obtains pairings

Z(s_{q})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{p_{1}},\mathbb{A}^{r_{1}})\otimes...\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{p_{k}},\mathbb{A}^{r_{k}})\rightarrow Cor(U\times\Delta^{q+\sum p_{j}},\mathbb{A}^{\sum r_{j}})

for “higher order homotopies,” which again can be restricted to all faces of
\Delta^{q}
compatibly, prior to shuffling, and where
s_{q}
is a given tuple
(\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{q})
for
\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma_{k}
. Explicitly, given closed subschemes
V_{j}
in
Cor(U\times\Delta^{p_{j}},\mathbb{A}^{l_{j}})
for
j=1,...,k
, we can form their product
V_{1}\times...\times V_{k}
and take the pull-back by
\Delta\times id
, where
\Delta:U\rightarrow U^{k}
is the
k
-fold diagonal. One again obtains closed subvarieties cut out by equations
p_{a}
in
l=l_{1}+...+l_{k}
variables
(x_{1}-(a_{2})_{1},...,x_{l}-(a_{2})_{l})
again depending on
a_{2}\in\mathbb{A}^{l}
for a given point
a=(a_{1},a_{2})
in
U\times\Delta^{\sum p_{j}}\times\mathbb{A}^{l}
. For a given
q
-simplex
s_{q}=(\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{q})
, substitute the variables
a_{2}
with
t_{0}\sigma_{0}(a_{2})+t_{1}\sigma_{1}(a_{2})+...+t_{q-1}\sigma_{q-1}(a_{2})+(1-t_{0}-...-t_{q-1})\sigma_{q}(a_{2})
. Again taking restrictions to different vertices via
t_{j}=1
and setting others equal to
0
, we obtain
q+1
maps and take the formal sum of the subvarieties
s_{q}(V\times W)|_{t_{0}=...=t_{q-1}=0}+\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}s_{q}(V\times W)|_{t_{j}=1}
to obtain a map

Z(s_{q})\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{p_{1}},\mathbb{A}^{r_{1}})\otimes...\otimes Cor(U\times\Delta^{p_{k}},\mathbb{A}^{r_{k}})\rightarrow Cor(U\times\Delta^{\sum p_{j}},\mathbb{A}^{\sum r_{j}})

for each
q
-simplex
s_{q}
. The maps constructed are compatible with the simplicial maps induced by the cosimplicial scheme
\Delta^{\bullet}
, so rewriting the above gives us maps

Z(s_{q})\otimes\mathbb{Z}(r_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathbb{Z}(r_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}(r)

for each fixed
q
-simplex
s_{q}
in
\Sigma_{k}
, and where
r=r_{1}+...+r_{k}
, and where the sheaves
\mathbb{Z}(r_{j})
are restricted to smooth affine schemes. One then extends linearly for all simplices of
E\Sigma_{k}
and proceeds by an inductive argument on skeleta of
E\Sigma_{k}
(see the rest of section 5 in
[
43
]
) to define the Barratt–Eccles operad action
\mathcal{E}(k)\otimes A^{\otimes k}\rightarrow A
, again restricted to smooth affine schemes. One then passes to all finite type smooth varieties by Zariski descent. The arguments that the maps constructed above satisfy the operad axioms are presented in the rest of section 5 of
[
43
]
.

We present a claim that we will not use in this paper, nor will we attempt to prove. We leave it to the interested algebraic topologist to solve.

Problem 3.1
.

The comparison map of Theorem
2.5
is a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras, with respect to the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures discussed above.

By Proposition 12.1 in
[
27
]
, the quasi-isomorphism of Theorem
2.5
is given by the zig-zag of inclusions

C^{*}\mathbb{Z}_{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{q},-)\hookrightarrow z^{q}(-\times\mathbb{A}^{q},*)\hookleftarrow z^{q}(-,*)

of complexes of sheaves. The content of Proposition 12.1 in
[
27
]
is that the first inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism, while we know that the second inclusion is a quasi-isomorphism by homotopy invariance. Moreover, the second inclusion is induced by a flat map (the projection from the product with
\mathbb{A}^{q}
) and so is already a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras. On the other hand, one could hope to construct an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
z^{q}(-\times\mathbb{A}^{q},*)
in a similar manner to Joshua’s: by building explicit actions of the Barratt–Eccles operad that permute only the factors on the affine space coordinates. This would then give that the first inclusion is a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras.

However, now there are a priori two
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures on
z^{q}(-\times\mathbb{A}^{q},*)
: one coming from the explicit Barratt–Eccles operad action that permutes the affine space factors, and the other coming from the partial algebra structure. The claim is that these two
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures are equivalent. We do not attempt to prove it here, as we do not need it, nor is it in our interest to do so; it is better for us to have varied sources of potentially non-trivial Steenrod operations.

Remark 3.3
.

We finally conclude our discussion on the different sources of Steenrod operations on motivic cohomology with a remark about the cycle class map. Guillou and Weibel give an alternative construction for Steenrod operations on mod
\ell
motivic cohomology (for
\ell\neq p
) in
[
37
]
: they use the natural
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the global sections of the Godement resolution of
\mu_{\ell}
, and transfer the structure over to mod
\ell
motivic cochains using the norm residue isomorphism theorem. They then assert in Remark 6.5.1 of
[
37
]
that these operations surely agree with those constructed by Kriz and May in I.7.1 of
[
47
]
and by Joshua in
[
43
]
.

On the other hand, Joshua also includes in
[
43
]
, as does Brosnan and Joshua in
[
8
]
, the following property of the Steenrod operations they construct.

Theorem 3.3
.

The operations
Q^{s}
of Joshua in
[
43
]
commute with the cycle class map into mod
\ell
étale cohomology, for
\ell\neq p
.

We warn the reader that this is a red herring; first, the paper
[
8
]
does not specify the a priori
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the sheaf cochains of
\mu_{\ell}
. Rather, Joshua is able to obtain the above theorem in
[
43
]
by transferring the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure he builds on mod
\ell
motivic cochains the other way over to the sheaf cochains of
\mu_{\ell}
via the cycle class map; the commutativity of the operations is then immediate by construction, with respect to this transferred
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure (see the last paragraph of 7.1 after Corollary 7.10 in
[
43
]
).

One should first define Steenrod operations on mod
\ell
étale cohomology via the
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the Godement cochains. Then, one should see that the cycle class map (which is actually built using the Godement resolution, as in section
2.3
), commutes with the operad actions on the cycle complexes and the Godement resolution respectively. We again leave this to the astute algebraic topologist to check, as again it does not matter for the purposes of this paper.

3.2

Cohomology theories and integrations with support

In this section we list the axioms of Panin–Smirnov–Levine and briefly verify that higher Chow groups with their projective pushforwards satisfy them. This allows us to use some of their results freely. The axioms also provide a framework in which other cohomology theories with potential obstructions may be used. Their work concerns ring cohomology theories equipped with an orientation (i.e., a Thom class). They show that this is equivalent to equipping a given ring cohomology theory with a Chern structure (i.e., Chern classes), and also equivalent to equipping it with a transfer (i.e., a proper pushforward) or a trace (i.e., a Thom isomorphism). Given a ring cohomology theory equipped with any one of these equivalent notions, and given a natural ring operation on the cohomology theory, they are then able to deduce Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch type formulas describing the failure of commutativity for the ring operation with proper pushforward. Most of the material in this section can be found in the papers of Panin
[
64
]
, Panin–Smirnov
[
67
]
, Smirnov
[
70
]
, and Levine
[
54
]
.

3.2.1

Cohomology and pushforward à la Panin, Smirnov, Levine

We repeat the definition of a ring cohomology theory, following Panin
[
64
]
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
SmOp
denote the category of pairs
(X,U)
where both
X
is a smooth variety and
U
is open in
X
. Morphisms are maps of pairs. The category
Sm
of smooth varieties is a full subcategory of
SmOp
by sending
X
to the pair
(X,\varnothing)
.

A
cohomology theory
is a contravariant functor
\textbf{SmOp}\xrightarrow{A}\textbf{Ab}
with natural maps
A(U)\xrightarrow{\partial}A(X,U)
satisfying the following properties:

1.

(Localization) For a pair
(X,U)\in\textbf{SmOp}
, the sequence

A(X)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}A(U)\xrightarrow{\partial_{(X,U)}}A(X,U)\xrightarrow{i^{*}}A(X)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}A(U)

is exact, where
j:U\hookrightarrow X
and
i:(X,\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,U)
are the natural inclusions.

2.

(Excision) The map
A(X,U)\rightarrow A(X^{\prime},U^{\prime})
induced by a morphism
e:(X^{\prime},U^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,U)
is an isomorphism, if the map
e:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
is étale, and for
Z=X-U
and
Z^{\prime}=X^{\prime}-U^{\prime}
, one has
e^{-1}(Z)=Z^{\prime}
and
e:Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
is an isomorphism.

3.

(Homotopy) The map
A(X)\rightarrow A(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
induced by the projection
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow X
is an isomorphism.

The operator
\partial
is the
boundary operator
. A morphism of cohomology theories
\phi:(A,\partial^{A})\rightarrow(B,\partial^{B})
is a natural transformation
\phi:A\rightarrow B
such that for every pair
(X,U)\in\textbf{SmOp}
, the boundary operators commute with the natural transformation maps. That is, we have
\partial^{B}\circ\phi_{U}=\phi_{(X,U)}\circ\partial^{A}
.

We also write
A_{Z}(X)
for
A(X,U)
where
U=X-Z
for
Z
a closed subset of
X
, and call
A_{Z}(X)
the cohomology of
X
with support on
Z
.

We see that the functor
(X,X-Z)\rightarrow\bigoplus H^{i}_{Z}(X,j)
given by the direct product of higher Chow groups is a cohomology theory: localization is Proposition
2.1
(3), étale excision is Proposition
2.7
, and homotopy invariance is Proposition
2.1
(4). Since the cycle complexes are free modules (and therefore flat), tensor products preserve quasi-isomorphisms, and so the mod
\ell
cycle complexes also satisfy all of the above properties (even homotopy invariance when
\ell=p
).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
P=(X,U)
and
Q=(Y,V)
be in
SmOp
. We define their product to be
P\times Q=(X\times Y,X\times V\cup U\times Y)\in\textbf{SmOp}
.

A
ring cohomology theory

A
is a cohomology theory such that, for every
P,Q\in\textbf{SmOp}
, there is a natural bilinear morphism
\times:A(P)\times A(Q)\rightarrow A(P\times Q)
which we call the
external product
functorial in both variables with the following properties:

1.

(Associativity)
(a\times b)\times c=a\times(b\times c)\in A(P\times Q\times R)
for
a\in A(P)
,
b\in A(Q)
, and
c\in A(R)
;

2.

(Unit) There is an element
1\in A(\textnormal{Spec }k)
such that for any pair
P\in\textbf{SmOp}
and any
a\in A(P)
, one has
1\times a=a=a\times 1\in A(P)
;

3.

(Partial Leibniz)
\partial_{P\times Y}(a\times b)=\partial_{P}(a)\times b\in A(X\times Y,U\times Y)
for a pair
P=(X,U)\in\textbf{SmOp}
, a smooth variety
Y
, and elements
a\in A(U),b\in A(Y)
.

Given an external product, one can define cup products
A_{Z}(X)\times A_{Z^{\prime}}(X)\rightarrow A_{Z\cap Z^{\prime}}(X)
by defining
a\cup b=\Delta^{*}(a\times b)
, where
\Delta:(X,U\cup V)\hookrightarrow(X\times X,X\times V\cup U\times X)
is the diagonal. If
p:X\rightarrow\textnormal{Spec }k
is the structure map, then
p^{*}(1)\in A(X)
is the unit for the cup products
A_{Z}(X)\times A(X)\rightarrow A_{Z}(X)
and
A(X)\times A_{Z}(X)\rightarrow A_{Z}(X)
. A partial Leibniz rule for cup product also holds:
\partial(a\cup j^{*}b)=\partial a\cup j^{*}b
for
a\in A(U),b\in A(X)
.

Moreover, given a cup product, one can construct an external product
a\times b=\pi_{X}^{*}(a)\cup\pi_{Y}^{*}(b)
for
a\in A(X,U)
and
b\in A(Y,V)
, where
\pi_{X}
and
\pi_{Y}
are the natural projections from
X\times Y
. This construction is inverse to the construction above.

We have constructed the external product and cup product for higher Chow groups in Proposition
2.2
, and can extend them to the case with supports by using relative cycles. We defer the case with supports to the Appendix
A
. Moreover, we have that the boundary operator on higher Chow groups is of degree
+1
, as a map
H^{i}(U,j)\rightarrow H^{i+1}_{Z}(X,j)
. This, combined with the fact that our cup product is graded commutative in
i
, yields the partial Leibniz rule (see Remark 1.7 in
[
64
]
). This establishes higher Chow groups as a ring cohomology theory. Other examples of ring cohomology theories can be found in the paper of Panin
[
63
]
such as classical singular cohomology over
\mathbb{C}
, mod
\ell\neq p
étale cohomology over a field of characteristic
p>0
, algebraic
K
-theory, algebraic cobordism theory, any representable theory, etc.

Definition 3.4
.

Suppose we have a fiber product diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where all varieties are smooth,
Y^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{i}Y
is a closed embedding, with normal bundles
N:=N_{Y/Y^{\prime}}
and
N^{\prime}:=N_{X/X^{\prime}}
. The square is
transversal
if the canonical morphism
N^{\prime}\rightarrow f^{\prime*}N
is an isomorphism.

We now present the definition of an integration for a ring cohomology theory, following Levine
[
54
]
and Panin
[
64
]
. Levine works in the setting where his cohomology theory is
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded; however, his results hold for bi-graded theories with the assumption that integrations shift bi-degrees
f_{!}:A^{p,q}(X)\rightarrow A^{p+2d,q+d}(Y)
, where
d=\textnormal{dim }(Y)-\textnormal{dim }(X)
. See Remark 1.7 in
[
54
]
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
A
be a ring cohomology theory. A
proper pushforward
(or
integration
or
trace structure
or
orientation
or
transfer
) is an assignment
(f:X\rightarrow Y)\mapsto f_{!}:A(X)\rightarrow A(Y)
where
f_{!}
is a two-sided
A(X)
-module map, and
f
is a projective morphism between smooth varieties
X
and
Y
, such that the following properties are satisfied:

1.

(g\circ f)_{!}=g_{!}\circ f_{!}
for projective morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
g:Y\rightarrow Z
;

2.

for every transversal square, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(Y);}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

3.

for any morphism of smooth varieties
f:X\rightarrow Y
, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(Y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{X})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{Y})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id\times f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

where
\pi_{X}:\mathbb{P}^{n}\times X\rightarrow X
and
\pi_{Y}:\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y\rightarrow Y
are the natural projections;

4.

for any smooth variety
X
, one has
id_{!}=id^{*}
on
A(X)
;

5.

for any closed embeddings of smooth varieties
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
, with complementary open embedding
P-X\xhookrightarrow{j}P
, the sequence
A(X)\xrightarrow{i_{!}}A(P)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}A(P-X)
is exact.

We have that the induced pushforward on higher Chow groups for a proper map
f:X\rightarrow Y
satisfies the axioms of an integration: properties (2) and (3) follow from Proposition
2.2
, property (5) is localization, and the fact that
f_{*}
is a
H^{*}(X,*)
-module homomorphism is the projection formula (Proposition
2.4
). Notice that, for our theory, the extra hypothesis on the square being transversal for condition (2) is superfluous.

For sake of completion, we also present the notion of an integration with support, due to Levine
[
54
]
, following Panin
[
67
]
. This is essentially Definition
3.5
, with supports added everywhere.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
A
be a ring cohomology theory. We define a new category
SmOp’
where objects are pairs
(X,U)
where
U
is open in smooth
X
, and morphisms
f:(X,U)\rightarrow(Y,V)
are projective maps
f:X\rightarrow Y
such that
f(X-U)\subset Y-V
. Equivalently, we can consider pairs
(X,Z)
where
Z
is a closed subset of
X
(called the support), and consider projective maps
f:X\rightarrow Y
that send supports to supports.

An
integration with supports
is an assignment to every morphism
f:(X,Z)\rightarrow(Y,W)
in
SmOp’
a map
f_{!}:A_{Z}(X)\rightarrow A_{W}(Y)
satisfying the following properties:

1.

(g\circ f)_{!}=g_{!}\circ f_{!}
for composable morphisms;

2.

for
f:(X,Z)\rightarrow(Y,W)
in
SmOp’
, and
S
a closed subset of
Y
,
f_{!}
is a
A_{S}(Y)
-module map; i.e., the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S}(Y)\otimes A_{Z}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z\cap f^{-1}(S)}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S}(Y)\otimes A_{W}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S\cap W}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}

commutes;

3.

let
i:(X,Z)\rightarrow(Y,W)
be in
SmOp’
such that
i:X\rightarrow Y
is a closed embedding, and let
g:(Y^{\prime},W^{\prime})\rightarrow(Y,W)
be a morphism in
SmOp
. Let
X^{\prime}=X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
i^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
be the projections. Suppose in addition that
X^{\prime}
is smooth and the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

is transverse. Then the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i^{\prime-1}(W)}(X^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{W^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{W}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}

commutes;

4.

let
f:(X,Z)\rightarrow(Y,W)
be a morphism in
SmOp
and let
p_{X}:\mathbb{P}^{n}\times X\rightarrow X
and
p_{Y}:\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y\rightarrow Y
be the projections. Then the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Z}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathbb{P}^{n}\times W}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{W}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{X})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id\times f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{Y})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

commutes;

5.

given
(X,Z)
and
(X,Z^{\prime})
such that
Z^{\prime}\subset Z
, the maps
id_{*}
and
id^{*}
from
A_{Z^{\prime}}(X)\rightarrow A_{Z}(X)
agree (here, the pull-back is induced by the long exact sequence of a triple, see Remark 1.4 in Levine
[
54
]
);

6.

let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a projective morphism of smooth varieties, let
S\subset S^{\prime}\subset X
and
T\subset T^{\prime}\subset Y
be closed subsets, and suppose
f^{-1}(T)\cap S^{\prime}=S
, and
f(S^{\prime})\subset T^{\prime}
. Then the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S^{\prime}-S}(X-S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{S}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{T^{\prime}-T}(Y-T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{T}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{X,S^{\prime},S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{Y,T^{\prime},T}}

commutes. Here,
\partial_{X,S^{\prime},S}
and
\partial_{Y,T^{\prime},T}
are the boundary maps in the long exact sequences for the triples
(X,S^{\prime},S)
and
(Y,T^{\prime},T)
, and the pushforward map
f:A_{S^{\prime}-S}(X-S)\rightarrow A_{T^{\prime}-T}(Y-T)
is the composition

A_{S^{\prime}-S}(X-S)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}A_{S^{\prime}-S}(X-f^{-1}(T))\xrightarrow{(f|_{X-f^{-1}})_{*}}A_{T^{\prime}-T}(Y-T).

We have that the projective pushforward with supports (Definition
2.9
) satisfies the axioms of an integration with support: property (2) is the projection formula with supports (Proposition
A.6
), properties (3) and (4) follow from Proposition
A.5
, and we leave properties (5) and (6) to the reader, using that localization and excision imply the long exact sequence of a triple, and the discussion of relative cycles in the beginning of section
2.2
.

Given an integration, one can construct an integration with supports uniquely due to the following theorem of Levine (Theorem 1.12 and Corollary 1.13 in
[
54
]
). Although stated for the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded theories, the proof holds for the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded and bi-graded cases similarly.

Theorem 3.4
.

(Levine) Let
A
be a ring cohomology theory. Given an integration
f\mapsto f_{!}
on
A
, there is a unique integration with supports on
A
extending
f
.

The proof of the theorem follows the usual strategy of decomposing a given projective morphism
f
into a closed embedding and a projection from a trivial projective bundle. For the closed embedding case, one employs the deformation to the normal cone. For the projective bundle case, there is a projective bundle formula (see Theorem 3.9 in
[
63
]
). Moreover, Levine proves the following proposition which will be useful (Theorem 1.24(a) in
[
54
]
).

Proposition 3.4
.

(Levine) Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a projective morphism. Factor
f
as a closed embedding
i:X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y
where
i=i_{X}\times f
and where
i_{X}
is some choice of closed embedding for
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and a projection
\pi:\mathbb{P}^{n}\times Y\rightarrow Y
. Then
f=\pi\circ i
, and the morphism
f_{!}=\pi_{!}\circ i_{!}
does not depend on the choice of factorization.

Remark 3.4
.

Smirnov defines the notion of a
transfer
in
[
70
]
, which has axioms (1)-(4) of an integration with support. We will quote Smirnov’s theorem later for integrations with support, and not the more general notion of transfer. Our projective pushforward for higher Chow groups satisfies the axioms of both notions, so there is no real difference for our setting.

3.2.2

Generalized Todd classes

In this section, we will discuss Smirnov’s generalized Riemann–Roch theorem for ring cohomology theories and integrations with support. We will follow
[
70
]
.

We present the definition of an operation for a ring cohomology theory; it is a ring homomorphism. We avoid the categorical generalities of Smirnov’s definition in
[
70
]
, and only present what is necessary for our discussion.

Definition 3.7
.

An
operation

\phi
of a ring cohomology
A
theory is a natural transformation
A\rightarrow A
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X)\otimes A(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X)\otimes A(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(X\times Y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\otimes\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Moreover, we require that it is compatible with the cup product structure on cohomology with supports; namely, that it is also defined for cohomology with support:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z}(X)\otimes A_{Z^{\prime}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z}(X)\otimes A_{Z^{\prime}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z\cap Z^{\prime}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z\cap Z^{\prime}}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\otimes\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

In general, one can consider two different ring cohomology theories
A
and
B
, and one needs a functor to compare the monoidal structures on two possibly different output categories; for example, the Chern character is an operation between
K
-theory and cohomology. However, we will only consider ring operations
\phi:A\rightarrow A
in this paper.

Example 3.1
.

The total Steenrod power
\phi=Q^{\bullet}:=\sum_{s}Q^{s}
given by the formal series of Steenrod operations from Theorem
3.1
is an operation on motivic cohomology by the Cartan formula.

Example 3.2
.

The total Steenrod power
\phi=P^{\bullet}:=\sum_{s}P^{s}
given by the formal series of Steenrod operations in the sense of Voevodsky
[
81
]
is also an operation on motivic cohomology, again since it satisfies the Cartan formula.

Before we continue, we must consider the notion of Chern classes for our generalized ring cohomology theories. See Definition 1.12 in
[
64
]
.

Definition 3.8
.

A
Chern structure
on a ring cohomology theory
A
is an assignment
L\mapsto c(L)
for every smooth variety
X
and every line bundle
L
over
X
an even degree element
c(L)\in A(X)
satisfying the following properties:

1.

(Functoriality)
c(L_{1})=c(L_{2})
if
L_{1}\cong L_{2}
, and for every map
f:Y\rightarrow X
between smooth varieties, we have
f^{*}c(L)=c(f^{*}L)
;

2.

(Non-degeneracy) the map
A(X)\oplus A(X)\rightarrow A(X\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
given by sending
(x,y)
to
\pi^{*}x+\pi^{*}y\cup\xi
is an isomorphism, where
\xi=c(\mathcal{O}(-1))
for the tautological bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1)
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
\pi:X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow X
is the projection;

3.

(Vanishing)
c(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})=0\in A(X)
for the trivial line bundle
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
on any smooth variety
X
.

The element
c(L)\in A(X)
is called the
Chern class
of
L
.

As stated in section
2.4
, for higher Chow groups, we have Chern classes from ordinary Chow groups. Panin and Smirnov show that equipping a ring cohomology theory with an integration is equivalent to equipping it with a Chern structure; in fact, the set of integrations is in bijection with the set of Chern structures (Theorem 2.5 in
[
64
]
). One obtains higher Chern classes using the projective bundle formula, which holds for our notion of ring cohomology theory (see Theorem 3.9 in
[
63
]
).

Given an operation for a ring cohomology theory equipped with Chern classes, there is a notion of an inverse Todd class. As in the classical setting, one uses the splitting principle, which is available to us in our general setting via the projective bundle formula (see Definition 5.1.1 from the preprint
[
66
]
).

First we define
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
as the direct limit of projective spaces
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}:=\varinjlim\mathbb{P}^{k}
induced by inclusions of all finite dimensional subspaces. Similarly, we define
A(P^{\infty}):=\varprojlim A(\mathbb{P}^{k})
where
A
is our ring cohomology theory; we extend our given operation
\phi
to
A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})
by taking the direct limit of natural operations
\phi
on the projective spaces in the directed system. It follows from the projective bundle theorem that we can identify
A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})
with the ring
A(\textnormal{Spec }k)[[u]]
of formal power series with coefficients in
A(\textnormal{Spec }k)
, with the class
u
pulling back along any of the inclusions
\mathbb{P}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
to
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))
. We then define the inverse Todd genus for
\phi
on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
, then extend the definition to all vector bundles by splitting principle.

Lemma 3.4
.

The operation
\phi
on
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
maps the generator
u\in A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})
to a formal power series
\phi(u)
which is divisible by
u
.

Proof.

The constant term in the formal power series
\phi(u)
is given by the action of
\phi
on the class
u
pulled back to
A(\textnormal{Spec }k)
via compatible choices of inclusion
\textnormal{Spec }k\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}
in the direct system. However, the class
u
pulled back to
\textnormal{Spec }k
is
0
, since
u=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))
and
\mathcal{O}(1)
trivializes over a point.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

We define the
inverse Todd genus associated to an operation

\phi
on a ring cohomology theory
A
