equipped with a Chern structure, for any vector bundle
E
on a smooth variety
X
as follows.

1.

We first define the
inverse Todd genus
of
\phi
as the formal power series

itd_{\phi}(u)=\frac{\phi(u)}{u}\in A[[u]]

where we consider the ring operation
\phi:A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})\rightarrow A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty}).

2.

Let
t_{1},...,t_{n}
be independent variables and let
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}
be the elementary symmetric polynomials in these variables (i.e.,
\sigma_{1}=t_{1}+...+t_{n},\sigma_{2}=t_{1}t_{2}+...+t_{n-1}t_{n},...,\sigma_{n}=t_{1}...t_{n}
). We define

itd_{\phi}(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}itd_{\phi}(t_{i})\in A[[\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}]].

3.

We define the
inverse Todd genus
of a vector bundle
E
with rank
n
over a smooth variety
X
as the evaluation of the series
itd_{\phi}(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})
on the Chern classes of
E
:

itd_{\phi}(E)=itd_{\phi}(c_{1}(E),...,c_{n}(E))\in A(X).

The Todd genus will measure the failure for the given operation
\phi
to commute with the proper pushforward (which is uniquely determined by the Chern structure). However, we require one additional hypothesis on
\phi
to make the notion of Todd genus precise.

Definition 3.10
.

Assume that the series
itd_{\phi}(u)
is invertible in
A[[u]]
(this is equivalent to the free term of the series being a unit in
A
). In this case, we say that
\phi
has
well-defined Todd genus
.

1.

Define the
Todd genus
of
\phi
as the multiplicative inverse of
itd_{\phi}(u)

td_{\phi}(u):=\frac{u}{\phi(u)}\in A[[u]].

2.

Set
td_{\phi}(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}td_{\phi}(t_{i})\in A[[\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}]]
.

3.

Define the
Todd genus
of a vector bundle
E
with rank
n
over a smooth variety
X
as

td_{\phi}(E)=td_{\phi}(c_{1}(E),...,c_{n}(E))\in A(X).

Before we compute the inverse Todd genus for our operations from Theorem
3.1
, we first compute the motivic cohomology of
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
. By the projective bundle formula, this only depends on the Chow groups of projective space, and the motivic cohomology of our underlying field. We present below the main theorem of Geisser–Levine in
[
34
]
.

Theorem 3.5
.

(Geisser–Levine) The mod
\ell=p
motivic cohomology of a field
k
of characteristic
p
is zero in bidegrees
(i,j)
where
i\neq j
. For
i=j
, it is given by the
i
-th Milnor
K
-theory group
K^{M}_{i}(k)/p
. In particular, if
k
is algebraically closed, then the mod
\ell=p
motivic cohomology groups vanish for all
(i,j)
except in bidegree
(0,0)
.

Proof.

We only prove the last statement: by definition,
K^{M}_{i}(k)
is the
i
-fold tensor product of
K^{M}_{1}(k)
with itself, modulo Steinberg relations (i.e., Milnor
K
-theory is generated as a ring in degree
1
). We also have that
K^{M}_{1}(k)\cong k^{*}
as groups; since
k
is algebraically closed,
K^{M}_{1}(k)/p=0
, since every element of
k^{*}
admits a
p
-th root. Thus all the higher Milnor
K
-groups vanish. In degree
0
, we have that
K^{M}_{0}(k)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

On the other hand, if
\ell\neq p
, we have by the Beilinson–Lichtenbaum conjecture and the work discussed in section
2.3
that the mod
\ell
motivic cohomology groups are isomorphic to
H^{i}(k_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}},\mu^{\otimes j}_{\ell})
. Since
k
is algebraically closed, we have that
\mu_{\ell}
identifies with the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
, and
\mu_{\ell}^{\otimes j}\cong\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
for all
j
. Moreover, étale cohomology of a field agrees with the group cohomology of its absolute Galois group, so since
k
is algebraically closed, we have that the étale cohomology groups all vanish above degree
0
. In degree
0
, we have that
H^{0}(k_{\textnormal{\'{e}t}},\mu^{\otimes j}_{\ell})\cong\mathbb{Z}/\ell
for any
j\geq 0
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The groups
H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))
are
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
in degrees
(i,j)=(2m,j)
where
j,m\geq 0
, for
\ell\neq p
. The groups are
\mathbb{Z}/p
in degrees
(i,j)=(2m,m)
for
m\geq 0
, for
\ell=p
.

Proof.

By the projective bundle theorem, for a given projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we have that the mod
\ell
motivic cohomology is given by the ordinary Chow groups of projective space tensored with the mod
\ell
motivic cohomology groups of the underlying field. That is, we have

H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j))\cong\bigoplus^{n}_{m=0}H^{i-2m}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j-m))

where
k
is our underlying field. A given element in degree
(i,j)
is then a series
\alpha=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\alpha_{m}u^{m}
, where
\alpha_{j}\in H^{i-2m}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(j-m))
, and
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
is the generator for the normal Chow groups of projective space.

When
\ell\neq p
, by Beilinson–Lichtenbaum, we have that the only vanishing groups for our field is when
i=2m
, for arbitrary
j
. In this case, the group is
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
. When
\ell=p
, all the groups vanish except when
(i,j)=(2m,m)
. That is, the only non-zero higher Chow groups mod
p
of projective space are those given by the ordinary Chow groups mod
p
, over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

The mod
\ell\neq p
operation
Q^{\bullet}
sends the generator
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
to
u+u^{\ell}
.

Proof.

The class
u
is in
H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
, and so
Q^{s}(u)=0
if
2s>2
by vanishing of the Steenrod operations on classes of lower degree. Thus,
Q^{\bullet}(u)=Q^{0}(u)+Q^{1}(u)
. We have that
Q^{1}(u)=u^{\ell}
by definition of the Steenrod operations. By Proposition 8.4 in
[
43
]
, using naturality of the operations
Q^{\bullet}
on any mod
\ell
sheaf cohomology, it suffices to check
Q^{0}
on the cohomology of the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{Z}/\ell}
. However, we have that
Q^{0}=id
in this case, since
x^{\ell}=x\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell
.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

The inverse Todd genus of
Q^{\bullet}
of a vector bundle
E
with rank
n
over a smooth variety
X
is given by

itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(E)=c_{n}(E)^{p-1}

where
Q^{\bullet}
acts on mod
p
motivic cohomology of
X
.

Proof.

If we apply
Q^{\bullet}
to the generator
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
, we obtain
Q^{\bullet}(u)=u^{p}
. The proof is as follows: again by vanishing of Steenrod operations on classes of lower degree, we have that
Q^{s}(u)=0
if
2s>2
. Thus,
Q^{\bullet}(u)=Q^{0}(u)+Q^{1}(u)
. However, we also have
Q^{0}(u)\in H^{2}(P^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/p(p))=0
as we computed above. Moreover,
Q^{1}(u)=u^{p}
by definition of the simplicial operations. Tracing through the definition of an inverse Todd genus for a bundle, we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The result of Geisser–Levine holds for any field of characteristic
p
; thus, the vanishing of groups
H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/p(p))
holds for any field of characteristic
p
, not necessarily algebraically closed, for
p>2
. If
p=2
, and the field is not algebraically closed, it is possible for
H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/2(2))
to be non-zero. From the projective bundle theorem, we see that an element
\alpha
is given by
\sum\alpha_{m}u^{m}
for
\alpha_{m}\in H^{2-2m}(\textnormal{Spec }k,\mathbb{Z}/2(2-m))
. However, again by Geisser–Levine, the only a priori non-zero term is the constant term
\alpha_{0}\in K^{M}_{2}(k)/2
. Since the series
Q^{\bullet}(u)
must be divisible by
u
, we see that
Q^{0}(u)
still vanishes even in this case. We conclude this section with a computation of the inverse Todd genus for the mod
\ell\neq p
simplicial operations and the mod
\ell
motivic operations.

Lemma 3.6
.

The inverse Todd genus of
Q^{\bullet}
acting on mod
\ell\neq p
motivic cohomology of a vector bundle
E
with rank
n
over a smooth variety
X
is given by

itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(E)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+\alpha_{i})^{\ell-1}

where
\alpha_{i}
is the
i
-th Chern root of the bundle
E
. Moreover, the inverse Todd genus for the motivic operation
P^{\bullet}
mod
\ell
(including when
\ell=p
) is given by the same formula.

Proof.

The operations in the lemma all map the generator
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}/\ell(1))
to the formal power series
u+u^{\ell}
. Thus the formal power series
\frac{\phi(u)}{u}
is given by
1+u^{\ell-1}
. One then traces through the definition of the inverse Todd genus and obtains the result.
∎

3.3

Riemann–Roch formulas for projective pushforward

We now present two theorems of Smirnov from
[
70
]
, which are similar to the main theorems of Panin–Smirnov in
[
66
]
,
[
67
]
. The main theorems are Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch style formulas describing failure of commutativity of a given ring operation
\phi
with proper pushforward on a ring cohomology theory
A
, with the defects arising from the Todd classes. We present the theorems from
[
70
]
because Smirnov specifically deals with the case of cohomology with support. We assume we are in the setting of Definition
2.9
.

Theorem 3.6
.

(Relative Wu formula) Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
and
X^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
be closed immersions into smooth varieties
P
and
P^{\prime}
, and suppose we have closed immersions
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

Suppose we have an operation
\phi
on a ring cohomology theory
A
equipped with a transfer (or equivalently a Chern structure) so that the proper pushforward
f_{!}
on
A
is defined. Then we have

\phi(f_{!}(\alpha))=f_{!}(\phi(\alpha)\cup itd_{\phi}(N_{P}P^{\prime}))

where
N_{P}P^{\prime}
denotes the normal bundle of
P
in
P^{\prime}
and
\alpha\in A_{X}(P)
.

Smirnov actually only requires the embedding to be proper on the support
X
for the theorem to hold (i.e.,
f
need not be a closed immersion, but
f^{\prime}
must be). This allows us to deal with quasi-projective varieties in general; however, we will only work with ambient spaces given by projective varieties.

If we take our theory
A
to be singular cohomology, our varieties to be defined over
\mathbb{C}
, our ring operation to be the classical mod
2
Steenrod operations, and forget the supports, then we recover the classical Wu formula.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\phi=Q^{\bullet}
be defined mod
\ell=p
, let
A
be given by motivic cohomology, and let
f_{!}
be the proper pushforward in Definition
2.9
for a diagram as in the hypothesis of Theorem
3.6
. Then we have

Q^{\bullet}(f_{!}(\alpha))=f_{!}(\phi(\alpha)\cup c_{\textnormal{top}}^{p-1}(N_{P}P^{\prime}))

where
c_{\textnormal{top}}(N_{P}P^{\prime})
is the top Chern class of the normal bundle
N_{P}P^{\prime}
.

Theorem
3.6
deals with the closed embedding case only; Theorem
3.7
generalizes to any proper map. However, Theorem
3.7
requires an additional hypothesis on the operation
\phi
; namely,
\phi
must have well-defined Todd genus.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
and
X^{\prime}\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
be closed immersions into smooth varieties
P
and
P^{\prime}
, and suppose we have proper maps
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

Suppose we have an operation
\phi
on a ring cohomology theory
A
equipped with a transfer (or equivalently a Chern structure) so that the proper pushforward
f_{!}
on
A
is defined. Suppose that
\phi
has well-defined Todd genus. Then we have

\phi(f_{!}(\alpha))\cup td_{\phi}(TP^{\prime})=f_{!}(\phi(\alpha)\cup td_{\phi}(TP))

where
TP^{\prime}
and
TP
denote the tangent bundles of
P^{\prime}
and
P
respectively, and
\alpha\in A_{X}(P)
. We call this equation a
Riemann–Roch type formula
.

Theorems
3.6
and
3.7
again hold when one has two different cohomology theories with an operation
\phi
that is able to compare the two appropriately, such as
K
-theory and singular cohomology, with the Chern character. In this classical setting, the Todd genus is the usual Todd class, and one recovers the Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch formula. We also immediately obtain the following corollary from Lemma
3.6
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\phi=Q^{\bullet}
be defined for mod
\ell\neq p
, or
\phi=P^{\bullet}
for any prime
\ell
. Let
A
be motivic cohomology and let
f_{!}
be the proper pushforward in Definition
2.9
for a diagram as in the hypothesis of Theorem
3.7
. Then we have

\phi(f_{!}(\alpha))\cup td_{\phi}(TP^{\prime})=f_{!}(\phi(\alpha)\cup td_{\phi}(TP))

where
TP^{\prime}
and
TP
denote the tangent bundles of
P^{\prime}
and
P
respectively, and
\alpha\in H_{X}^{*}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
.

The proof of Theorem
3.7
is in the same spirit as Satz 3.2 of Atiyah–Hirzebruch
[
3
]
and other Riemann–Roch type formulae where one first realizes the given projective morphism as the composition of a closed embedding and a projection, then proves the formula for each case separately. The case of a closed embedding is exactly Theorem
3.6
. However, one needs to take care formalizing the argument for cohomology with support on a singular subvariety; to do this, we employ the formalism of Panin–Smirnov
[
64
]
,
[
67
]
and Levine’s extension of their theory to the case of supports (Section 1 of
[
54
]
). We will carefully analyze the proofs of the above theorem specialized to our specific operation
Q^{\bullet}
defined mod
\ell
for any prime
\ell
.

Remark 3.6
.

The paper of Brosnan and Joshua
[
8
]
identifies the mod
\ell\neq p
operations with operations defined through a cyclic group action construction (analogous to Voevodsky’s construction of operations). There they are able to describe a Riemann–Roch type formula as well, similar to work in
[
13
]
. However, their paper has an error, where they claim to do so for
\ell=p
as well: the problem is the cyclic group action construction requires a primitive
\ell
-th root of unity in the underlying field, which does not exist if
\ell
equals the characteristic.

3.3.1

Closed embeddings

We analyze the proof of Theorem
3.6
for our operations
Q^{\bullet}
mod
\ell
, for any prime
\ell
. First we recall the deformation to the normal cone (see Chapter 5 in
[
29
]
).

Let
X
be a closed subvariety of
Y
. Then there is a family of embeddings
X\hookrightarrow Y_{t}
parameterized by
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
(resp.
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
), such that for
t\neq\infty
(resp.
t\neq 0
), the embedding is the given closed immersion
X\hookrightarrow Y
, and for
t=\infty
(resp.
t=0
), the embedding is the zero section of
X
in the normal cone
N_{X}Y
. Moreover, if
X
is smooth, then the normal cone is actually a normal bundle. The family of embeddings is constructed by first building a flat family
M=M_{X}Y
, with a closed embedding of
X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
M
and a flat morphism
\rho:M\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
so that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
\pi
is the projection, and such that

1.

over
\mathbb{P}^{1}-\{\infty\}=\mathbb{A}^{1}
, we have
\rho^{-1}(\mathbb{A}^{1})=Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
and the embedding is the product embedding
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
,

2.

over
\infty
, the divisor
M_{\infty}=\rho^{-1}(\infty)
is the sum of two effective Cartier divisors

M_{\infty}=\mathbb{P}(N_{X}Y\oplus 1)+Bl_{X}Y,

where
\mathbb{P}(N_{X}Y\oplus 1)
is the hyperplane at infinity for the normal cone, and
Bl_{X}Y
is the blowup of
Y
along
X
. We call the flat family
M
the
deformation to the normal cone
.

If we wish to parameterize with
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
(as in 2.2.7 in
[
63
]
), we obtain the same diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where the fiber of
\rho
over
1\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
is canonically isomorphic to
Y
, and the embedding
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\hookrightarrow M
is the initial embedding
X\hookrightarrow Y
over
1
, and the fiber of
\rho
over
0
is canonically isomorphic to
N_{X}Y
, with the embedding over
0
given by the zero section
X\hookrightarrow N_{X}Y
. Thus we obtain maps of pairs

(N_{X}Y,N_{X}Y-X)\xrightarrow{i_{0}}(M,M-(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}))\xleftarrow{i_{1}}(Y,Y-X)

allowing us to relate the cohomology of the Thom space of the normal bundle, with the cohomology of the pair given by the ambient space modulo the complement of the subspace. In fact, for a ring cohomology theory, we have the following general theorem (Theorem 2.2 in
[
63
]
, Lemma 1.14 in
[
54
]
).

Theorem 3.8
.

For a ring cohomology theory
A
, the induced maps of pairs

A_{X}(N_{X}Y)\xleftarrow{i_{0}^{*}}A_{X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}(M)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}}A_{X}(Y)

are isomorphisms. Moreover, for each closed subset
Z\subset X
, the induced maps of pairs

A_{Z}(N_{X}Y)\xleftarrow{i_{0}^{*}}A_{Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}(M)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}}A_{Z}(Y)

are isomorphisms.

Remark 3.7
.

In the mod
\ell\neq p
étale setting, one can use the tubular neighborhoods of Friedlander
[
26
]
and Cox
[
16
]
to construct similar isomorphisms. However, they are not needed, as the isomorphisms still hold even without their constructions.

It is now appropriate for us to define a
Thom structure
in the sense of Panin–Smirnov
[
66
]
(Definition 3.3 in
[
63
]
). It is essentially the notion of a Thom isomorphism.

Definition 3.11
.

A
Thom structure
on a ring cohomology theory
A
is an assignment
L\mapsto\tau(L)
for every smooth variety
X
and every line bundle
L
over
X
an even degree element
\tau(L)\in A_{X}(L)
satisfying the following properties:

1.

(Functoriality)
\phi^{*}(\tau(L_{1}))=\tau(L_{2})
for every isomorphism
\phi:L_{1}\cong L_{2}
of line bundles; moreover
f_{L}^{*}(\tau(L))=\tau_{L_{Y}}
for every morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
and each line bundle
L
over
X
, where we write
L_{Y}=L\times_{X}Y
as the pullback line bundle over
Y
and
f_{L}:L_{Y}\rightarrow L
is the projection;

2.

(Non-degeneracy) the map
-\cup\tau(1):A(X)\rightarrow A_{X}(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
is an isomorphism, where
X
is viewed as the zero section of the trivial line bundle.

The element
\tau(L)\in A_{X}(L)
is called the
Thom class
of
L
.

Recall we have a notion of Thom class for higher Chow groups, from Corollary
2.2
. Moreover, by splitting principle, one extends the notion of Thom class for line bundles to general vector bundles. The main results of Panin–Smirnov
[
66
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
64
]
are then that the notions of Chern structures, Thom structures, and proper pushforwards are all in bijection with each other for a given ring cohomology theory; that is, given any one of the structures, one can recover the other two uniquely. This extends to the case of ring cohomology with support by work of Levine in
[
54
]
; one such result is as follows (see Theorem 1.12 and Proposition 1.15 in
[
54
]
).

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}Y
be a closed immersion of smooth varieties and consider the maps

A_{X}(N_{X}Y)\xleftarrow{i_{0}^{*}}A_{X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}(M)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}}A(Y)

where
i_{1}^{*}
is induced from the map of pairs
(Y,\varnothing)\xrightarrow{i_{1}}(M,M-(X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}))
. In this case,
i_{0}^{*}
is still an isomorphism (though
i_{1}^{*}
may not be). Moreover, the proper pushforward
i_{!}:A(X)\rightarrow A(Y)
agrees with the composition

A(X)\xrightarrow{\Phi}A_{X}(N_{X}Y)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}\circ(i_{0}^{*})^{-1}}A(Y)

where the map
\Phi
is given by
A(X)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}A(N_{X}Y)\xrightarrow{\cup\tau}A_{X}(N_{X}Y)
, where
\tau
is the Thom class of the normal bundle
N_{X}Y\xrightarrow{\pi}X
, and
X
is thought of as the zero section in
N_{X}Y
. More generally, for any closed subset
Z\subset X
and any closed subset
W\subset Y
that contains
i(Z)
, we have the map

A_{Z}(N_{X}Y)\xleftarrow{i_{0}^{*}}A_{Z\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}(M)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}}A_{W}(Y)

where again
i_{0}^{*}
is an isomorphism. In this case, the proper pushforward on cohomology with support
i_{!}:A_{Z}(X)\rightarrow A_{W}(Y)
is given by the composition

A_{Z}(X)\xrightarrow{\Phi}A_{Z}(N_{X}Y)\xrightarrow{i_{1}^{*}\circ(i_{0}^{*})^{-1}}A_{W}(Y)

where the map
\Phi
is given by
A_{Z}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}A_{\pi^{-1}(Z)}(N_{X}Y)\xrightarrow{\cup\tau}A_{Z}(N_{X}Y)
, where
\tau\in A_{X}(N_{X}Y)
is the Thom class of the normal bundle
N_{X}Y
.

In other words, the proper pushforward for a closed embedding is exactly given by cupping with the Thom class (aka the Thom isomorphism), up to deformation of the normal cone. In the original context, Proposition
3.7
is actually proven to show that, given a transfer structure, one can obtain a unique transfer with supports; Levine then constructs the transfer with supports explicitly. For our purposes, our logic is somewhat backwards: we have a notion of transfer with support already, but by Levine’s result on uniqueness, Proposition
3.7
says the transfer with support (for closed embeddings) must agree with a given form (that is, it is cupping with the Thom class).

Let us now verify Corollary
3.3
, the relative Wu formula for our simplicial operations
Q^{\bullet}
mod
p
.

Proof.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}Y
be a closed immersion. Then we have

Q^{\bullet}(i_{!}\alpha)=Q^{\bullet}(\varphi\circ(\pi^{*}\alpha\cup\tau))

where
\varphi=i^{*}_{1}\circ(i_{0}^{*})^{-1}
commutes with
Q^{\bullet}
by naturality of the operations, and
\tau
is the Thom class of the normal bundle
N_{X}Y
. After commuting
\varphi
and
Q^{\bullet}
, we obtain

\varphi(Q^{\bullet}(\pi^{*}\alpha\cup\tau))=\varphi(\pi^{*}Q^{\bullet}\alpha\cup Q^{\bullet}\tau)

but now we note that
Q^{\bullet}\tau=\tau\cup\pi^{*}(itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(N_{X}Y))
by definition of the inverse Todd genus, the splitting principle, and tracing through the equivalences between Thom structures and Chern structures. This leaves us with
\varphi(\pi^{*}Q^{\bullet}\alpha\cup\pi^{*}(itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(N_{X}Y))\cup\tau)=\varphi(\pi^{*}(Q^{\bullet}\alpha\cup itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(N_{X}Y))\cup\tau)=i_{!}(Q^{\bullet}\alpha\cup itd_{Q^{\bullet}}(N_{X}Y))
. We have computed the inverse Todd genus in Proposition
3.6
, which finishes the proof.
∎

With the definitions of proper pushforwards (Definition
3.5
), Chern structures (Definition
3.8
), and Thom structures (Definition
3.11
), we can formulate the following associations for a ring cohomology theory
A
in the sense of Panin–Smirnov.

For a proper pushforward on
A
, we can associate to it a Thom structure by applying the proper pushforward to zero sections of vector bundles. The Thom class is the image of the unit under the proper pushforward. We get a map

\{\textnormal{proper pushforwards on }A\}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{\textnormal{Thom structures on }A\}.

Given a Thom structure, one can define an associated Chern structure by defining
c(L)=s^{*}(th(L))
. That is, we can define an associated Chern structure by pulling back the Thom class of a line bundle
L
over a smooth variety
X
via the zero section
(X,\varnothing)\xhookrightarrow{s}(L,L-X)
. We then obtain a map of sets

\{\textnormal{Thom structures on }A\}\xrightarrow{\gamma}\{\textnormal{Chern structures on }A\}

sending
th(L)
to
c(L)=s^{*}(th(L))
.

Finally, given a Chern structure, we can define an associated proper pushforward as follows. For a proper map
f
, we again write it as a composition
f=\pi\circ i
where
\pi
is a projective bundle map and
i
is a closed embedding. For a closed embedding, first recall that the Thom space of a bundle
E
can be defined equivalently as the pair
(\mathbb{P}(E\oplus\mathbb{A}^{1}),\mathbb{P}E)
. One defines a Thom class for line bundles
L\rightarrow X
over smooth varieties by defining the Thom class as the class
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(1)\otimes p^{*}L)
, where
p:\mathbb{P}E\rightarrow X
is the projective bundle associated to
E=L\oplus\mathbb{A}^{1}
. We extend the definition to general vector bundles via splitting principle. We then define the associated proper pushforward for closed embeddings as cupping with the Thom class of the normal bundle, and using deformation to the normal cone. The proper pushforward associated to the projective bundle map
\pi
is determined by the formal group laws associated to the Chern structure (see Section
3.3.2
). This yields a map

\{\textnormal{Chern structures on }A\}\xrightarrow{\delta}\{\textnormal{proper pushforwards on }A\}.

We can now state the main theorem of Panin–Smirnov (see Theorems 3.5, 3.35, and 3.36 in
[
63
]
) for completion.

Theorem 3.9
.

For a ring cohomology theory
A
, we have the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{proper pushforwards on }A\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Chern structures on }A\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Thom structures on }A\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

where each arrow is a bijection and each round trip coincides with the identity.

3.3.2

Projective bundles

As mentioned, the strategy of the proof of Theorem
3.7
involves decomposing a given proper map
f
into a composition
f=\pi\circ i
where
i
is a closed embedding and
\pi
is a projection from a projective bundle. More specifically, if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a proper map of smooth varieties, then we can decompose it as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times\mathbb{P}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times e}

where the map
e
is some choice of embedding of
X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}
(recall all varieties are quasiprojective; since the map
f
is proper, the map
i=f\times e
will be a closed embedding). Recall also that the choice of decomposition does not matter (Proposition
3.4
). The resulting diagram on cohomology with supports is then
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}}(Y\times\mathbb{P}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Z^{\prime}}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\times e)_{!}}

where the maps are proper on the closed supports
Z\subset X
and send
Z
into
Z^{\prime}\subset Y
. The proof of Theorem
3.7
then involves proving the formula for the closed embedding case and projective bundle case separately. We have already dealt with the embedding case, and this section will cover the projective bundle case.

Suppose
X\subset P
is a closed subvariety with
P
smooth. We have the following projective bundle theorem for the case of supports (Corollary 3.17 in
[
63
]
, Remark 1.17 in
[
54
]
), by using the exact sequence for the pairs
(P,X)
and
(P\times\mathbb{P}^{n},X\times\mathbb{P}^{n})
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion with
P
smooth. For any closed subvariety
Y\subset P
that also contains
X
, we have an isomorphism of
A_{Y}(P)
-modules

A_{X\times\mathbb{P}^{n}}(P\times\mathbb{P}^{n})\cong A_{X}(P)\otimes_{A(P)}A(P)[t]/(t^{n+1})

with
a\otimes t^{i}
mapping to
\pi^{*}(a)\cup c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))^{i}
, where
\pi:P\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\rightarrow P
is the projection.

It suffices now to compute the proper pushforward
\pi_{!}
induced by the projective bundle. For more general ring cohomology theories
A
, the situation is more complicated: one passes from cohomology of the projective bundle
A(P\times\mathbb{P}^{n})
to the inverse limit
A(P\times\mathbb{P}^{\infty}):=\varprojlim A(P\times\mathbb{P}^{n})
and one reduces the proof to proving the formula for the infinite projective bundle. For a ring cohomology theory
A
equipped with a Chern structure, the fact that isomorphism classes of line bundles is a group yields a formal group law associated to the Chern structure; i.e.,
c_{1}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})=F_{A}(c_{1}(L_{1}),c_{1}(L_{2}))
where
F_{A}
is a formal group law over
A(\textnormal{Spec }k)
. The proper pushforward
\pi_{!}
, and by extension, the proof of the Riemann–Roch formula for the infinite projective bundle, is then determined by this formal group law.

Fortunately, we will now return to our original setting where our ring cohomology theory
A
is given by higher Chow groups, equipped with the usual notion of Chern class. In this case, we have from normal Chow group theory that
c_{1}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})=c_{1}(L_{1})+c_{1}(L_{2})
, and by definition the proper pushforward
\pi_{!}:A_{X}(P)\otimes_{A(P)}A(P)[t]/(t^{n+1})\rightarrow A_{X}(P)
is given by

\pi_{!}(a\otimes t^{i})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $i\neq n$}\\
a&\text{if $i=n$}\end{cases}

for an element
a\otimes t^{i}\in A_{X}(P)\otimes_{A(P)}A(P)[t]/(t^{n+1})
. Geometrically, the only subvarieties we are projecting are those originally in
P
, since the class
a\otimes t^{n}
corresponds to the product of a given subvariety with the class of a point. We recall Corollary
3.4
.

Corollary 3.5
.

For the mod
\ell\neq p
operations
Q^{\bullet}
on mod
\ell
motivic cohomology, and a diagram as in Definition
2.9
, we have the following formula describing their failure to commute with proper pushforward:

Q^{\bullet}(f_{!}(\alpha))\cup td_{Q^{\bullet}}(TP^{\prime})=f_{!}(Q^{\bullet}(\alpha)\cup td_{Q^{\bullet}}(TP))

where
\alpha\in H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
.

Once one has this formula, one can mimic Brosnan’s approach, as in
[
13
]
and
[
8
]
, to define new operations that do commute with pushforward. One simply defines

U^{\bullet}(\alpha)=Q^{\bullet}(\alpha)\cup td_{Q^{\bullet}}(TP)

again for a smooth variety
P
with closed support
X
, and
\alpha\in H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
. Then, for diagrams as in Definition
2.9
, the operations
U^{\bullet}
commute due to the Riemann–Roch type formula.

We can now dualize these cohomology operations to obtain homology operations on the higher Chow groups of a (possibly singular) subspace. Of course, this is now purely formal. However, doing so sheds some information on how they relate to the embedding
X\hookrightarrow P
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed embedding of a (possibly singular) variety
X
in a smooth variety
P
. We define operations
U_{\bullet}
on the higher Chow groups of
X
by defining

U_{s}:=(\chi\circ i_{*})^{-1}\circ U^{s}\circ(\chi\circ i_{*})

where
\chi\circ i_{*}:H_{i}(X,j)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{2d-i}_{X}(P,d-j)
is the twisted Poincaré duality isomorphism of Theorem
2.2
. Similarly, for
\ell=p
, we can define

Q_{s}:=(\chi\circ i_{*})^{-1}\circ Q^{s}\circ(\chi\circ i_{*})

where
Q^{\bullet}
is defined for any prime
\ell
. Similarly, we write
P_{s}
as the dual motivic operations.

We note that the operations
Q_{s}
are defined for any prime
\ell
, but for
\ell=p
, we do not have a Riemann–Roch type formula that describes how they interact with proper pushforward. For
\ell\neq p
, and for diagrams as in Definition
2.9
, we see that the operations
U_{\bullet}
do commute with proper pushforward. We also note that
Q_{s}:H_{i}(X,j)\rightarrow H_{i-2s(\ell-1)}(X,\ell j-d(\ell-1))
where
d
is the dimension of the ambient space
P
.

Remark 3.8
.

As we are interested in the action of
Q_{\bullet}
on the fundamental class
[X]
, let us see what happens on higher Chow groups. Let
n
be the dimension of
X
and
d
be the dimension of
P
, and let
c=d-n
be the codimension. The class
[X]
lives in
H_{2n}(X,n)\cong CH_{n}(X,0)
, and so the operation
Q_{s}
sends
[X]
to the group
H_{2n-2s(\ell-1)}(X,d-\ell c)
. Rewriting this latter group in terms of higher Chow groups gives
CH_{d-\ell c}(X,2n+2\ell c-2s(\ell-1)-2d)
.

By definition of higher Chow groups, both indices need to be greater than
0
for the group to be a priori non-zero. The indices are greater than or equal to
0
if and only if
c\geq s
, and
d\geq\ell c
. This suggests the operations
Q_{s}
are only meaningful when the codimension is larger than the degree
s
, which is consistent with the cohomological vanishing for the simplicial Steenrod operations, since we are actually acting on the dual Thom class
\tau
which lives in
H^{2c}_{X}(P,c)
. That is, the operations are only a priori non-zero when
s
is less than or equal to the degree of the class it acts on.

On the other hand, the inequality
d\geq\ell c
is more strange. Rewriting
c=d-n
, we obtain the inequality

\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}\geq\frac{d}{n}

where again
d
is the dimension of the ambient smooth variety
P
, and
n
is the dimension of the possibly singular
X
, and
\ell
is the prime for which the operations are defined. This ratio must be satisfied by the embedding
X\hookrightarrow P
for the operations
U_{s}
to be a priori non-trivial. Thus, to find examples of singular varieties with embeddings where these operations are non-zero, one must consider embeddings into spaces that are not too large relative to the subspace.

We also must consider the vanishing theorems (Theorem 3.6 and Theorem 19.3 in
[
61
]
) that says that the motivic cohomology groups
H^{i,j}(X,A)
vanish for
X
smooth and
A
abelian, when
i>j+\textnormal{dim }X
and when
i>2j
. The inequality
i>2j
recovers the constraints on the degree
s
of our operations (i.e., that
s\leq c
where
c
is the codimension of an embedded variety
X
in a smooth variety
P
), but the inequality
i>j+\textnormal{dim }X
applied to our cohomology with support yields that the inequality

2s(\ell-1)\leq c(\ell-1)+n

must be satisfied for
s
for the operation to be a priori nontrivial, where
n
is the dimension of the subvariety
X
and
c
is the codimension of
X
in
P
. One can play around with numerics and see plenty of examples where such inequalities are satisfied, but they also seem to suggest that as the characteristic gets larger, the fewer possible a priori non-zero operations (and therefore fewer a priori obstructions) there are. This heuristically may suggest that resolution of singularities is true for generic characteristic.

3.3.3

Pushing forward the Bockstein

We include a small section here to show that we can always pushforward the Bockstein operation.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\beta
be the Bockstein operation and let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper map between smooth varieties. Then we have
f_{!}\beta(x)=\beta f_{!}(x)
for any
x\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
.

Proof.

We write our given proper map into a composition
f=\pi\circ i
of a closed embedding and a projection; by Proposition
3.4
the choice of decomposition does not matter. We check each case individually.

The case of a closed embedding:
For a closed embedding
i
, recall by Proposition
3.7
that we have
i_{!}(x)=\varphi\circ(\pi^{*}(x)\cup\tau)
, where
\varphi
are natural maps from the deformation to the normal cone,
\pi^{*}
is the pull-back induced by the projection from the normal bundle, and
\tau
is the Thom class of the normal bundle. So we check

\displaystyle\beta i_{!}(x)=

\displaystyle\ \beta(\varphi\circ(\pi^{*}(x)\cup\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varphi(\beta(\pi^{*}(x)\cup\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varphi(\beta(\pi^{*}(x))\cup\tau+\pi^{*}(x)\cup\beta(\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varphi(\pi^{*}(\beta x)\cup\tau)=i_{!}(\beta x)

where we have used that
\beta
is a derivation, and that it vanishes on the Thom class
\tau
since
\tau
is an integral class. That is,
\tau
is defined with integral coefficients, and so one can reduce it mod
\ell^{2}
for any
\ell
, and so from the long exact sequence on cohomology that defines the Bockstein,
\tau
is in the kernel of
\beta
.

The case of a projective bundle:
We have a projection
\pi:Y\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\rightarrow Y
that again sends elements
a\otimes t^{i}
to
a
if
i=n
, and
0
otherwise. So again we check

\displaystyle\pi_{!}(\beta(a\otimes t^{i}))=

\displaystyle\ \pi_{!}(\beta(a)\otimes t^{i}+a\otimes\beta(t^{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \pi_{!}(\beta(a)\otimes t^{i})

since
\beta
is a derivation, and vanishes on the integral class
t^{i}
.
∎

Once we know we can push forward the Bockstein, we will also abuse notation and write
\beta
to denote its action on the homology of a subspace
X\hookrightarrow P
, again by pre- and post-composing with Poincaré duality. This yields a map
\beta:H_{i}(X,j)\rightarrow H_{i-1}(X,j)
.

The following proposition is so trivial, we almost did not mention it. However, we present it to demonstrate explicitly why we cannot utilize the motivic operations to define Thom obstructions, as mentioned in Remark
3.1
. See also Theorem 8.4 in
[
81
]
.

Proposition 3.8
.

The motivic operations
\beta P_{s}
vanish on
[X]
for any closed subvariety
X\hookrightarrow P
.

Proof.

The operations
P_{s}
send an element of bidegree
(i,j)
to bidegree
(i-2s(\ell-1),j-s(\ell-1))
, where again
\ell
is any prime. The fundamental class of a variety
[X]
is in bidegree
(2n,n)
and so
\beta P_{s}[X]
lives in bidegree
(2n-2s(\ell-1)-1,n-s(\ell-1))
. In terms of higher Chow groups, this lives in the group
CH_{j-s(\ell-1)}(X,2n-2s(\ell-1)-1-(2n-2s(\ell-1)))=CH_{j-s(\ell-1)}(X,-1)=0
. That is, the group vanishes for trivial degree reasons.
∎

4

Embedding bundles and classifying maps

This is the first section of the paper which contains anything arguably new. We discuss a trick due to Deligne and Sullivan
[
20
]
that allows us to build a “derived classifying map” without leaving the category of finite dimensional smooth varieties. That is, we do not have to consider ind- or simplicial schemes, or sheaves of spaces in appropriate topologies, or classifying stacks, etc. We will see that smoothness of a closed subvariety
X
inside a smooth variety
P
gives us a normal bundle, and analyze how to make statements about its Thom class using an appropriately large dimensional Grassmannian. The Deligne–Sullivan trick is to pass to an auxiliary bundle, which does admit a classifying map to this Grassmannian, but is roughly homotopy equivalent to the base space. It is not so much a trick, as it is an observation about the construction of the classifying map.

We note that the argument here could be done using the theory of algebraic stacks, or by passing to the category of sheaves of simplicial sets, etc., but we have not investigated this point. The reason for this is twofold: one, we would like to stay in the category of varieties as long as possible, and two, the author needs to learn the theory of algebraic stacks. See also the relevant article
[
2
]
.

4.1

The Deligne–Sullivan trick for algebraic classifying maps

To reiterate the introduction of the section, recall that classifying maps for vector bundles do not exist in the category of varieties. However, the argument in
[
20
]
observes that the construction of the classifying map is completely algebraic, except for the very last step in which one uses a partition of unity to build a section to an auxiliary bundle. The auxiliary bundle here admits a classifying map tautologically, and has the same cohomology as the base space up to large degree. The auxiliary bundle is described below.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
E
be a vector bundle over a smooth variety
X
. Let
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
denote the
embedding bundle
, where a fiber over a point
x
is the set of linear embeddings
Emb(E_{x},\mathbb{A}^{N})
into a fixed vector space.

The bundle
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
is a fiber sub-bundle of the
Hom(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
vector bundle, where we abuse notation and write
\mathbb{A}^{N}
as the trivial rank
N
bundle over
X
. Suppose now that
E
has rank
m
. Notice that the fiber
Emb(E_{x},\mathbb{A}^{N})
can be identified with the variety of full rank
N\times m
matrices, which we denote by
W(N,m)
. That is,
W(N,m)
is the open subvariety of
\mathbb{A}^{Nm}
determined by the non-vanishing of at least one
m\times m
-minor (i.e.,
W(N,m)
is quasi-affine). We shall refer to varieties of the form
W(N,m)
as
Stiefel varieties
; without loss of generality, we assume
m\leq N
.

The point now is that Stiefel varieties have cohomology that vanish up to large degree; as a result, the embedding bundle and base space have roughly the same cohomology up to large degree. To prove this, we can employ the same techniques as in the work of Williams
[
86
]
. There, Williams determines the exact structure of the motivic cohomology of a Stiefel variety (see Proposition 5 in
[
86
]
); however, all we really require is cohomological vanishing for large bidegrees.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
W(N,m)
be the Stiefel variety of
N\times m
-matrices of full rank. Let
A
be a bi-graded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov; suppose the boundary operator
\partial
in the localization sequence increases bidegree by
(1,0)
, and suppose
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
. Then
A^{i,j}(W(N,m))
vanishes for
0<i<2(N-(m-1))-1
.

Proof.

For
N=1
, there is only the case when
m=1
. But then
W(N,m)=\mathbb{A}^{1}-\{0\}
so by localization, homotopy invariance, and purity, we have that
A^{i,j}(W(N,m))\cong A^{i-1,j-1}(\textnormal{Spec }k)
.

We now assume
N\geq 2
. The proof is by induction on
m
. Again if
m=1
, then we have that
W(N,m)=\mathbb{A}^{N}-\{0\}
. Again by localization, homotopy invariance, and purity, the boundary map yields an isomorphism
A^{i,j}(W(N,m))\xrightarrow{\partial}A^{i-(2N-1),j-N}(\textnormal{Spec }k)
. Thus, we obtain
A^{i,j}(W(N,m))=0
for degrees
0<i<2N-1=2(N-(m-1))-1
.

Now we consider
W(N,m-1)
. The Stiefel variety
W(N,m-1)
is a dense open set of
\mathbb{A}^{Nm}
and for
m<N
, we have a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{N}
bundle
E=W(N,m-1)\times\mathbb{A}^{N}
over
W(N,m-1)
as follows: over a matrix
A
, the fiber is all
N\times m
-matrices whose first
m-1
columns are given by
A
. That is, the fiber is all matrices

\begin{pmatrix}&&&v_{1}\\
&A&&\vdots\\
&&&v_{N}\\
\end{pmatrix}

where the vector
(v_{1},...,v_{N})
is allowed to vary. Similarly, there is also a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{m-1}
sub-bundle
F=W(N,m-1)\times\mathbb{A}^{m-1}
of
E
over
W(N,m-1)
, where over a matrix
A
we have the set of
N\times m
-matrices where the first
m-1
columns are given by
A
, and we require that
(v_{1},...,v_{N})
is in the column space (image) of
A
.

The total space
F
is a closed smooth subvariety of
E
, so we can apply the localization sequence to obtain

\cdots\rightarrow A^{i,j}(E,E-F)\rightarrow A^{i,j}(E)\rightarrow A^{i,j}(E-F)\xrightarrow{\partial}\cdots

which we can rewrite as

\cdots\rightarrow A^{i-2(N-(m-1)),j-(N-(m-1))}(F)\rightarrow A^{i,j}(W(N,m-1))\rightarrow A^{i,j}(W(N,m))\xrightarrow{\partial}\cdots

where the first factor is by purity and localization since
F
is smooth, the second factor is by homotopy invariance since
E
is a vector bundle, and the last factor is by the fact that
E-F
consists of
N\times m
-matrices where the last column is linearly independent from the first
m-1
; i.e., it is exactly the
N\times m
-matrices of rank
m
. Moreover, by homotopy invariance, and since
F
is a vector bundle, we have that the first term is also isomorphic to the cohomology of
W(N,m-1)
. In other words, we obtain an exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow A^{i,j}(W(N,m-1))\rightarrow A^{i,j}(W(N,m))\xrightarrow{\partial}A^{i-2(N-(m-1))+1,j-(N-(m-1))}(W(N,m-1))\rightarrow\cdots

and by induction, we have that
A^{i,j}(W(N,m-1))
is zero for
i<2(N-(m-2))-1
. If
i<2(N-(m-1))-1
, then the terms involving
W(N,m-1)
to the left and right of the
W(N,m)
vanish. The left term vanishes by the inductive step, and the right term vanishes as the index becomes negative. By the assumptions on the field, we can again show by induction that any negative degree cohomology for
W(N,m)
automatically vanishes. Thus, we conclude that
A^{i,j}(W(N,m)
also vanishes for
i<2(N-(m-1))-1
; observe that the larger the dimension
m
, the less number of groups we can say vanish. Of course if
m
is fixed and
N
is made larger, more groups vanish. Notice also that the degree
j
is irrelevant.
∎

We now introduce one additional axiom we would like for our ring cohomology theory to satisfy. It is exactly that it is given by the cohomology of some appropriate complex of sheaves. This way, we can freely use the tools of sheaf theory, such as the Leray spectral sequence and hypercohomology spectral sequence.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
A
be a bi-graded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov. We say
A
is a
sheaf cohomology theory
if for every smooth variety
X
there exists a cohomologically graded bi-complex of sheaves (with respect to some Grothendieck topology) of abelian groups
\mathcal{A}
whose sheaf hypercohomology computes
A
. That is,
H^{i}(X,\mathcal{A}^{j})\cong A^{i,j}(X)
, where the left hand side is understood to mean the
i
-th hypercohomology of the complex of sheaves
\mathcal{A}^{j}
.

Similarly, if
A
is only
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, we say it is a sheaf cohomology theory if there is a complex of sheaves of abelian groups whose hypercohomology computes
A
.

Moreover, we require the assignment of sheaves to be contravariant with respect to flat morphisms; i.e., if
f:Y\rightarrow X
is a flat morphism between smooth varieties, then there is an induced map of complexes of sheaves
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y}
for every
j
.

As examples, we have that motivic cohomology is computed by the Zariski or Nisnevich sheaf hypercohomology of the motivic complexes, and that étale cohomology is computed by the étale sheaf cohomology of the constant sheaf. The former is a bi-graded example, with
\mathcal{A}^{i}=\mathbb{Z}(i)
, and the latter is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded one.

Lemma 4.2
.

(Zariski descent)
Let
A
be a ring cohomology theory that is a sheaf cohomology theory. Then
A
satisfies Zariski descent; i.e., the hypercohomology of
\mathcal{A}
is computed by the cohomology of its global sections.

Proof.

Since
A
is a ring cohomology theory in the sense of of Panin–Smirnov, it satisfies localization. As in the proof of Lemma
2.1
on smooth finite type varieties, this implies the complexes of sheaves satisfy the Mayer–Vietoris property. Zariski descent then follows from Thomason’s criterion.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
Y\rightarrow X
be a fiber bundle. The stalk of the pushforward
\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}^{i}_{Y}
over a point
x
is given by
A^{i}(Y_{x})
, where
Y_{x}
denotes the fiber over
x
.

Proof.

We will prove that
A^{i}(Y_{x})
satisfies the universal property of a direct limit for the directed system in the definition of a stalk. There are natural maps
A^{i}(Y_{x})\rightarrow A^{i}(U\times Y_{x})
induced by the projection
U\times Y_{x}\rightarrow Y_{x}
from the fiber bundle trivializations, for every open
U
containing
x
. However, there are also canonical sections
Y_{x}\rightarrow U\times Y_{x}
since we have specified a point
x
; this induces maps
A^{i}(U\times Y_{x})\rightarrow A^{i}(Y_{x})
, for every open
U
containing
x
. Moreover, both collections of maps are compatible with the directed system of inclusions. The collection of maps given by the sections then induce a unique map from the direct limit to
A^{i}(Y_{x})
, and its inverse is given by the collection of maps given by the natural projections.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})\xrightarrow{\pi}X
denote the fiber bundle projection map to smooth
X
for a rank
m
bundle
E\rightarrow X
. Let
A
denote a cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov (bi-graded or
\mathbb{Z}
-graded). Suppose
A
is a sheaf cohomology theory with
\mathcal{A}
a sheaf of modules over a field; suppose moreover that
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
. Then the projection map
\pi
induces an isomorphism
A^{i,j}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}A^{i,j}(Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N}))
on cohomology for
i<2(N-(m-1))-1
.

Proof.

We will prove the bi-graded case (as the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded case is identical and simpler). Let
\mathcal{A}
denote an associated bi-complex of sheaves whose hypercohomology computes
A
, with the term
\mathcal{A}^{i}
understood to be a complex of sheaves. Let us assume also that
\mathcal{A}
is bounded below. For a space
X
, we write
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X}
to denote the complex of sheaves defined on the space
X
. For notational convenience, let us write
Y=Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
.

For each
j
, we can now use the Leray spectral sequence, which has terms on its
E_{2}
page given at index
(p,q)
by
H^{p}(X,R^{q}\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j})
. The spectral sequence converges to
H^{p+q}(Y,\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j})
so it suffices to compute the complex of sheaves
R^{q}\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j}
. Here, the cohomology of
H^{p+q}(Y,\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y})
is the hypercohomology of the complex of sheaves
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y}
.

Since we are assuming the theory
A
takes values in vector spaces over a field, every module is injective. So the complex
R\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j}
can be computed as
\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j}
, since
\mathcal{A}_{Y}^{j}
already consists of injective modules. Now, since the projection
\pi
is flat, there is a map of complexes of sheaves
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X}\rightarrow\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y}
. We claim this is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves, up to degree
q<2(N-(m-1))-1.

Since taking stalks is exact, taking cohomology of the complex of sheaves commutes with taking stalks. Thus, it suffices to check the quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves on the level of stalks. The stalk of
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X}
at a point
x
is given by the complex of abelian groups
A^{j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)
. On the other hand, the stalk of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y}
is given by
A^{j}(Y_{x})
by Lemma
4.3
. But as proven in Lemma
4.1
, the map on stalks is a quasi-isomorphism up to degree
2(N-(m-1))-1
; in particular,
A^{j}(Y_{x})
vanishes for
q>0
until
q=2(N-(m-1))-1
.

Thus, for
q
up to sufficiently large degree, we have that
R^{q}\pi_{*}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{Y}\cong R^{q}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X}
. But now we have the hypercohomology spectral sequence, which has terms on its
E_{2}
page given at index
(p,q)
by
H^{p}(X,R^{q}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X})
, which converges to the hypercohomology
H^{p+q}(X,\mathcal{A}^{j}_{X})
. Thus we obtain an isomorphism between Leray and hypercohomology spectral sequences for
q
up to degree
2(N-(m-1))-1
, induced by the projection
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
. Thus we get the isomorphism
A^{i,j}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}}A^{i,j}(Y)
where we can take
i=p+q=0+q
for
q<2(N-(m-1))-1
.
∎

Now, given a bundle
E\rightarrow X
, we form the embedding bundle
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})\xrightarrow{\pi}X
for some choice of sufficiently large
N
. We can pull back the bundle
E
over
X
by the projection
\pi
, and obtain a bundle
\pi^{*}E
over
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
. There is then a tautological map
Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})\rightarrow Gr(m,N)
given by sending an embedding
f
to its image
f(E_{x})
, which classifies the pullback bundle
\pi^{*}E
; that is, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr(m,N)}

where
\gamma^{m}
denotes the tautological bundle of
m
-dimensional subspaces over
Gr(m,N)
.

We also have the pull back diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

We will use these two diagrams in the following way: we will use the first diagram to obtain statements about cohomology of the embedding bundle with respect to cohomology of the Grassmannian. Then, using Lemma
4.4
, we will pass these statements to the cohomology of the base space
X
. Notice that it does not matter which dimension
N
we choose; there are maps to different Grassmannians for different
N
, which then commute with the natural maps between the Grassmannians. For our purposes, it suffices to choose
N
sufficiently large. Finally, for a smooth variety
X\hookrightarrow P
embedded in a smooth variety
P
, we will use the isomorphisms
A_{X}(N_{X}P)\cong A_{X}(P)
given by the deformation to the normal cone (as in Theorem
3.8
) to relate statements about cohomology of the Thom space of the normal bundle to statements about cohomology of the pair
(P,P-X)
.

The usual construction of a classifying map in topology involves this entire construction with the two diagrams above, which is all algebraic up until the last step, where one must choose a partition of unity to build a section
X\rightarrow Emb(E,\mathbb{A}^{N})
. Composing this section with the tautological classifying map yields the classifying map for the bundle
E\rightarrow X
.

Remark 4.1
.

For the reader who is interested in generalizing the Deligne–Sullivan argument to cohomology theories that do not satisfy
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, we note the following. There is a natural free
GL(m)
-action on
W(N,m)
given by pre-composing an embedding
f
with an element of
GL(m)
. The orbit space with respect to this action is exactly
Gr(m,N)
and the quotient map is precisely the tautological map sending an embedding
f
to its image
f(\mathbb{A}^{m})
in
\mathbb{A}^{N}
. Thus, to handle the cohomology of
W(N,m)
, one must handle the cohomology of
GL(m)
. Of course there is an action of
GL(m)
on the full flag variety
Fl(m)
, which itself is an iterated fibration of projective spaces. The isotropy subgroup of
GL(m)
of this action on
Fl(m)
are the invertible upper triangular matrices
B_{m}
, which can be identified as a product of
\mathbb{A}^{\binom{m}{2}}
and
(\mathbb{A}^{1}-0)^{m}
. The projective bundle formula should allow one to control the cohomology up to this point; here, one must handle the cohomology of affine space and the multiplicative group of units somehow.

4.2

Cohomology of a finite Grassmannian

In this section, we will analyze the cohomological structure of a finite dimensional Grassmannian. The arguments here are standard in algebraic geometry. We present the weakest form necessary for our result. See also Lemmas 5, 6, and 7 in
[
49
]
, and Example 14.6.2 in
[
29
]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
A
be a bigraded cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov. Assume
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
, and let
Gr(m,N)
denote the Grassmannian of
m
-dimensional subspaces in
\mathbb{A}^{N}
. Then
A^{i,j}(Gr(m,N))=0
when
i
is odd.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
and
N
. If
m=1
, then
Gr(m,N)=\mathbb{P}^{N}
, and by the assumption on our field
k
and by the projective bundle theorem, the result follows.

Suppose we have the result for all
m\leq N
. Write
\mathbb{A}^{N+1}=\mathbb{A}^{N}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Then there is a closed embedding
Gr(m,N)\hookrightarrow Gr(m+1,N+1)
which maps a subspace
C
to
C\oplus\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Let us write the complement of this embedding as
U
. By Lemma 7 in
[
49
]
, the open complement
U
is an affine bundle over
Gr(m+1,N)
. By the localization sequence, we then obtain

\cdots\rightarrow A^{i-2c,j-c}(Gr(m,N))\rightarrow A^{i,j}(Gr(m+1,N+1))\rightarrow A^{i,j}(U)\rightarrow\cdots

where
c
is the codimension of
Gr(m,N)
in
Gr(m+1,N+1)
. Moreover, since
U
is an affine bundle, by homotopy invariance we can identify the last term with
A^{i,j}(Gr(m+1,N))
. By the inductive step, the two outer terms in the three term sequence above both vanish when
i
is odd. The result follows immediately.
∎

Of course, the Grassmannian
Gr(m,N)
has affine pavings and Schubert varieties that allow one to prove a much stronger theorem. Namely, using the projective bundle formula, one could deduce a Grassmannian bundle theorem as well. We do not do so here, since we do not require it for our motivic examples in mind. However, for other generalized theories, it may be helpful to use the Grassmannian bundle theorem to deduce further results on cohomology classes that arise as pull-backs from classes in the Grassmannian.

5

Thom obstructions to resolution of singularities

5.1

Vanishing for smooth varieties

We are now ready to present the motivic analog of the classical argument due to Thom, modified to be appropriately algebraic by Sullivan.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed embedding with
X
and
P
both smooth, over an algebraically closed field. Let
Q^{\bullet}
be the simplicial Steenrod operations on
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
, where
\ell
is any prime, and let
Q_{\bullet}
denote the dual operations on
H_{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
. The odd degree operations
\beta Q_{s}
vanish on the fundamental class
[X]
.

Proof.

The statement is equivalent to the odd degree operations
\beta Q^{s}
vanishing on the mod
\ell
dual class
\tau\in H^{2c}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(c))
, where
c
is the codimension of
X
in
P
. By deformation to the normal cone, we have a natural isomorphism
H^{*}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))\cong H^{*}(N_{X}P,N_{X}P-X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
where
N_{X}P
denotes the normal bundle of
X
in
P
. Moreover, as the maps in the deformation to the normal cone are all between smooth varieties, the operations
\beta Q^{s}
commute with the natural isomorphism above.

We now let
E
denote the embedding bundle
Emb(N_{X}P,\mathbb{A}^{N})\xrightarrow{\pi}X
for some sufficiently large
N
. Let
\pi^{*}N_{X}P
denote the pullback of the normal bundle. Lemma
4.4
implies we have a natural isomorphism
H^{*}(N_{X}P,N_{X}P-X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))\cong H^{*}(\pi^{*}N_{X}P,\pi^{*}N_{X}P-E,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
for large degree, since
H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))\cong H^{*}(E,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
for large degree, and since the Thom isomorphism is functorial. In particular, the dual class
\tau
of
X
in
P
is naturally mapped to the Thom class
\tau
of the normal bundle, which in turn is naturally mapped to the Thom class
\tilde{\tau}
of the pullback bundle over
E
.

Now we have the classifying map diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}N_{X}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Emb(N_{X}P,\mathbb{A}^{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr(c,N)}

and again by functoriality of the Thom isomorphism, we have that there is a cohomology class
\gamma
in
H^{*}(\gamma^{c},\gamma^{c}-Gr(c,N),\mathbb{Z}/\ell(*))
that is mapped to
\tilde{\tau}
. Specifically,
\gamma
is in bidegree
(2c,c)
, where
c
is the codimension of
X
in
P
. The operations
\beta Q^{s}
then vanish on
\gamma
since
Q^{s}
keeps the first index even, but
\beta
increases it by
1
. Since
Gr(c,N)
has vanishing cohomology when the first index is odd, we then have
\beta Q^{s}(\gamma)=0
. By naturality of the operations, these pullback to give that
\beta Q^{s}(\tilde{\tau})=0
. By the isomorphism given by the Deligne–Sullivan trick, we have that
\beta Q^{s}(\tau)=0
. Passing through deformation to the normal cone, we conclude that
\beta Q^{s}
vanish on the dual class of
X
, and so the dual homology operations vanish on
[X]
.
∎

Let us present the version for generalized cohomology theory. The proof is exactly the same.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed embedding with
X
and
P
both smooth. Let
A
be a bigraded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov, that is given by a sheaf cohomology theory valued in modules over a field, and is equipped with a proper pushforward. Suppose
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)
vanishes for
i\neq 0
. Let
\phi=\phi^{\bullet}
be a ring operation that preserves even degrees (in the first index) and let
\beta
be a natural operation that sends bidegrees
(i,j)
to
(i+1,j)
. The operation
\beta\phi^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau\in A^{2c,c}_{X}(P)
, where
c
is the codimension of
X
in
P
.

5.2

Vanishing for singular varieties with a resolution

We have seen a vanishing result for smooth varieties. We now extend the result to singular varieties that admit a resolution of singularities. Recall given a ring operation
\phi
on a bi-graded ring cohomology theory
A
in the sense of Panin–Smirnov, where
\phi
has invertible Todd genus on
A(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})
, we can define a new operation
\tilde{\phi}
by

\tilde{\phi}(\alpha)=\phi(\alpha)\cup td_{\phi}(TP)

where
\alpha\in A_{X}(P)
. The operation
\tilde{\phi}
commutes with proper pushforward by the Riemann–Roch type formula we have discussed in Section
3.3
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
A
be a bigraded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov, that is given by a sheaf cohomology theory valued in modules over a field, and is equipped with a proper pushforward. Suppose
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
. Let
\phi=\phi^{\bullet}
be a ring operation with well-defined Todd genus that preserves even degrees in the first index, and let
\beta
be a natural operation that sends bidegrees
(i,j)
to
(i+1,j)
that commutes with proper pushforward. Moreover, assume
\beta
is a derivation that vanishes on the Todd class of any bundle. Let
X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow P^{\prime}
be a singular variety with a closed embedding into a smooth variety
P^{\prime}
. Suppose the pair
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
admits an embedded resolution of singularities. The operation
\beta\tilde{\phi}^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau^{\prime}\in A^{2c,c}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime})
where
c
is the codimension of
X^{\prime}
in
P^{\prime}
, and where
\tilde{\phi}
is the operation associated to
\phi
that commutes with proper pushforward.

Proof.

An embedded resolution of singularities is a diagram of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

where
X
and
P
are smooth, and the maps
f
and
f^{\prime}
are proper, with
f^{\prime}
a birational isomorphism. By Theorem
5.2
, the operation
\beta\phi
vanishes on
\tau\in A^{2c,c}_{X}(P)
the dual class of
X
in
P
. We then see that

\beta\tilde{\phi}(\tau)=\beta(\phi(\tau)\cup td_{\phi}(TP))=\beta\phi(\tau)\cup td_{\phi}(TP)+\phi(\tau)\cup\beta td_{\phi}(TP)=0

since
\beta\phi(\tau)=0
and by our assumptions on
\beta
. We have that the proper pushforward
f_{!}
maps
\tau
to
\tau^{\prime}
, since
f
is generically degree
1
and by functoriality of the Thom structure. Thus, we conclude that

\beta\tilde{\phi}(\tau^{\prime})=\beta\tilde{\phi}(f_{!}(\tau))=f_{!}(\beta\tilde{\phi}(\tau))=0

since all operations above commute with proper pushforward.
∎

Thus, if
X^{\prime}
is a singular variety with closed embedding into a smooth variety
P^{\prime}
, and
X^{\prime}
admits an embedded resolution of singularities, then the mod
\ell\neq p
operations
U_{\bullet}
from Definition
3.12
vanish on its fundamental class
[X^{\prime}]
. At first, this may seem like a necessary condition for a variety to admit a resolution of singularities; however, we will see later that resolution of singularities is not needed to prove this vanishing result. Unfortunately, all the examples the author knows (such as motivic mod
\ell\neq p
, and étale mod
\ell\neq p
), vanishes for other a priori reasons other than resolution of singularities.

Let us see what happens for the operations
Q^{\bullet}
defined mod
\ell=p
. Suppose we have an embedded resolution of singularities
f:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}
, with a diagram as in Definition
2.9
. We can again write our proper map
f
as a composition
f=\pi\circ i
where
i
is a closed embedding
i=f\times e:P\rightarrow P^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
, where
e
is some embedding of
P
into
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. We again write the dual class of
X
in
P
as
\tau
, and the dual class of
X^{\prime}
in
P^{\prime}
as
\tau^{\prime}
. Since the map
f
is generically degree
1
, we have that
f_{*}[X]=[X^{\prime}]
, and so
f_{!}(\tau)=\tau^{\prime}
. We also have
i_{!}(\tau)=(f\times e)_{!}(\tau)=\tau^{\prime}\otimes t^{n}
, by definition of proper pushforward for higher Chow groups.

By Theorem
5.2
, we have that
\beta Q^{\bullet}(\tau)=0
. On one hand, we have that

\beta Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})=\beta Q^{\bullet}(f_{!}(\tau))=\beta Q^{\bullet}(\pi_{!}\circ i_{!})(\tau)=\beta Q^{\bullet}(\pi_{!}(\tau^{\prime}\otimes t^{n}))

and we would like to commute
\beta Q^{\bullet}
with the proper pushforward
\pi_{!}
from the projective bundle
P^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}\xrightarrow{\pi}P^{\prime}
. But on the other hand,

\pi_{!}(\beta Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime}\otimes t^{n}))=\pi_{!}(\beta(Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})\otimes Q^{\bullet}(t^{n})))=0

since
\beta(Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})\otimes Q^{\bullet}(t^{n}))=\beta Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})\otimes Q^{\bullet}(t^{n})+Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})\otimes\beta Q^{\bullet}(t^{n})
as
\beta
is a derivation, and
Q^{\bullet}(t^{n})=0
for degree reasons (since
Q^{0}
mod
p
is not the identity!). Thus, we unfortunately cannot say anything about
\beta Q^{\bullet}(\tau^{\prime})
for singular varieties
X^{\prime}
in smooth
P^{\prime}
, as the operation
Q^{\bullet}
does not have a well-defined Todd genus. Recall that the argument for mod
\ell\neq p
worked since one could check that
Q^{0}
on the cohomological generator
t
of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
was indeed the identity, and so all formulas apply.

6

Vanishing of mod
\ell\neq p
obstructions

In this section we show the mod
\ell\neq p
obstructions we have defined are not obstructions to resolution of singularities at all. That is, they vanish without using resolution of singularities.

Theorem 6.1
.

The mod
\ell\neq p
operations
\beta U_{\bullet}
from Definition
3.12
vanish on
[X^{\prime}]
for a singular variety
X^{\prime}
with closed embedding into a smooth variety
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Every singular variety
X^{\prime}
admits an alteration
X
by work of de Jong
[
19
]
. Moreover, by work of Temkin
[
75
]
, one can find an alteration
X
of degree equal to powers of
p
, the characteristic. Let us now assume
X^{\prime}
has an embedded alteration
X
where the map
f
has degree equal to
p^{m}
for some
m
.

By Theorem
5.2
, the operations
\beta U_{\bullet}
vanish on
[X]
. Then we have that

0=f_{!}(\beta U_{\bullet}[X])=\beta U_{\bullet}f_{!}([X])=p^{m}\beta U_{\bullet}[X^{\prime}]

but since the operations are defined mod
\ell\neq p
, we have that
p^{m}
is invertible. So we conclude
\beta U_{\bullet}[X^{\prime}]=0
.
∎

The above argument then proves the following modification of Theorem
5.3
. Essentially, if the cohomology theory
A
takes values mod
\ell\neq p
, then we can drop the assumption on the singular variety
X^{\prime}
having a resolution of singularities.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
A
be a bigraded ring cohomology theory in the sense of Panin–Smirnov, that is given by a sheaf cohomology theory valued in modules over
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
for
\ell\neq p
, and is equipped with a proper pushforward. Suppose
A^{i,j}(\textnormal{Spec }k)=0
for
i\neq 0
. Let
\phi=\phi^{\bullet}
be a ring operation with well-defined Todd genus that preserves even degrees in the first index, and let
\beta
be a natural operation that sends bidegrees
(i,j)
to
(i+1,j)
that commutes with proper pushforward. Moreover, assume
\beta
is a derivation that vanishes on the Todd class of any bundle. Let
X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow P^{\prime}
be a singular variety with a closed embedding into a smooth variety
P^{\prime}
. The operation
\beta\tilde{\phi}^{s}
vanishes on the dual class
\tau^{\prime}\in A^{2c,c}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime})
where
c
is the codimension of
X^{\prime}
in
P^{\prime}
, and where
\tilde{\phi}
is the operation associated to
\phi
that commutes with proper pushforward.

In other words, the mod
\ell\neq p
simplicial operations on motivic and étale cohomology all vanish, without using resolution of singularities. In fact, the mod
\ell\neq p
simplicial operations vanish for simpler reasons without even using alterations, by the following theorem of Brosnan and Joshua (Proposition 6.1 in
[
8
]
).

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
k
have a primitive
\ell
-th root of unity, for
\ell\neq p
. Let
X
be a variety with closed embedding into a smooth variety
P
. Then

Q^{s}(\alpha)=B^{(q-s)(\ell-1)}P^{s}(\alpha),\beta Q^{s}(\alpha)=B^{(q-s)(\ell-1)}\beta P^{s}(\alpha)

for
s\leq q
, where
B
denotes the motivic Bott element from section
2.3
, and where
P^{s}
denotes the motivic operations, for
\alpha\in H^{i}_{X}(P,\mathbb{Z}/\ell(q))
, with
i\leq 2q
. For
s>q
both operations are zero.

Recall that the motivic operations
\beta P^{\bullet}
vanish on fundamental classes of varieties for trivial degree reasons (Proposition
3.8
). And so the mod
\ell
simplicial operations vanish similarly, since they differ only by multiplication by an invertible element.

We also remark that the only mod
\ell=p
operations we know that do push forward are those given by the motivic operations
P^{\bullet}
. But of course they again vanish for trivial degree reasons (Proposition
3.8
).

7

Conjectures and counterexamples

We list some questions and suggest some further directions, aside from simply proving resolution of singularities. We have defined the mod
p
operations
\beta Q^{\bullet}
on the mod
p
motivic cohomology for a pair
(P,X)
where
X
is possibly singular and has a closed embedding into a smooth variety
P
. We have shown that the operations
\beta Q^{\bullet}
vanish on the dual class if
X
is smooth. Unfortunately, we could not push these operations forward. This leaves the following concrete question.

Question 7.1
.

Do the operations
\beta Q^{s}
vanish on the dual class of any variety
X
with closed embedding in smooth
P
? Does the vanishing depend on resolution of singularities? Do one of the arguments claiming to prove resolution of singularities actually show the operations vanish?

If one uses resolution of singularities to show these operations vanish, then the operations immediately become obstructions (either to a particular variety admitting a resolution, or the whole underlying field
k
admitting resolutions of singularities). Note that if we assume resolution over the whole field, then we are now able to freely use as many categorical constructions from the field of motivic cohomology as we would like. These constructions may then show these operations vanish, which would make them obstructions to resolution over a field.

The author has also learned from Jason Starr that the question of whether every variety admits a resolution up to birational equivalence is not known. That is: every variety is birational to a hypersurface (not necessarily smooth). After blowing up and blowing down, the question of whether one can resolve is still open. It is possible these operations vanish after blowing down, as geometrically blowing down would kill these homology obstructions.

It is also possible that some of the many arguments attempting to prove resolution may show these operations vanish but we have not checked this.

7.1

Higher order
p
-adic obstructions

Question 7.2
.

Can one use obstruction theory in the motivic setting to construct primary, secondary, etc. obstructions to representability of a cohomology class by a smooth variety?

The “obstructions” we have defined in this paper are not obstructions per se in the classical sense. That is, they are not obstruction theoretic classes whose vanishing is equivalent to representing a cohomology class by a manifold. One may now try to mimic Thom’s entire program on Steenrod’s problem in the characteristic
p
setting.

In the classical setting, the question of whether a given homology class was representable by a manifold was equivalent to the question of its Alexander–Lefschetz–Poincaré dual (with respect to some ambient space) being the pull-back of some cohomology class from the universal Thom space
MU
. For a given cohomology class to be the pull-back of some class from
MU
, one must bypass the obstructions to lifting a map to an Eilenberg–Maclane space to
MU
. These primary obstructions to lifting would then be obstructions to representability of a homology class. Bypassing these, one could then further hope to formulate secondary, tertiary, etc. obstructions.

7.2

Other cohomology theories

Question 7.3
.

Is there another cohomology theory satisfying the axioms of Panin–Smirnov for which obstructions can be defined? E.g., mod
p
algebraic
K
-theory? Can the axioms be relaxed?

Other cohomology theories satisfying
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance have the potential to formulate Thom obstructions, such as mod
p
algebraic
K
-theory, with the ring operation given by the Adams operations. However, we have not investigated this point, as that would have required the author to learn yet another cohomology theory from scratch. One could also try and find a different ring operation on motivic cohomology other than the Steenrod operations we have discussed.

There is a growing body of work in
p
-adic cohomology theories seeking to replace the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance property with the projective bundle formula. As many
p
-adic cohomology theories satisfy the projective bundle formula but not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance (e.g., de Rham, crystalline, prismatic, etc.) if the arguments in this paper were able to use the projective bundle formula in lieu of homotopy invariance, we would have more examples of theories where a priori obstructions could lie (the aforementioned theories all have Steenrod operations on them, again coming from
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures via the cosimplicial Godement resolution; see for example, work by Scavia
[
69
]
).

For example there is recent work of Annala, Hoyois, and Iwasa
[
4
]
that relaxes the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance condition and is still able to obtain the isomorphisms
A_{X}(P)\cong A_{X}(N_{X}P)
, where
A
is the given cohomology theory,
A_{X}(P)
is the cohomology with closed support, and
A_{X}(N_{X}P)
is the cohomology with support of the normal bundle.

There is also work of Déglise
[
17
]
that discusses proper pushforwards for general cohomology theories. One would hope to reproduce the Riemann–Roch type formulas we have discussed in this paper assuming only the projective bundle formula, instead of the full strength of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance and localization.

To that end, one could hope to obtain the isomorphisms relating the Thom space of the normal bundle with the cohomology of the ambient pair, and the Riemann–Roch type formulas for more general
p
-adic theories that only satisfy projective bundle formula and not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance. One only needs to then bypass the Deligne–Sullivan embedding bundle trick; one could either hope that the Stiefel variety is sufficiently linear algebraic enough that the trick still holds (see Remark
4.1
), or one could actually leave the category of varieties and pass to the category of sheaves of spaces. By doing so, one could work with a Grassmannian not in the category, but still obtain results on invariants related to the original space.

However, as mentioned in the introduction, it seems to go against the central ideas of Thom to omit the purity/Thom isomorphism theorems (which are based on localization and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance). That is, forsaking purity seems similar to forsaking the idea that “topology is local”, e.g., the topology of a subspace should not depend on its ambient neighborhood. Without
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, there is no local contractibility, and there is the possibility of infinite dimensional topology coming from the étale coverings of affine space.

7.3

Candidate counterexamples

Problem 7.1
.

Construct an example of a singular variety
X
with a closed embedding into a smooth variety
P
where the operations
\beta Q^{\bullet}
do not vanish on the dual class.

As seen in Remark
3.8
, one requires an embedding where the ratio of the dimension of the ambient space and the dimension of the subspace is not too large. A candidate counterexample suggested to the author by Sullivan is some variety that approximates an Eilenberg–Maclane space
K(\mathbb{Z}/p,n)
: from classical topology, such spaces admit many nontrivial cohomology operations. One could try to employ a Dold–Thom argument to construct such an example (e.g., a large but finite
p
-fold symmetric product). Finite
p
-power quotient singularities are not known to be resolvable. Assuming one can first show that the operations
\beta Q^{\bullet}
are obstructions to resolution of singularities, this example may serve as a counterexample.

Appendix A

Appendix

A.1

Algebras over operads and generalized Steenrod operations

In this section, we review basic definitions of operads, algebras over operads, and May’s construction in
[
57
]
of generalized Steenrod operations on the
\mathbb{Z}/\ell
cohomology of
E_{\infty}
-algebras for every prime
\ell
.

We consider cochain complexes over a fixed ground field
k
, which in our context we take as
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. We recall the definition of an operad.

Definition A.1
.

An
operad

\mathcal{O}
consists of a sequence
\mathcal{O}(n)
,
n\geq 0
of cochain complexes over
k
, with a unit map
\eta:k\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)
, a right action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
on
\mathcal{O}(n)
for each
n
, and maps (of cochain complexes)

\gamma:\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(n_{k})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(n_{1}+...+n_{k})

for
k\geq 1
and
n_{j}\geq 0
. The maps
\gamma
must satisfy the following diagrams.

a) (Associativity) We have the following commutative diagram, where
\sum_{s}j_{s}=j
and
\sum_{t}i_{t}=i
, with
g_{s}=j_{1}+...+j_{s}
and
h_{s}=i_{g_{s-1}+1}+...+i_{g_{s}}
for
1\leq s\leq k
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes(\bigotimes^{k}_{s=1}\mathcal{O}(j_{s}))\otimes(\bigotimes^{j}_{r=1}\mathcal{O}(i_{r}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes(\bigotimes^{j}_{r=1}\mathcal{O}(i_{r}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes(\bigotimes^{k}_{s=1}\mathcal{O}(j_{s})\otimes(\bigotimes^{j_{s}}_{q=1}\mathcal{O}(i_{g_{s}-1+q})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes(\bigotimes^{k}_{s=1}\mathcal{O}(h_{s}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes Id}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\otimes(\otimes_{s}\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

b) (Unital) The following unit diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes k^{\otimes n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes\mathcal{O}(1)^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\otimes\eta^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes\mathcal{O}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

c) (Equivariance) The following diagrams commute, where
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
,
\tau_{s}\in\Sigma_{j_{s}}
, and
\sigma(j_{1},...,j_{k})\in\Sigma_{k}
permutes
k
blocks of letters as
\sigma
permutes
k
letters, and
\tau_{1}\oplus...\oplus\tau_{k}\in\Sigma_{k}
is the block sum:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{\sigma(1)})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{\sigma(k)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\otimes\sigma^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(j_{\sigma(1)}\textnormal{,...,}j_{\sigma(k)})}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\otimes\tau_{1}\otimes...\otimes\tau_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}\oplus...\oplus\tau_{k}}

Operads parameterize
n
