-ary operations. Next we recall the definition of an algebra over an operad.

Definition A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad. An
algebra
A
over an operad
is a cochain complex with maps

\theta:\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}\rightarrow A

for every
n\geq 0
that are associative, unital, and equivariant in the following sense.

a) (Associativity) The following associativity diagrams commute, where
\sum j_{s}=j

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A^{\otimes j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes A^{\otimes j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes A^{\otimes j_{1}}...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A^{\otimes j_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes A^{\otimes k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes Id}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\otimes\theta^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

b) (Unital) The following unit diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(1)\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

c) (Equivariance) The following diagram commutes, for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{j}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes A^{\otimes j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes A^{\otimes j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\otimes\sigma^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

A
map of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras
is a map of complexes
f:A\rightarrow B
such that the operadic action commutes. That is, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes A^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes B^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id\otimes f^{\otimes n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

A
quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras
is a morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras that is a quasi-isomorphism of the underlying complexes.

Definition A.3
.

An operad
\mathcal{O}
is
unital
if
\mathcal{O}(0)=k
. If
\mathcal{O}
is unital, then there are augmentation maps
\epsilon=\gamma:\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes\mathcal{O}(0)^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(0)=k
. A unital operad
\mathcal{O}
is
acyclic
if the augmentations are quasi-isomorphisms for every
n
. If the unital operad
\mathcal{O}
is acyclic and
\Sigma_{n}
acts freely on
\mathcal{O}(n)
for every
n
, we say
\mathcal{O}
is an
E_{\infty}
-operad
. An
E_{\infty}
-algebra
is any algebra over an
E_{\infty}
-operad.

Definition A.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a unital operad. An
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with unit
is an algebra
A
equipped with a map
\eta_{A}:k=\mathcal{O}(0)\rightarrow A
which we call a
unit
. A
unit-preserving map
or
map of algebras with unit
is a map
f:A\rightarrow B
of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras such that
\eta_{B}=f\circ\eta_{A}
.

A warning: the unital property listed as an axiom in the definition of an operad and algebra over an operad is not to be confused with the existence of a unit. The unital property describes the identity operation, and is part of the definition for any operad, whereas the existence of a unit depends on the operad being unital.

The main importance of
E_{\infty}
-algebras in our setting is that their mod
p
cohomology has cohomology operations on it analogous to the Steenrod operations on the cohomology of spaces (where
p
is any prime). The work of May
[
57
]
defines generalized Steenrod operations satisfying the usual properties (such as functoriality, the Cartan formula, and Adem relations) for a category of objects
(K,\theta)
where roughly,
K
is a homotopy associative differential graded algebra and
\theta
describes the operad action
\theta_{p}:\mathcal{E}(p)\otimes A^{\otimes p}\rightarrow A
. In particular, May defines the operations with explicit formulas, which can be computed using the induced map on homology of the chain complexes
\theta_{*}:H_{*}(\mathcal{E}(p)\otimes_{\Sigma_{p}}A^{\otimes p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\rightarrow H_{*}(A,\mathbb{F}_{p})
. That is, the formulas for the operations themselves involve
\theta_{*}
, and it follows from computation that if
A
has an additional (Adams) grading to keep track of (such as the Tate twist index in motivic cohomology), then the Steenrod operations send elements
x
of Adams degree
r
to degree
pr
(as the operations involve the term
x^{\otimes p}
, which lives in degree
pr
).

We now state the following consequence of a theorem of Berger and Moerdijk (Theorem 3.5(c) in
[
11
]
). It states that quasi-isomorphisms of chain complexes transfer
E_{\infty}
-algebra structures, at least when our chain complexes consist of modules over a field.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
A\rightarrow B
be a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes over a field
k
, and suppose
A
(resp. B) is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra. Then there is an
E_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
B
(resp. A) such that
f
is a map of
E_{\infty}
-algebras.

To apply the theorem of Berger and Moerdijk, one needs to impose a model category structure on the category of chain complexes over a field. In Theorem
A.1
, we consider the usual projective model structure: cofibrations are monomorphisms whose cokernels are degree-wise projective modules, fibrations are degree-wise epimorphisms, and weak equivalences are quasi-isomorphisms. Since our complexes are over a field, all objects in our model category are cofibrant and fibrant. Moreover, the particular choice of
E_{\infty}
-operad does not matter; the homotopy categories of algebras over two distinct
E_{\infty}
-operads are equivalent (see Remark 4.6 in
[
11
]
).

We present some necessary definitions from
[
47
]
, which gives us a method of finding
E_{\infty}
-algebras in nature.

Definition A.5
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded module over
k
(i.e., a chain complex over
k
). A
domain

A_{*}
is a sequence of
\Sigma_{j}
-invariant submodules
A_{j}\subset A^{\otimes j}
such that the inclusions
\delta_{j}:A_{j}\hookrightarrow A^{\otimes j}
satisfy the following properties:

1.

\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{1}
are the identity maps, and each
\delta_{j}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

2.

For each partition
j=j_{1}+...+j_{k}
, the map
\delta_{j}
factors through
A_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes A_{j_{k}}
in the sense of the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\otimes j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes A^{\otimes j_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes A_{j_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{j}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes\delta_{j_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{j}}

Definition A.6
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad. A
partial
\mathcal{O}
-algebra

A
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
k
-module with a domain
A_{*}
with
\Sigma_{j}
-equivariant maps

\theta_{j}:\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes A_{j}\rightarrow A,j\geq 0

such that

1.

\theta_{1}(1\otimes a)=a
,

2.

the map

\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A_{j}\rightarrow A^{\otimes k}

(where
j=\sum j_{i}
) obtained by including
A_{j}
into
A_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes A_{j_{k}}
, shuffling, and applying
\theta_{k}
, factors through
A_{k}
, and

3.

the following associativity diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(j)\otimes A_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes A_{j_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{1})\otimes A_{j_{1}}\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{O}(j_{k})\otimes A_{j_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)\otimes A^{\otimes k}.\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\theta^{\otimes k}}

As mentioned in Remark II.2.6 in
[
47
]
, partial
E_{\infty}
-algebras admit Steenrod operations on their mod
p
cohomology as well: one constructs the map
\theta_{*}
by considering the diagram

\mathcal{E}(p)\otimes_{\Sigma_{p}}A^{\otimes p}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}(p)\otimes_{\Sigma_{p}}A_{p}\rightarrow A

where the left-facing arrow is induced by the inclusion
A_{p}\subset A^{\otimes p}
(and induces an isomorphism on homology; see Lemma II.3.1 in
[
47
]
) and the right-facing arrow is
\theta_{p}
given for the partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra. This then realizes a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra as an object
(A,\theta_{*})
of May’s category of pairs on which Steenrod operations are defined. Moreover, given a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra, there is a functorial way of converting it into a genuine
E_{\infty}
-algebra, via the theorem of Kriz and May below (Theorem 2.1.1 in
[
47
]
). We state the theorem in less generality than in the original context.

Theorem A.2
.

There is a functor
V
that assigns to every partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra
A
a quasi-isomorphic
E_{\infty}
-algebra
VA
. In particular, both
A
and
VA
have the same Steenrod operations.

Remark A.1
.

There is a big nuance here; a partial commutative algebra can have non-trivial Steenrod operations (despite the product being commutative). As we do for Bloch’s cycle complexes, a partial commutative algebra can be realized as a partial
E_{\infty}
-algebra via pull-back over a map of operads
\mathcal{E}\rightarrow Comm
. However, after pull-back, if one applies the above functor
V
, the resulting genuine
E_{\infty}
-algebra will not necessarily be quasi-isomorphic to a genuine commutative algebra. Moreover, the more general theorem does not apply to the
Comm
operad itself, as
Comm
does not admit free symmetric group actions. The Steenrod operations on a partial algebra arise from failure of the inclusion
A_{p}\subset A^{\otimes p}
being an equivariant homotopy equivalence (i.e., the homotopy itself is equivariant with respect to the
\Sigma_{p}
action, where the action is trivial on the interval; see the remarks before Theorem 1.3 in
[
25
]
).

A.2

Cup product in hypercohomology

We recall basic sheaf theory and cup products on hypercohomology, following “Cycle” in
[
18
]
or Tag 0FKU in
[
71
]
. Given any two abelian sheaves
F
and
G
, and any two closed subschemes
A\xhookrightarrow{i}X
and
B\xhookrightarrow{j}X
, we have a natural map

\Gamma_{A}(X,F)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{B}(X,G)\rightarrow\Gamma_{A\cap B}(X,F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)

where
\Gamma_{\bullet}
denotes the sections with support on the appropriate closed subscheme, and the map sends
\alpha\otimes\beta
to the product section
\alpha\times\beta
. This in turn induces a map in the derived category
i_{*}Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}j_{*}Rj^{!}G\rightarrow k_{*}Rk^{!}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)
, where
A\cap B\xhookrightarrow{k}X
is the closed immersion for the intersection. This induces on hypercohomology the map
H_{A}(X,F)\otimes H_{B}(X,G)\xrightarrow{\cup}H_{A\cap B}(X,F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)
. This is the
cup product
on sheaf (hyper)cohomology.

Returning to our situation, we can take our sheaves to be Bloch’s cycle complexes, with
F=\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)
and
G=\mathbb{Z}_{X}(m)
. The external product on the cycle complexes induces a product
\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{X}(m)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{X}(n+m)
, which in turn yields the cup product on motivic cohomology
H^{i}(X,j)\otimes H^{r}(X,s)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{i+r}(X,j+s)
for smooth
X
. Thus, for smooth varieties we have that
H^{*}(X,*)=\oplus_{i,j}H^{i}(X,j)
forms a graded commutative ring. By Zariski descent, this agrees with the cup product structure of Proposition
2.2
. Moreover, for maps between smooth varieties
f:X\rightarrow Y
, the induced pullback map
f^{*}
is a ring homomorphism, and agrees with the ring homomorphism we defined in Proposition
2.3
.

We investigate the cup product structure a bit more closely for the case of cohomology with support. This will allow us to extend many of the results of section
2.1.1
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion into a smooth variety
P
, and let
F
be an abelian sheaf on
P
. Let
\underline{\Gamma}_{X}F
denote the kernel sheaf of the natural map
F\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}F
, where
j
is the complementary open immersion. Recall the functor
i^{!}
is defined as the composition of functors
i^{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X}
, and that its right derived functor agrees with the usual functor
Ri^{!}
that is right adjoint to
i_{!}=i_{*}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose we have a map
f:P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
, closed immersions
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
and
X^{\prime}=f^{-1}(X)\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
, and a Cartesian diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}-X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P-X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
j
and
j^{\prime}
are the complementary open immersions. Then
R\underline{\Gamma}_{X}Rf_{*}\cong Rf_{*}R\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We have that, for any abelian sheaf
F
, the kernel
\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}F
fits into the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}F\rightarrow F\rightarrow j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}F\rightarrow 0

by definition. The functor
f_{*}
is left exact, so applying it to the above sequence yields

0\rightarrow f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}F\rightarrow f_{*}F\rightarrow f_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}F.

On the other hand, we have the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}_{X}f_{*}F\rightarrow f_{*}F\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}f_{*}F\rightarrow 0.

By commutativity of the diagram, we have
f_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}=j_{*}f^{\prime}_{*}
. By adjunction, we obtain a base change map
j^{*}f_{*}F\rightarrow f^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}F
. Checking the base change map on stalks shows it is an isomorphism:
(j^{*}f_{*}A)_{x}=\varinjlim_{x\in V}\varinjlim_{V\subset W}F(f^{-1}(j(W))\cong\varinjlim_{x\in V}\varinjlim_{V\subset W}F(j^{\prime}(f^{\prime-1}(W))=(f^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime*}F)_{x}
where the middle isomorphism follows from the fact that
j
and
j^{\prime}
are inclusions on the underlying sets, and commutativity of the diagram, which implies
f^{-1}(W)=f^{\prime-1}(W)
. So we have built a morphism of short exact sequences, and by universal property of kernels, we have
\underline{\Gamma}_{X}f_{*}F\cong f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}F
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
F
and
G
be abelian sheaves on a variety
P^{\prime}
, and suppose we have a map
f:P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
. Then there is a map

Rf_{*}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}G\rightarrow Rf_{*}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G)

in the derived category of abelian sheaves
D(\textnormal{{Ab}}Sh(P))
on
P
.

Proof.

We repeatedly use the derived Hom-tensor adjunction (
[
71
]
Tag 09LP). We have

\begin{split}\textnormal{Hom}(Rf_{*}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}G,Rf_{*}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G))&\cong\textnormal{Hom}(Rf_{*}F,R\textnormal{Hom}(Rf_{*}G,Rf_{*}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G))\\
&\cong\textnormal{Hom}(Rf_{*}F,Rf_{*}R\textnormal{Hom}(f^{*}Rf_{*}G,F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G))\\
&\cong\textnormal{Hom}(f^{*}Rf_{*}F,R\textnormal{Hom}(f^{*}Rf_{*}G,F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G))\\
&\cong\textnormal{Hom}(f^{*}Rf_{*}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G,F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}Rf_{*}G)\end{split}

where the first and last isomorphisms are by derived Hom-tensor adjunction, and the third isomorphism is by the adjunction between
Rf_{*}
and
f^{*}
(recall the latter map is exact). The second isomorphism follows from the formula
Rf_{*}R\textnormal{Hom}(f^{*}F,G)\cong R\textnormal{Hom}(F,Rf_{*}G)
. This latter formula is proven as follows: first, the formula holds for sheaf Hom, since it holds for global Hom with the adjunction between
f_{*},f^{*}
for abelian sheaves. Then apply the composition theorem 10.8.2 in
[
83
]
(see also Theorem 10.7.6 in
[
83
]
for a similar result). There is then a natural map in the last Hom set, from the map
f^{*}Rf_{*}\rightarrow Id
induced by adjunction.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
F
and
G
be abelian sheaves on a variety
P^{\prime}
and suppose we have a map
f:P^{\prime}\rightarrow P
. Suppose further that we have closed immersions
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
and
X^{\prime}=f^{-1}(X)\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
such that we have the following Cartesian diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}-X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P-X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
j^{\prime}
and
j
are the complementary open immersions. Then there is a natural map of functors
i_{*}i^{!}f_{*}\rightarrow f_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}
. Moreover, this map of functors commutes with the map from lemma
A.2
, i.e., the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}Ri^{\prime!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}Ri^{\prime!}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)}

Proof.

For any two abelian sheaves
F
and
G
, we have the following composition of maps:

i_{*}Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G\xrightarrow{(1)}i_{*}Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}i_{*}i^{*}G\xrightarrow{(2)}i_{*}(Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}i^{*}i_{*}i^{*}G)\xrightarrow{(3)}i_{*}(Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}i^{*}G)\xrightarrow{(4)}i_{*}Ri^{!}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)

(10)

where map
(1)
is induced by the map
Id\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}
from adjunction, map
(2)
is a special case of Lemma
A.2
(recall that
i_{*}
is exact since
i
is a closed immersion), map
(3)
is induced by the map
i^{*}i_{*}\rightarrow Id
from adjunction, and map
(4)
is the cup product
Ri^{!}F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}i^{*}G\rightarrow Ri^{!}(F\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}G)
(see the discussion above, or Section 1.2 in (
[
18
]
, “Cycle”)).

The bottom horizontal map in the diagram arises from applying lemma
A.2

Rf_{*}
and the adjunction
f^{*}Rf_{*}\rightarrow Id
. The top horizontal map arises by composing the map from lemma
A.2
with the map of equation
10
.

We now describe the vertical maps: by lemma
A.1
, we have that
\underline{\Gamma}_{X}f_{*}\cong f_{*}\Gamma_{X^{\prime}}
as functors, so that
i^{!}f_{*}:=i^{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X}f_{*}\cong i^{*}f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}
. Then, by ordinary base change using ordinary adjunction, we have a map
i^{*}f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow f^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}
where
f^{\prime}
denotes the restriction of
f
to
X^{\prime}
(this map exists without any assumptions on
f
). Thus we have
i_{*}i^{*}f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow i_{*}f^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}\cong f_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}=:f_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}
since
i\circ f^{\prime}=f\circ i^{\prime}
. Taking the derived version of these maps yields the vertical maps in the diagram above.

To check commutativity of the diagram, it suffices to check commutativity on the level of sheaves. That is, we want the diagram of sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{!}f_{*}F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}f_{*}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{!}f_{*}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}f_{*}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

to commute. The horizontal maps arise from the map
f_{*}F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}f_{*}G\rightarrow f_{*}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}f^{*}f_{*}G)\rightarrow f_{*}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)
which we proved by formal adjunction properties. On the level of sections, this sends a pair
\alpha\otimes\beta
to
\alpha\times\tilde{\beta}
for some
\tilde{\beta}
since the tensor product of sheaves
F
and
G
is the sheafification of
U\mapsto F(U)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G(U)
. That is, the map is the identity on the first component
\alpha
. On the other hand, the vertical maps act by the canonical restriction maps in the following sense: let
U
be an open set in
X
and consider the action of the base change map
i^{!}f_{*}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)(U)\rightarrow f^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)(U)
. We see that
i^{!}f_{*}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)(U)\cong i^{*}f_{*}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)(U):=\varinjlim_{i(U)\subset V}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}(F(f^{-1}(V)\otimes G(f^{-1}(V))
, and that
f^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime!}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G)(U)\cong\varinjlim_{i^{\prime}(f^{\prime-1}(U))\subset V}\underline{\Gamma}_{X^{\prime}}(F(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}G(V))
. The claim is that the base change map as a map of direct limits is induced by inclusions of open sets: for fixed
W\supset i(U)
, there is an open
V\supset i^{\prime}(f^{\prime-1}(U))
such that
V\hookrightarrow f^{-1}(W)
. Simply take
V=f^{-1}(W)
. By commutativity of the diagram, we have
i^{\prime}(f^{\prime-1}(U))\subset f^{-1}(i(U))
, and the latter is a subset of
f^{-1}(W)
by assumption.

Thus the base change maps are induced by inclusions of suitable open sets; the vertical maps are then the canonical restriction maps. The diagram then commutes since restriction commutes with the cross product.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
X\xhookrightarrow{i}P
be a closed immersion of a variety
X
into a smooth variety
P
. Then
H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
is a
H^{*}(P,*)
-module with respect to the cup product described above. Moreover, given a Cartesian diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}-X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P-X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

where
X^{\prime}=f^{-1}(X)\xhookrightarrow{i^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
is a closed immersion, and where
j
and
j^{\prime}
are the complementary open immersions, we have that the induced map
f^{*}:H^{*}_{X}(P,*)\rightarrow H^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},*)
respects the module structures. That is, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},*)\otimes H^{*}(P^{\prime},*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{X^{\prime}}(P^{\prime},*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{X}(P,*)\otimes H^{*}(P,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{X}(P,*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(-)\otimes f^{*}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

or, more succinctly, we have
f^{*}(\alpha\cup\beta)=f^{*}\alpha\cup f^{*}\beta
, where
\alpha\in H^{*}_{X}(P,*)
and
\beta\in H^{*}(P,*)
.

Proof.

Take
F=\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)
and
G=\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m)
in equation (
10
) in the proof of lemma
A.3
to obtain a map; we then postcompose this map with the product map described in Proposition
2.2
to induce the module structure on hypercohomology
i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m)\rightarrow i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n+m)
.

We have a map of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)
for every
n
. We then obtain a natural diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}(\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}(Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(m))}

where the square commutes since the vertical arrows are induced from the map of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)
and we have a natural transformation of bifunctors
i_{*}Ri^{!}(-)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\bullet)\rightarrow i_{*}Ri^{!}(-\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bullet)
given by the cup product construction above.

Similarly, we have a commutative square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}(\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n+m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}(Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(m))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n+m)}

since the map
\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)
induces the pullback
f^{*}
on cohomology, which is a ring homomorphism. The bottom map of the square is the map from lemma
A.2
. Adjoining the bottom square to the right of the top square yields the top half of the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{P}(n+m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}Ri^{!}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n+m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}Ri^{\prime!}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n)\otimes^{L}_{\mathbb{Z}}Rf_{*}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(m)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}i^{\prime}_{*}Ri^{\prime!}\mathbb{Z}_{P^{\prime}}(n+m)}

The bottom half of the commutative diagram commutes by lemma
A.3
and again since
f^{*}
is a ring homomorphism.
∎

A.3

Relative cycles

Using the cup product in hypercohomology and the external product on higher Chow groups, we can immediately extend the cup product to cohomology with support. That is, for a smooth variety
X
with closed supports
S
and
T
, the cup product extends to a map

H^{*}_{S}(X,*)\otimes H^{*}_{T}(X,*)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{*}_{S\cap T}(X,*)

which satisfies the usual properties the ordinary cup product satisfies (e.g., associativity, graded commutativity, appropriate functoriality, etc.). However, there is no unit element (unless the support is the ambient space
X
itself).

In fact, we even have the external product for the case with supports, where

H^{*}_{S}(X,*)\otimes H^{*}_{T}(Y,*)\xrightarrow{\times}H^{*}_{S\times T}(X\times Y,*)

is exactly the external product defined in Proposition
2.2
but using relative cycles, as in the beginning of section
2.2
. Keeping track of the supports shows the product cycle
V\times W
of a cycle
V
with support on
S
and a cycle
W
with support on
T
will have support on
S\times T
in
X\times Y
. Moreover, the element
1\in H^{0}(\textnormal{Spec }k,0)
for the ground field
k
serves as the unit for the external product.

Lemma A.5
.

Suppose we have a fiber product diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where all varieties are smooth, and
f
is proper. Suppose further that
S\subset X
and
T\subset Y
are closed subsets such that
f(S)\subset T
. Then the following diagram is commutative:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{g^{\prime-1}(S)}(X^{\prime},*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{S}(X,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{g^{-1}(T)}(Y^{\prime},*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{T}(Y,*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}

or, more formulaically, we have
f^{\prime}_{!}g^{\prime*}=g^{*}f_{!}
.

Proof.

We have the statement for the case without supports, which is Proposition
2.2
. Moreover, we have that cohomology with support is given by cohomology of relative cycles. The lemma then follows from keeping track of the supports for the relative cycles, naturality of the localization sequence with respect to pull-back, and the assumption that
f(S)\subset T
with commutativity of the fiber square.
∎

Here we briefly outline the projection formula with supports.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper map between smooth varieties
X
and
Y
. Let
S\subset X
be a closed subset, and
T\subset Y
be a closed subset such that
f(S)\subset T
. Then we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{S}(X,*)\otimes H_{T^{\prime}}^{*}(Y,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{S}(X,*)\otimes H^{*}_{f^{-1}(T^{\prime})}(X,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{S\cap f^{-1}(T^{\prime})}(X,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{T}(Y,*)\otimes H^{*}_{T^{\prime}}(Y,*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{T\cap T^{\prime}}(Y,*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{S,T}_{!}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{S\cap f^{-1}(T^{\prime}),T\cap T^{\prime}}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cup}

where
T^{\prime}
is an arbitrary closed support in
Y
, and where the notation
f^{A,B}_{!}
denotes the proper pushforward defined by twisted duality (Definition
2.9
) with respect to the support
A
in the domain, and the support
B
in the target. Formulaically, we have

f^{S\cap f^{-1}(T^{\prime}),T\cap T^{\prime}}_{!}(\alpha\cup f^{*}\beta)=f^{S,T}_{!}\alpha\cup\beta

for
\alpha\in H^{*}_{S}(X,*)
and
\beta\in H^{*}_{T^{\prime}}(Y,*)
.

Proof.

Recall we have the projection formula for cohomology without support (Proposition
2.4
). To extend to the case with supports, we can interpret cohomology with support as the cohomology of relative cycles. This, combined with the ordinary projection formula and the
H^{*}(-,*)
-module structure of
H^{*}_{\bullet}(-,*)
discussed in the Appendix chapter prior, yields the result.
∎
